## 2.7 CS — Computer science

Standard v2 cited, not copied. Diagnosis: audit_engineering_cs.md. v1 source: twelve.txt 2608–3008. Theorems are owned by MA; complexity theory is MA bridge C3 (MSC 68Q), and CS cites it.

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** Computer science makes and studies computations: procedures, programs, machines and the systems built from them.
**Complication.** v1 divided by layer of the stack and by community. That gave complexity theorems two homes (MA and CS), wrongly called FLP open, and counted heuristics as a sixth method.
**Question.** By what single dimension can every claim about a computation be placed once, with the theorems left in MA?
**Answer.** By the property the claim asserts of a computation: that it is correct, what it costs, how it coordinates, what it learns, or what it protects.

### 2. Governing thought
Computer science is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five properties of a computation: correctness, cost, coordination, learning, and protection.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **the property asserted of a computation.** Order: structural, from the single program outward to many agents and adversaries.
1. Correctness
2. Cost
3. Coordination
4. Learning
5. Protection

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### CS.O.A1 Correctness
**SCQA.** A computation is first judged by whether it does what it is specified to do. Specifications, semantics and proof tools exist, but most software is never proved. What can be claimed about correctness, and how? Key line: semantics, specification and verification, types, testing.
- CS.O.A1.1 Semantics of languages — operational, denotational and axiomatic semantics. Lambda calculus and Turing machines are equivalent in power (the theorem is MA.O.A1).
- CS.O.A1.2 Specification and verification — Hoare logic proves partial correctness; total correctness also needs termination. Model checking decides temporal properties of finite-state models.
- CS.O.A1.3 Types — well-typed programs do not go wrong (progress and preservation), for languages whose type system is proved sound.
- CS.O.A1.4 Testing — testing can show the presence of bugs, never their absence (Dijkstra), for any input space too large to exhaust.
- CS.O.A1.5 Limits of correctness — Rice's theorem: every non-trivial semantic property of programs is undecidable, for Turing-complete languages. Uses MA.O.A1.

#### CS.O.A2 Cost
**SCQA.** A correct computation may still be too slow, too large or too costly in energy. Which costs can be bounded, and by what? Key line: algorithms, data structures, complexity, machines.
- CS.O.A2.1 Algorithms — each algorithm has a stated cost on a stated model. Comparison sorting needs Ω(n log n) comparisons in the worst case (a decision-tree lower bound, uses MA.B.C3).
- CS.O.A2.2 Data structures — amortised costs, e.g. union–find with path compression and union by rank runs in O(α(n)) per operation.
- CS.O.A2.3 Complexity classes — P, NP, PSPACE. Whether P = NP is OPEN and owned by MA.B.C3; CS uses it.
- CS.O.A2.4 Machines and architecture — memory hierarchy, pipelines, parallel speed-up. Amdahl's law bounds speed-up by 1/((1−p) + p/s) for a fixed workload whose fraction p is parallelisable by a factor s.
- CS.O.A2.5 Physical limits of hardware — Moore's law is an empirical trend, now slowing; it is not a law. Transistor physics is PH.

#### CS.O.A3 Coordination
**SCQA.** Many computations run at once, share state and communicate over unreliable links. What can and cannot be coordinated? Key line: concurrency, distributed agreement, networks, data systems.
- CS.O.A3.1 Concurrency — races, deadlock, linearisability.
- CS.O.A3.2 Distributed agreement — FLP (Fischer, Lynch and Paterson, 1985): no deterministic protocol guarantees consensus in an asynchronous system if even one process may crash. This is a proved theorem, not an open problem. Byzantine agreement needs n ≥ 3f + 1 processes to tolerate f faulty ones, with synchronous oral messages and no signatures.
- CS.O.A3.3 Consistency under partition — CAP (Gilbert and Lynch, 2002 proof): during a network partition, a system cannot provide both linearisable consistency and availability.
- CS.O.A3.4 Networks — protocols, routing, congestion control.
- CS.O.A3.5 Data systems — databases, transactions (ACID), query optimisation. Information retrieval as a claim about records is IS.

#### CS.O.A4 Learning
**SCQA.** Some computations improve from data instead of from a programmer's rules. What can such systems learn, and how well? Key line: learning theory, models and training, reasoning and planning, perception and language.
- CS.O.A4.1 Learning theory — a binary concept class is PAC-learnable if and only if its VC dimension is finite (uses MA.O.A5). No-free-lunch: averaged uniformly over all objective functions, every optimiser performs equally.
- CS.O.A4.2 Models and training — neural networks; universal approximation holds for one-hidden-layer networks with a non-polynomial activation, approximating continuous functions on compact sets to any accuracy. It says nothing about learnability. Empirical scaling laws are W4 claims within stated ranges.
- CS.O.A4.3 Reasoning and planning — search, logic programming, planning. Many planning problems are PSPACE-complete (uses MA.B.C3).
- CS.O.A4.4 Perception and language processing — vision, speech, NLP. Claims about language itself are LN.
- CS.O.A4.5 Alignment and evaluation of AI systems — whether a trained system pursues its intended objective. Methods are immature; much is OPEN.

#### CS.O.A5 Protection
**SCQA.** Computations run among adversaries. What can be protected, from whom, and on what assumptions? Key line: cryptography in use, security of systems, privacy.
- CS.O.A5.1 Cryptography in use — protocols are secure relative to stated hardness assumptions (e.g. factoring). Security proofs are MA; the deployed protocol is CS.
- CS.O.A5.2 Systems security — access control, memory safety, attack surfaces.
- CS.O.A5.3 Privacy — differential privacy bounds how much one record changes an output distribution, by a stated ε.
- CS.O.A5.4 Quantum threats — Shor's algorithm factors in polynomial time on a fault-tolerant quantum computer. Uses MA.B.C3 and PH.O.A2.

### 5. Face M — Methods

| ID | Role | Shared role | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| CS.M.1 | Formalise | M1 Represent | specify, define a model of computation |
| CS.M.2 | Prove | M2 Derive | correctness or bound proof |
| CS.M.3 | Build | M4 Intervene | implement a system |
| CS.M.4 | Run and measure | M5 Compute | benchmark, simulation, training run |
| CS.M.5 | Verify | M7 Verify | model checking, test suite, audit |

Five roles. M3 Observe and M6 Compare appear only inside CS.M.4 (empirical evaluation) and are not separate roles.

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
Premature optimisation is the root of all evil; worse is better; end-to-end argument; design patterns; "make it work, make it right, make it fast"; ablation as a habit.

### 6. Face W — Warrants

| Cell | Warrant | Standard of acceptance | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|
| CS.O.A1 | W1 + W11 | machine-checked proof, or verification against a formal specification | specification wrong, unsound tool, unmodelled environment |
| CS.O.A2 | W1 + W2 | proof of bound on a stated model; benchmark with stated variance | model mismatch with hardware, unrepresentative input |
| CS.O.A3 | W1 + W4 | impossibility or protocol proof; measured behaviour in deployment | failure model differs from assumed one |
| CS.O.A4 | W4 + W2 | held-out evaluation, stated metric and variance, replication | data leakage, benchmark overfitting, distribution shift |
| CS.O.A5 | W1 + W11 | reduction to a stated assumption; penetration test | broken assumption, side channel, implementation flaw |

### 7. Face B — Bridges

| ID | Field | Cells | Methods | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CS.B.C1 | Theory of computation | A1.5, A2.3 | M.1, M.2 | models (cites MA.B.C3) |
| CS.B.C2 | Programming languages | A1 | M.1, M.2, M.5 | languages |
| CS.B.C3 | Computer architecture | A2.4, A2.5 | M.3, M.4 | hardware (uses PH) |
| CS.B.C4 | Operating and distributed systems | A3 | M.3, M.4 | machines |
| CS.B.C5 | Databases | A3.5 | M.3, M.4 | data |
| CS.B.C6 | Artificial intelligence and machine learning | A4 | M.3, M.4 | data and models |
| CS.B.C7 | Security and privacy | A5 | all | systems |
| CS.B.C8 | Human–computer interaction | A1, A4 | M.3, M.4 | interfaces (user claims MS) |
| CS.B.C9 | Computational science | A2 | M.4 | other pyramids' models |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. Place by the property claimed: correctness, cost, coordination, learning or protection.
2. A theorem is MA, even when computer scientists prove it. Complexity theorems sit in MA.B.C3; CS cites "uses MA.B.C3".
3. A program delivered to a customer's specification is EN (EN.B.C5); whether the program is correct is CS.
4. A claim about users' minds is MS; the interface's measured effect is CS.B.C8.
5. A claim about the record, the catalogue or retrieval as such is IS; the algorithm's cost is CS.
6. A claim about language is LN; a system that processes it is CS.O.A4.4.
7. The legal duty on data is LA; the technical privacy guarantee is CS.O.A5.3.
8. A claim mixing two properties goes to the one its warrant establishes.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused | Owner |
|---|---|
| computability and complexity theorems | MA.O.A1, MA.B.C3 |
| device physics | PH.O.A4 |
| software delivery to contract | EN |
| users' cognition | MS |
| the record and its retrieval as information | IS |
| data protection law | LA |
| ethics of AI as argument | PL |

### 10. External crosswalk
**ACM CCS 2012** has 13 top-level classes. They land as follows:
- General and reference → refused, not a claim class
- Hardware → A2.4, A2.5, B.C3
- Computer systems organisation → A2.4, A3
- Networks → A3.4
- Software and its engineering → A1, EN.B.C5
- Theory of computation → A1.5, A2.3 (cites MA.B.C3)
- Mathematics of computing → refused to MA
- Information systems → A3.5, IS
- Security and privacy → A5
- Human-centred computing → B.C8
- Computing methodologies → A4
- Applied computing → refused, a catalogue
- Social and professional topics → refused to PL, LA, BU

Coverage: 9 of 13 land.

**CS2023** has 17 knowledge areas (checked: csed.acm.org/knowledge-areas):
- AL → A2.1–A2.2
- AR → A2.4
- AI → A4
- DM → A3.5
- FPL → A1.1–A1.3
- GIT → A4.4 (graphics as CS.B.C9)
- HCI → B.C8
- MSF → refused to MA
- NC → A3.4
- OS → A3
- PDC → A3.1–A3.3
- SEC → A5
- SEP → refused to PL and LA
- SDF → A1
- SE → A1.4 and EN.B.C5
- SPD → refused, a catalogue of platforms
- SF → A2.4

Coverage: 14 of 17 land; 3 are refused.

**ANZSRC 46** (checked 9 Oct 2026 against the ABS file anzsrc2020_for.xlsx) has 14 groups. 4601 Applied computing → catalogue. 4602 AI, 4603 Computer vision, 4611 Machine learning → A4. 4604 Cybersecurity → A5. 4605 Data management → A3.5 (records side is IS). 4606 Distributed computing → A3. 4607 Graphics → B.C9. 4608 Human-centred → B.C8. 4609 Information systems → A3.5 and IS. 4610 Library and information studies → IS. 4612 Software engineering → A1 and EN. 4613 Theory of computation → A1.5, A2.3. 4699 Other → not a cell. Coverage: 11 of 13 substantive groups land in CS; 4610 goes to IS; 4601 is refused.

### 11. Validation
Sizes: key line 5. Face O 5 cells + 24 sub-cells = 29. M roles 5. Heuristics 6, outside the count. W rows 5. B bridges 9.
Key line, word for word: Correctness. Cost. Coordination. Learning. Protection.
OPEN: P vs NP (owned by MA.B.C3, cited); AI alignment methods (A4.5).
CONTESTED: software engineering in EN vs CS (alternative: CS.O.A1); HCI as a CS bridge vs an MS cell.
Fixed from the audit: FLP is now stated as proved (1985), with its hypotheses. Heuristics are moved out of the role count.

## 2.8 MS — Mind, brain and society

Rebuilt at Standard v2 from twelve.txt 3236–3463. Diagnosis applied: audit_mind_econ_business.md Part I, §4, §5a–c, §7, §8, §9. The audit's errors 1–15 and fact-check rows 1–11 are fixed in place; this file does not re-audit.

ID note. v1 had seven items under A1 and A5. Standard v2 allows five. Items are regrouped, so some audit IDs move. The map is in §8, rule 14.

### 1. SCQA

Situation. Psychology studies mental process and the person. Neuroscience studies the nervous system. Sociology studies patterns that outlast the persons in them. APA lists 54 divisions; ASA lists 55 sections; SfN lists 11 themes. Complication. One fact is filed three ways: a memory is a process, a hippocampal trace and a shared story. v1 also filed methods inside Side A, counted heuristics as a role and left anthropology, criminology, communication and education without a home. Question. Which single partition places every claim about mind, brain and society exactly once, with methods, warrants and bridges kept apart? Answer. Sort every claim by the unit it is about; sort practices by what they do; read each named field as a bridge.

### 2. Governing thought

Mind, brain and society is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges. Its objects are mental processes, nervous systems, persons, interactions and social structures.

### 3. Key line

Logic: inductive. Plural noun: units. Dimension: the unit the claim is about. Order: structural, from the smallest unit that bears a mental claim to the largest pattern.

1. Mental processes
2. Nervous systems
3. Persons
4. Interactions
5. Social structures

Boundary sentences kept from v1. The ion channel is neuroscience. The report of a memory is psychology. The distribution of that report across a class is sociology. Social psychology is the interaction unit asked with psychological methods. Cognitive neuroscience is the process unit asked with neural measures.

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### MS.O.A1 Mental processes

Situation. Psychology's core is the process that turns input into report and act. Complication. Its laws hold over stated ranges, and its biases are relative to a stated norm. Question. Which processes does a claim about the working mind concern? Answer. Five, ordered from input to evaluation.

Key line (inductive; plural noun processes; dimension stage of the processing; order input to evaluation): sensation and perception · attention and consciousness · learning and memory · emotion and motivation · thought, language and judgement.

- **MS.O.A1.1 Sensation and perception.** A stimulus is transduced; a percept is constructed. Named objects:
  - MS.O.A1.1.1 Weber's law: over a mid-range of intensities, the just-noticeable difference ΔI is a roughly constant fraction k of the base intensity I (Gescheider 1997).
  - MS.O.A1.1.2 Fechner's law: if Weber's law holds and each JND is a unit step, sensation grows with the logarithm of intensity.
  - MS.O.A1.1.3 Stevens's power law: for direct magnitude estimation over a stated range, perceived magnitude is a power function of intensity.
  - MS.O.A1.1.4 Signal detection theory: under equal-variance Gaussian noise and signal distributions, d′ measures sensitivity and c (or β) measures criterion. A classical threshold confounds the two (Green and Swets 1966).
- **MS.O.A1.2 Attention and consciousness.** Selection has a cost; reportable experience has measurable conditions.
  - MS.O.A1.2.1 Selective attention and capacity limits; inattentional blindness.
  - MS.O.A1.2.2 Consciousness as reportable experience, studied with no-report controls. The hard problem is PL.O.A2; MS uses it and does not claim to solve it.
  - MS.O.A1.2.3 Hypnosis and altered states, as changes in attention and report (APA Div. 30 home).
- **MS.O.A1.3 Learning and memory.**
  - MS.O.A1.3.1 Classical conditioning: a cue comes to predict an outcome. Rescorla–Wagner model: on each trial, ΔV = αβ(λ − ΣV), where α and β are cue and outcome salience rates in (0,1], λ is the outcome's maximum, and ΣV sums all cues present.
  - MS.O.A1.3.2 Operant conditioning and schedules of reinforcement.
  - MS.O.A1.3.3 Memory systems: declarative (episodic, semantic) and nondeclarative (procedural, priming, classical conditioning, nonassociative) (Squire 2004).
  - MS.O.A1.3.4 Working-memory capacity: about 3–5 chunks when rehearsal and chunking are blocked (Cowan 2001). The 7±2 figure is a heuristic (register).
  - MS.O.A1.3.5 Forgetting. Cue-dependent retrieval failure (Tulving 1974) is one answer. Interference, decay and failed consolidation are rivals (CONTESTED; Wixted 2004).
- **MS.O.A1.4 Emotion and motivation.** A state with a trigger, a bodily change and an action tendency.
  - MS.O.A1.4.1 Appraisal accounts and basic-emotion accounts, as rival answer-families.
  - MS.O.A1.4.2 Motivation and reward as process claims. The tissue (orbitofrontal cortex, vmPFC, ventral striatum) is MS.O.A2.3.
- **MS.O.A1.5 Thought, language and judgement.**
  - MS.O.A1.5.1 Concepts and categories.
  - MS.O.A1.5.2 Judgement heuristics (availability, representativeness, anchoring; Tversky and Kahneman 1974). They are biases relative to a stated normative model. The rule of thumb used by an analyst is a different sentence, in the register.
  - MS.O.A1.5.3 Psycholinguistic processing (comprehension, production). The grammar itself is LN.
  - MS.O.A1.5.4 Creativity and problem solving (APA Div. 10 home). The aesthetic verdict is AR.
  - MS.O.A1.5.5 RETIRED in Phase 2.1 (ID not reused). Learning claims under an educational arrangement, including physics and mathematics education (MSC 97), are ED.O.A2–A4. MS keeps the general mechanism of learning and memory in MS.O.A1.5.

#### MS.O.A2 Nervous systems

Situation. Neuroscience's core runs from molecule to system. Complication. v1 filed lesion, imaging and computational models here, which are methods. Question. Which tissue claims does neuroscience own? Answer. Four, ordered by scale and then by time.

Key line (inductive; plural noun levels; dimension scale of the tissue, then time; order cell to system, then development): neurons, glia and synapses · plasticity · circuits and systems · development of the nervous system.

- **MS.O.A2.1 Neurons, glia and synapses.**
  - MS.O.A2.1.1 Action potential: an all-or-none spike, given a threshold depolarisation of an excitable membrane.
  - MS.O.A2.1.2 Chemical and electrical synapses; excitation and inhibition.
  - MS.O.A2.1.3 Glia: astrocytes, oligodendrocytes, microglia and their signalling roles (SfN Theme B).
  - The equilibrium potential (Nernst, GHK) uses CH.O.A3.3. The cell as a cell is BI; electrical signalling is native here.
- **MS.O.A2.2 Plasticity.**
  - MS.O.A2.2.1 Long-term potentiation and depression, as candidate mechanisms of learning. A changed synapse is not yet a memory; the memory is MS.O.A1.3.
- **MS.O.A2.3 Circuits and systems.**
  - MS.O.A2.3.1 Sensory and motor pathways.
  - MS.O.A2.3.2 Hypothalamus and homeostasis.
  - MS.O.A2.3.3 Valuation circuits: orbitofrontal cortex, vmPFC, ventral striatum. "Limbic system" is a contested grouping and is not used as a cell.
  - MS.O.A2.3.4 Localisation as a starting map; a function is usually a circuit.
- **MS.O.A2.4 Development of the nervous system.**
  - MS.O.A2.4.1 Overproduction and pruning.
  - MS.O.A2.4.2 Critical and sensitive periods, with their measured windows.

#### MS.O.A3 Persons

Situation. Persons differ stably and change across the life course. Complication. Differences invite reification, and clinical categories are conventions. Question. Which claims concern the person as the unit? Answer. Four, ordered from stable difference to disorder.

Key line (inductive; plural noun aspects; dimension what persists or changes in the person; order from stable trait to clinical pattern): personality · intelligence and abilities · life-course development · psychopathology.

- **MS.O.A3.1 Personality.**
  - MS.O.A3.1.1 Five-factor model as the common answer. HEXACO (six factors; Ashton and Lee 2007) is the rival (CONTESTED).
  - MS.O.A3.1.2 Person–situation debate: both variances are measured.
- **MS.O.A3.2 Intelligence and abilities.**
  - MS.O.A3.2.1 g as a statistical factor (Spearman 1904), not a thing seen.
  - MS.O.A3.2.2 Heritability of intelligence: a variance fraction in a stated population, environment and time, broad (H²) or narrow (h²). The quantitative-genetics result uses BI.O.A4.
- **MS.O.A3.3 Life-course development.**
  - MS.O.A3.3.1 Stage theories as ideal types.
  - MS.O.A3.3.2 Attachment classification (Ainsworth; Main and Solomon).
  - MS.O.A3.3.3 Ageing and adult development.
- **MS.O.A3.4 Psychopathology.** The psychological process account of distress and disability.
  - MS.O.A3.4.1 Diagnostic categories as conventions: DSM-5-TR (APA 2022) and ICD-11 (WHO, in force 2022). The category is W12.
  - MS.O.A3.4.2 RDoC as the process-first alternative (CONTESTED as a basis for classification).
  - Disorder as function failure, its treatment and its trials are MD.O.A1. MS.O.A3.4 holds the process account only (CONTESTED; alternative: MD owns both).

#### MS.O.A4 Interactions

Situation. Something passes between persons. Complication. Classic effects have mixed replication records. Question. Which claims concern what passes between persons? Answer. Five, ordered from the situation to the self it builds.

Key line (inductive; plural noun interactions; dimension what passes between persons; order from situation to self): social influence · social cognition · attitudes · groups and relationships · self and identity.

- **MS.O.A4.1 Social influence.** Conformity, obedience and bystander effects, each with its replication record (Burger 2009; Fischer et al. 2011 meta-analysis on bystanders). Environment–behaviour claims (APA Div. 34) sit here.
- **MS.O.A4.2 Social cognition.** Attribution; the fundamental attribution error (Ross 1977), flagged as weaker in non-WEIRD samples (Henrich et al. 2010).
- **MS.O.A4.3 Attitudes.** Cognitive dissonance (Festinger 1957), with self-perception as the rival account. Consumer attitudes (APA Div. 23) sit here; BU.O.A4.1 uses them.
- **MS.O.A4.4 Groups and relationships.** Small groups, roles, norms, status; couples and families; conversation as turn-taking; human–animal interaction (CONTESTED; alternative BI.O.A3).
- **MS.O.A4.5 Self and identity.** The self as a role taken toward others; identity as a claimed category; body and embodiment. The category's distribution is MS.O.A5.1.

#### MS.O.A5 Social structures

Situation. Sociology's core is the pattern that outlasts its members. Complication. v1 mixed movement into population and sector into institution. Question. Which claims concern a pattern no single person carries? Answer. Five, ordered from position to change.

Key line (inductive; plural noun structures; dimension the kind of pattern; order from position to change): positions and inequality · organisations · institutions and culture · populations · collective action and change.

- **MS.O.A5.1 Positions and inequality.** Status, role, role conflict. Inequality as a residual in life chances after stated controls. Bases include class, race, gender, sexuality and disability.
- **MS.O.A5.2 Organisations.** Weberian bureaucracy (rule, file, career); informal structure. PO.O.A2 uses it for state bureaucracy.
- **MS.O.A5.3 Institutions and culture.** An institution is a repeated, taken-for-granted pattern: family, market, state, religion, school, media, science. Culture is shared meaning in use. Consumption as practice sits here. A paper merely set in a church or school is a sector instance.
- **MS.O.A5.4 Populations.** Births, deaths, migration and their rates; life table; cohort. Human population is owned here; non-human population dynamics are BI.O.A5.
- **MS.O.A5.5 Collective action and change.** Social movements that target a norm or an organisation. A movement contesting authority is PO.O.A3.3 (CONTESTED target rule). The free-rider result is EC.O.A5.3. Differentiation, diffusion, globalisation. Named theories of history (Marxian, functionalist) are answer-families, reconstructed under MS.M.3.

### 5. Face M — Methods

Key line (inductive; plural noun roles; dimension what the practice does to a claim; order from recording to checking): measure · perturb · compare and interpret · model · replicate and review.

| Role ID | Role | Maps to M1–M7 | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| MS.M.1 | Measure | M3 Observe (construct specification M1 inside it) | Psychometric reliability and validity (Messick); measurement invariance; census and survey; EEG; fMRI as a haemodynamic (BOLD) correlate lagging about 4–6 s (Logothetis 2008) |
| MS.M.2 | Perturb | M4 Intervene | Randomised experiment; lesion; stimulation; optogenetics; field experiment on a norm |
| MS.M.3 | Compare and interpret | M6 Compare and reconstruct | Developmental and cross-cultural designs (HRAF); comparative-historical sociology; ethnography; interview; history of the field |
| MS.M.4 | Model | M5 Compute (analytic models M2) | Drift-diffusion; Rescorla–Wagner fits; network and agent-based models; computational neuroscience |
| MS.M.5 | Replicate and review | M7 Verify | Preregistration; registered reports; many-labs replication; peer review |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)

MS.M.H. The 7±2 rule for working memory. Stages of grief as a fixed sequence (Kübler-Ross 1969 proposed stages for dying patients; evidence for a fixed order is weak, Maciejewski et al. 2007). "Society made them do it" without a mechanism. Folk psychology. Analysts' "people judge by ease of recall" when used as a rule of thumb. Useful; never evidence.

### 6. Face W — Warrants

Every A-level and named sub-cell has a row. Named objects inside a sub-cell share that sub-cell's row; this is stated explicitly.

| Cell ID | Warrant W# | Standard of acceptance | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|
| MS.O.A1 | W3, W4 | Effect replicates across paradigms with stated range | Task artefact; range extrapolation |
| MS.O.A1.1 (incl. .1–.4) | W3, W4 | Psychophysical function with d′ and c, over a stated range | Criterion shift read as sensitivity; out-of-range use |
| MS.O.A1.2 (incl. .1–.3) | W3, W4 | Effect survives no-report and demand controls | Report confound; demand characteristics |
| MS.O.A1.3 (incl. .1–.5) | W3 | Double dissociation; acquisition and extinction with controls | Single dissociation; ceiling or floor; pseudo-conditioning |
| MS.O.A1.4 (incl. .1–.2) | W3, W4 | Convergent physiological and report measures | Self-report bias; construct ambiguity |
| MS.O.A1.5 (incl. .1–.5) | W3; W12 for the normative model | Bias shown against an explicit norm; learning effect in a controlled trial | Wrong normative model; ecological rationality; teaching-to-the-test |
| MS.O.A2 | W3, W4 | Necessity and sufficiency tests converge with recording | Correlate taken for mechanism |
| MS.O.A2.1 (incl. .1–.3) | W3, W4 | Recording with stated noise | Artefact; slice versus in-vivo gap |
| MS.O.A2.2 (incl. .1) | W3 | Block and induce tests | Correlate taken for mechanism |
| MS.O.A2.3 (incl. .1–.4) | W3, W4 | Lesion or stimulation convergent with recording | Diaschisis; reverse inference |
| MS.O.A2.4 (incl. .1–.2) | W3, W4, W6 | Timed manipulation; cross-species comparison | Species transfer |
| MS.O.A3 | W4 | Reliable measurement with invariance across groups | Method variance |
| MS.O.A3.1 (incl. .1–.2) | W4 | Reliability; factor invariance | Culture non-invariance |
| MS.O.A3.2 (incl. .1–.2) | W4, W5 | g loadings; heritability with stated population | Flynn effect; range restriction; G×E |
| MS.O.A3.3 (incl. .1–.3) | W4, W6 | Longitudinal design with attrition stated | Cohort confound; attrition |
| MS.O.A3.4 (incl. .1–.2) | W12 for the category; W4 for the process | Inter-rater reliability; process measure predicts course | Low validity; comorbidity |
| MS.O.A4 | W3 | Preregistered replication | Replication failure |
| MS.O.A4.1 | W3 | Preregistered replication; meta-analysis | Demand characteristics; small samples |
| MS.O.A4.2 | W3, W6 | Cross-cultural replication | WEIRD sample |
| MS.O.A4.3 | W3 | Behavioural, not only self-report, outcome | Self-perception alternative |
| MS.O.A4.4 | W4, W8 | Coded interaction; inter-coder reliability | Observer effect |
| MS.O.A4.5 | W8, W4 | Trace to record; scale validity | Reification |
| MS.O.A5 | W4, W6, W8 | Pattern shown beyond any one member | Ecological fallacy |
| MS.O.A5.1 | W5, W4 | Residual after stated controls | Omitted variable; bad controls |
| MS.O.A5.2 | W4, W6, W8 | Comparative cases plus records | Selection on outcome |
| MS.O.A5.3 | W6, W8 | Comparative-historical trace; practice traced to record | Functionalist circularity; culture as residual |
| MS.O.A5.4 | W4; W12 for definitions | Registers or census with coverage stated | Undercount; definition change |
| MS.O.A5.5 | W6, W7, W9 | Dated record; rival answer-families tested | Teleology; unfalsifiable theory |

W13 is never a warrant in MS. A religious claim studied here is a claim about practice (MS.O.A5.3), warranted W6/W8.

### 7. Face B — Bridges

Key line (inductive; plural noun bridges; dimension the unit the community takes as home; order matching Face O): process sciences · neural sciences · person sciences · interaction sciences · structure sciences.

| Bridge ID | Named field(s) | Cell(s) | Method subset | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| MS.B.C1 | Cognitive and experimental psychology; psychophysics; cognitive science (with CS and PL); psycholinguistics; learning sciences (mechanism only; education research is ED) | MS.O.A1 | MS.M.1, .2, .4, .5 | Human performers |
| MS.B.C2 | Neuroscience; cognitive neuroscience; neuropsychology; behavioural neuroscience | MS.O.A2 (with A1 for cognitive neuroscience) | MS.M.1, .2, .4 | Nervous tissue, human and animal |
| MS.B.C3 | Personality, developmental, clinical, counselling, health and forensic psychology | MS.O.A3 | MS.M.1, .3, .5 | Persons across age; clinics; courts (forensic, with LA) |
| MS.B.C4 | Social psychology; industrial-organisational psychology; interpersonal communication | MS.O.A4 | MS.M.1, .2, .5 | Dyads, groups, workplaces |
| MS.B.C5 | Sociology; social and cultural anthropology; demography; criminology; media and communication studies | MS.O.A5 (with A4) | MS.M.1, .3, .4 | Organisations, communities, populations, media systems |

Sectors (education, medicine, religion, crime, sport, military) are a register, not bridges. Each paper set in a sector still owes a cell.

### 8. Assignment rules

1. A neural correlate is evidence for a process claim, not the process. It is labelled a correlate until a mechanism is shown.
2. The ion channel and the spike are MS.O.A2.1. The cell as a living unit is BI. The equilibrium potential uses CH.O.A3.3.
3. A diagnostic category is a convention (W12) at MS.O.A3.4.1. Disorder as function failure, treatment and trials are MD.O.A1. Treatment outcome is MD; MS.O.A3.4 cites it.
4. Psychoanalytic theory is an answer-family at MS.O.A1 or MS.O.A3, reconstructed under MS.M.3. Folk psychology is in the register.
5. The availability heuristic as a claim about people is MS.O.A1.5.2. The analyst's rule of thumb is MS.M.H. They are two sentences.
6. Heritability as a quantitative-genetic quantity is owned by BI.O.A4. MS.O.A3.2.2 uses BI.O.A4.
7. An institution as a pattern is MS.O.A5.3. A paper merely set in a church or school is a sector instance.
8. A movement targeting a norm or organisation is MS.O.A5.5. A movement contesting authority uses PO.O.A3.3. The free-rider result uses EC.O.A5.3.
9. Market as allocation is EC.O.A2; market as institution is MS.O.A5.3. Authority is PO.O.A2.
10. Human population is MS.O.A5.4. EC.O.A2.5 (labour supply) uses it. Non-human population is BI.O.A5.
11. Reference-dependent choice as a model of the economic agent is EC.O.A1.3. The bias as a mental process is MS.O.A1.5.2. Strategic exploitation of a bias against a reacting party is ST.O.A5.2.
12. A learning claim under an educational arrangement, including physics and mathematics education research (MSC 97), is ED. A claim about the mechanism of learning, independent of any arrangement, is MS.O.A1.5. The theorem or law taught is MA or PH.
13. Dated claims about past psychologists are HI. The reconstruction of a past theory as an answer is MS.M.3.
14. Audit-to-v2 ID map: A1.5 judgement → A1.5.2; A1.4 memory → A1.3; A1.6 emotion → A1.4; A1.7 consciousness → A1.2.2; A2.5 computational → MS.M.4; A5.3 organisation → A5.2; A5.4 institution and A5.5 culture → A5.3; A5.6 population → A5.4; A5.7 change → A5.5.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused claim type | Owner pyramid id |
|---|---|
| The hard problem; concept of mind; ethics of research | PL.O.A2 |
| Cell biology of neurons as cells; heritability as quantitative genetics; human evolution; non-human behaviour | BI.O.A2, BI.O.A4, BI.O.A3 |
| Disorder as function failure, treatment, drug and trial | MD.O.A1 |
| Grammar: phonology, syntax, semantics | LN |
| Authority, the state, movements contesting authority | PO.O.A2, PO.O.A3.3 |
| Allocation, prices, the free-rider theorem | EC.O.A2, EC.O.A5.3 |
| Criminal offence as legal definition | LA.O.A3.2 |
| Dated past events and persons | HI |
| Aesthetic verdict on a creative work | AR |
| Theorems used in models (statistics, stochastic processes) | MA.O.A5 |
| Learning analytics software; information retrieval | CS (bridge "information and the record") |
| Environmental state and coupled human–natural systems | EA |
| Religious truth claims (theology) | RE·T |
| Mātauranga Māori and other Indigenous knowledge claims | ANZSRC 45 scope note only; MS studies practice (W6/W8) and never applies W13 |

### 10. External crosswalk

Authorities: APA divisions; ASA sections; SfN themes. Lists and statuses are taken from the audit, which checked apa.org, asanet.org and sfn.org in October 2026. I did not re-check them.

**APA divisions (54; numbered 1–56, 4 and 11 vacant).** Refused (5): Div. 2 Teaching of Psychology (pedagogy), 18 Public Service (guild), 31 State/Provincial Association Affairs (guild), 42 Independent Practice (guild), 52 International (area). The audit's ten "missing" divisions now land:

| Div | Name (short) | v2 home |
|---|---|---|
| 10 | Aesthetics, Creativity and the Arts | MS.O.A1.5.4 |
| 14 | Industrial and Organizational | MS.B.C4 |
| 15 | Educational | refused to ED (uses MS.O.A1.5 for mechanism) |
| 23 | Consumer | MS.O.A4.3 |
| 26 | History of Psychology | MS.M.3 (dated claims HI) |
| 30 | Hypnosis | MS.O.A1.2.3 |
| 34 | Environmental, Population and Conservation | MS.O.A4.1, MS.O.A5.4 |
| 38 | Health | MS.B.C3 (disorder MD) |
| 41 | Psychology-Law | MS.B.C3 (duty LA) |
| 46 | Media Psychology and Technology | MS.B.C5 |

Remaining divisions keep the audit's homes (§5a). Elsewhere (4): 21 → EN human factors; 24 → PL; 28 and 55 → MD. **Coverage: 49 of 49 eligible land (45 home, 4 elsewhere); 5 of 54 refused.**

**ASA sections (55 listed).** Refused (5): Asia and Asian America (area), Global MENA (area), Creative Sociology (in formation), Sociological Practice and Public Sociology (practice), Teaching and Learning (pedagogy). Elsewhere (3): Peace, War and Social Conflict and Political Sociology → PO; Human Rights → PO/LA. ## 2.9 EC — Economics

Standard v2 cited, not copied. Diagnosis: audit_mind_econ_business.md. v1 source: twelve.txt 3464–3729. Proofs are owned by MA, mostly MA.B.C4 (MSC 91, game theory and economics). The choice of game, and moves that reshape it, are ST.

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** Economics states how scarce resources are allocated among people with competing wants.
**Complication.** v1 let a theorem, the allocation it implies and the strategic game behind it share one cell. It stated welfare theorems, Arrow and Coase without hypotheses, and it confused VAT with sales tax.
**Question.** How can every economic claim be placed once, with the theorem, the allocation and the game kept apart?
**Answer.** Place it by the scale at which the allocation is settled. The theorem stays in MA and the choice of game stays in ST. EC owns the claim that this allocation results, or would result, in this economy.

### 2. Governing thought
Economics is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five scales of allocation: choice, markets, mechanisms and institutions, the aggregate economy, and welfare and public choice.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **the scale at which an allocation is settled.** Order: from one agent to the whole society.
1. Choice
2. Markets
3. Mechanisms and institutions
4. The aggregate economy
5. Welfare and public choice

**The three-way border (the economics repair).** Take any equilibrium result. The existence proof is MA (uses MA.O.A3 fixed-point theorems, via MA.B.C4). The choice of which game the parties are in, and any move that changes it, is ST. The claim that an actual or counterfactual allocation follows is EC.

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### EC.O.A1 Choice
**SCQA.** Allocation begins with agents choosing under constraints. Choice theory is precise, but people often depart from its axioms. What is claimed about how agents choose? Key line: preferences, choice under risk, behavioural departures, intertemporal choice.
- EC.O.A1.1 Preferences and demand — complete, transitive and continuous preferences have a utility representation (Debreu; proof uses MA). Demand theory follows, with the Slutsky decomposition.
- EC.O.A1.2 Choice under risk — expected utility follows from the von Neumann–Morgenstern axioms (completeness, transitivity, continuity, independence).
- EC.O.A1.3 Behavioural departures — the Allais paradox violates independence; prospect theory fits loss aversion. The psychological mechanism is MS; the allocative consequence is EC.
- EC.O.A1.4 Intertemporal choice — exponential discounting is time-consistent; quasi-hyperbolic discounting is not.

#### EC.O.A2 Markets
**SCQA.** Choices meet in markets, where prices coordinate them. When do markets clear, and with what result? Key line: competitive equilibrium, market power, information, factor markets.
- EC.O.A2.1 Competitive equilibrium — a general equilibrium exists given continuous, convex, locally non-satiated preferences, convex production, and endowments in the interior (Arrow–Debreu; proof MA).
- EC.O.A2.2 Market power — monopoly, oligopoly, entry. The equilibrium of the Cournot or Bertrand game is MA. Which game the firms are in is ST. The resulting prices and quantities are EC.
- EC.O.A2.3 Information — adverse selection (Akerlof), moral hazard, signalling. A market can unravel when quality is hidden and sellers know more than buyers.
- EC.O.A2.4 Factor markets — labour and capital; wages and returns.
- EC.O.A2.5 Tax incidence — in a competitive market, the burden falls by relative elasticities of supply and demand, not by who legally pays. Incidence is EC; the duty to pay is LA; computing the tax is BU.

#### EC.O.A3 Mechanisms and institutions
**SCQA.** Many allocations are settled by designed rules or by institutions, not by anonymous prices. Which rules produce which allocations? Key line: mechanism design, bargaining, transaction costs, institutions.
- EC.O.A3.1 Mechanism design — the revelation principle: any equilibrium outcome of a mechanism can be achieved by a direct, truthful mechanism (proof MA). Vickrey auctions make truthful bidding dominant for private values.
- EC.O.A3.2 Bargaining — Rubinstein: with two players, alternating offers, an infinite horizon, complete information and discount factors below one, there is a unique subgame-perfect equilibrium with immediate agreement. The theorem is MA. The choice of bargaining protocol is ST. The split is EC.
- EC.O.A3.3 Transaction costs — Coase: with well-defined property rights, zero transaction costs and no wealth effects, bargaining reaches an efficient outcome whatever the initial assignment. With positive costs the assignment matters, and that is the theorem's main use.
- EC.O.A3.4 Institutions — property rights, contracts and firms as allocative rules. Political authority is PO; legal validity is LA.
- EC.O.A3.5 Repeated interaction — folk theorem: in an infinitely repeated game with a discount factor close enough to one, any feasible, strictly individually rational payoff is sustainable in equilibrium (with a full-dimensionality condition for more than two players). Theorem MA; use in collusion or cooperation EC; choice of game ST.

#### EC.O.A4 The aggregate economy
**SCQA.** Output, prices and employment move together across an economy. What determines them? Key line: growth, fluctuations, money and inflation, open economies.
- EC.O.A4.1 Growth — the Solow model: with diminishing returns to capital and constant returns to scale, output per worker converges to a steady state, and long-run growth needs technical change.
- EC.O.A4.2 Fluctuations — business cycles; unemployment. The Phillips curve is an empirical relation that proved unstable once expectations adjusted.
- EC.O.A4.3 Money and inflation — the quantity equation MV = PY is an identity. A theory needs assumptions about V and Y. Central bank rules such as the Taylor rule are heuristics or policy prescriptions.
- EC.O.A4.4 Open economies — comparative advantage (Ricardo): with two goods and differing opportunity costs, both countries gain from specialisation and trade. Heckscher–Ohlin and Stolper–Samuelson hold under their stated factor and technology assumptions.
- EC.O.A4.5 Expectations and policy — the Lucas critique: relations estimated under one policy regime need not hold under another if agents' expectations change.

#### EC.O.A5 Welfare and public choice
**SCQA.** Societies judge and alter allocations collectively. When is an allocation good, and can a collective choose consistently? Key line: welfare theorems, market failure, public finance, social choice.
- EC.O.A5.1 First welfare theorem — with complete markets, price-taking agents, locally non-satiated preferences and no externalities, every competitive equilibrium is Pareto efficient. Theorem MA; use EC.
- EC.O.A5.2 Second welfare theorem — with convex preferences and technologies, continuity and lump-sum transfers, any Pareto-efficient allocation can be supported as a competitive equilibrium. Theorem MA; use EC.
- EC.O.A5.3 Market failure — externalities, public goods, common pool resources. Pigouvian taxes correct an externality when its marginal damage is known.
- EC.O.A5.4 Public finance — taxes and spending. A VAT is levied on value added at each stage, with credit for tax paid on inputs. A retail sales tax is levied once, on the final sale. Their economic incidence can be similar, but their collection differs.
- EC.O.A5.5 Social choice — Arrow: with at least three alternatives and a finite set of voters, no rule satisfies unrestricted domain, weak Pareto, independence of irrelevant alternatives and non-dictatorship together. Theorem MA; the meaning for collective choice is EC; the normative argument is PL.

| EC.M.4 | Experiment | M4 Intervene | field and laboratory experiments |
| EC.M.5 | Estimate and simulate | M5 Compute | structural estimation, DSGE |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
Ceteris paribus; "follow the money"; incentives matter; there is no free lunch; the representative agent as a convenience (flagged).

| EC.O.A2 | W5 + W1 | causal identification against a derived prediction | omitted variables, misspecified market definition |
| EC.O.A3 | W1 + W3 + W6 | theorem plus experimental or comparative evidence | hypotheses not met in the field |
| EC.O.A4 | W5 + W2 | identified macro evidence; model with stated fit | Lucas critique, small samples, revisions |
| EC.O.A5 | W1 + W9 + W5 | theorem; welfare argument; identified effect | contested welfare weights |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. Place a claim by the scale at which the allocation is settled.
2. The theorem is MA, the allocation is EC, the choice of game is ST.
3. Incidence is economics; the duty is law; the computation is business.
4. A firm's accounts and capital structure are BU. The general-equilibrium price of risk is EC.
5. A psychological mechanism is MS; its allocative effect is EC.
6. Political authority over an allocation is PO; the legal rule is LA.
7. A dated economic event is HI; the explanation of its allocation is EC.
8. A normative welfare argument is PL; the efficiency claim is EC.

|---|---|
| proofs of equilibrium, welfare, Arrow, folk and bargaining theorems | MA.B.C4 |
| choice of game and moves that change it | ST |
| tax duty and legal rules | LA |
| firm practice, accounts, tax computation | BU |
| psychological mechanism | MS |
| normative argument about justice | PL |
| dated economic events | HI |

## 2.10 BU — Business, trade, tax and accounting

Standard v2 cited, not copied. Diagnosis: audit_mind_econ_business.md. v1 source: twelve.txt 3730–3917.

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** Business knowledge states how firms record, finance, organise and sell, and how they trade and pay tax across borders.
**Complication.** v1 mixed practice with theory. It said Incoterms allocate title (they never do), confused VAT with sales tax, and shared finance theorems with EC.
**Question.** By what dimension can each business claim be placed once, with economic theory in EC and legal duty in LA?
**Answer.** By the practice of the firm the claim is about: its accounts, its finance, its operations and organisation, its marketing, and its trade and tax.

### 2. Governing thought
Business is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five practices of the firm: accounts, finance, operations and organisation, marketing, and trade and tax.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **the practice of the firm.** Order: from the record outward to customers and borders.
1. Accounts
2. Finance
3. Operations and organisation
4. Marketing
5. Trade and tax

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### BU.O.A1 Accounts
**SCQA.** A firm first keeps a record of what it owns, owes and earns. The rules for the record are conventions, set by standard setters. What do accounts claim, and under which rules? Key line: the record, reporting standards, management accounting, audit.
- BU.O.A1.1 The record — double entry: every transaction posts equal debits and credits, so assets = liabilities + equity. This is an identity of the convention (W12), not an empirical law.
- BU.O.A1.2 Reporting standards — IFRS (IASB) and US GAAP (FASB). They differ, for example in that US GAAP permits LIFO inventory and IFRS does not.
- BU.O.A1.3 Management accounting — costing, budgeting, variance analysis.
- BU.O.A1.4 Audit and assurance — an audit gives reasonable, not absolute, assurance that statements are free of material misstatement (W11).

#### BU.O.A2 Finance
**SCQA.** Firms raise and invest capital at a price set by markets. How should a firm value projects and choose its funding? Key line: valuation, capital structure, risk and return, derivatives.
- BU.O.A2.1 Valuation — net present value at the opportunity cost of capital. Discounted cash flow assumes the forecast and the rate.
- BU.O.A2.2 Capital structure — Modigliani–Miller: with no taxes, no bankruptcy costs, symmetric information and perfect capital markets, firm value does not depend on the debt–equity mix. With corporate tax, debt adds a tax shield. Theorem MA.B.C4; use BU.
- BU.O.A2.3 Risk and return — CAPM: with mean–variance investors, homogeneous expectations, frictionless markets and unlimited risk-free borrowing and lending, expected excess return is linear in beta. Empirical support is weak (W4 defeaters).
- BU.O.A2.4 Derivatives — Black–Scholes prices a European option assuming log-normal prices, constant volatility and rate, continuous frictionless trading and no dividends. Theorem MA.B.C4; use BU.
- BU.O.A2.5 Market efficiency — the efficient-market hypothesis comes in weak, semi-strong and strong forms. Tests face the joint-hypothesis problem: each needs a model of normal returns.

#### BU.O.A3 Operations and organisation
**SCQA.** Firms turn inputs into outputs through people and processes. What makes operations and organisations perform? Key line: operations, supply chains, organisation design, people management.
- BU.O.A3.1 Operations — Little's law L = λW holds for any stable queueing system in steady state (theorem MA.O.A5).
- BU.O.A3.2 Supply chains — inventory, logistics. The bullwhip effect amplifies demand variance upstream.
- BU.O.A3.3 Organisation design — structure, governance, agency. The agency problem in principal–agent form is EC.O.A2.3; its corporate remedy is BU.
- BU.O.A3.4 People management — human resources and industrial relations. Group behaviour is MS.
- BU.O.A3.5 Entrepreneurship and innovation — founding and scaling firms. A rival's response to a move is ST.

#### BU.O.A4 Marketing
**SCQA.** Firms must find and keep customers. What do marketing claims state? Key line: customers, products and brands, pricing practice, channels.
- BU.O.A4.1 Customers — segmentation, consumer behaviour. The cognition is MS.
- BU.O.A4.2 Products and brands.
- BU.O.A4.3 Pricing practice — list prices, discounts. Market equilibrium price is EC.
- BU.O.A4.4 Channels — retail, digital, distribution.

#### BU.O.A5 Trade and tax
**SCQA.** Firms sell across borders and owe tax. What does the firm's practice of trade and tax claim? Key line: trade terms, customs, tax computation, transfer pricing.
- BU.O.A5.1 Trade terms — Incoterms 2020 (ICC, eleven rules) allocate delivery tasks, costs and the passing of risk between seller and buyer. They do not allocate title. Title passes under the sale contract and the governing law (LA).
- BU.O.A5.2 Customs and trade compliance — tariff classification (Harmonized System, W12), rules of origin.
- BU.O.A5.3 Tax computation — a VAT is charged at each stage on the value added, with credit for input tax. A retail sales tax is charged once at the final sale. New Zealand GST is a VAT-type tax (15%).
- BU.O.A5.4 Transfer pricing — the arm's-length principle (OECD guidelines, W12). Its legal force is LA.
- BU.O.A5.5 Payments and trade finance — letters of credit (UCP 600, W12).

### 5. Face M — Methods

| ID | Role | Shared role | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| BU.M.1 | Record and report | M1 Represent | bookkeeping, financial statements |
| BU.M.2 | Value and plan | M2 Derive | DCF, budgeting |
| BU.M.3 | Survey the market | M3 Observe | market research |
| BU.M.4 | Compare cases | M6 Compare and reconstruct | case method, benchmarking |
| BU.M.5 | Audit | M7 Verify | external audit, compliance review |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
Rules of thumb for payback; the 80/20 rule; SWOT; Porter's five forces (a framework, not a law); "cash is king".

| BU.O.A2 | W1 + W4 | derivation under stated assumptions; market data | assumptions violated; estimation error |
| BU.O.A3 | W4 + W6 | measured performance; comparative cases | survivorship bias |
| BU.O.A4 | W4 + W3 | surveys; A/B tests | sampling bias |
| BU.O.A5 | W12 + W10 | stated rule (ICC, OECD, HS); legal force before a forum | jurisdictional difference |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. Place by the firm's practice.
2. The finance theorem is MA (MSC 91G); its use by the firm is BU; the market price of risk is EC.
3. The incidence of a tax is EC, the duty is LA, the computation is BU.
4. Title to goods is LA; Incoterms allocate risk and costs (BU).
5. A rival's strategic response is ST.
6. Behaviour of people inside firms is MS; the firm's management practice is BU.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused | Owner |

| finance theorems (MM, Black–Scholes proofs) | MA.B.C4 |
| equilibrium prices and incidence | EC |
| legal duty, title, enforceability | LA |
| strategic contest with rivals | ST |
| group psychology | MS |

## 2.11 ST — Strategy

Status: prototype (T/prototype_strategy.md) brought to Standard v2. Not restarted. Card IDs (e.g. [soc-c07]) and the Mechanism Concordance mapping M01–M27 are kept unchanged. The standard is cited, not copied (T/00_STANDARD_V2.md). Theorems are owned by MA and cited as "uses MA.B.C4 (91A)".

Repairs made in this pass:
- Governing thought drift fixed. The prototype said "two sides" while its key line had three partitions. It now uses the four-faces sentence.
- Heuristics IDs renamed from ST.H.n to ST.M.H.n, matching MA.M.H.
- A full Face W row set (A-level and sub-cell) and a registry were added.
- Named results now carry their hypotheses in the sentence (R5).
- The LCC U/V coverage is stated as "n of N".

### 1. SCQA

Situation: strategy is the theory of purposeful action against an adaptive opponent. It covers war, coercion, bargaining, grand strategy, irregular war, the information contest, competitive business strategy and judgement under uncertainty. Complication: its literature is filed by substrate (nuclear, cyber, business), by author or by period, so one mechanism appears under many names. Game theory is claimed by economics and mathematics, deterrence by politics, and competitive strategy by business. Question: what single partition places every strategic claim, method and heuristic exactly once, with clean borders to MA, EC, BU, PO, MS, LA, PL and HI? Answer: the four faces below, with objects divided by the feature of the strategic situation that a claim explains.

Source corpus: *The Encyclopedia of Strategy* (31 books, 10 theatres, 410 codex cards, a 10-axis Structural Diagnostic Panel, Mechanism Concordance M01–M27). External authority: LCC classes U and V.

### 2. Governing thought

Strategy is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges. Its objects are five features of the strategic situation: actors, the game, coercion, conduct, and information.

### 3. Key line

- Logic: inductive.
- Plural noun: features (of the strategic situation).
- Dimension: the feature of the strategic situation that the claim explains.
- Order: structural, from who contends to what each side knows.

1. **Actors** (ST.O.A1): who contends, how they align, and whether each can bind itself.
2. **The game** (ST.O.A2): the structure of payoffs and moves, and the moves that reshape it.
3. **Coercion** (ST.O.A3): how a threat of harm, held in reserve, changes another's choice, and how it escalates.
4. **Conduct** (ST.O.A4): how means are applied in time and space to reach a purpose.
5. **Information** (ST.O.A5): what each side knows, believes and can predict, and how that knowledge is contested.

Why this dimension. The Encyclopedia's own protocol forbids retrieval by "a scenario's own vocabulary" and requires retrieval "by structure". Its ten Panel axes are ten questions about features of the situation. They group into these five features without remainder (section 4, Panel table).

Rejected dimensions. The ten theatres mix substrate, method and record. The levels of strategy are a scale on which every mechanism recurs [luttwak-c17]. The medium or service is a substrate [wylie-c14]. Clausewitz's trinity [clausewitz-c03] does not place bargaining or forecasting. Purpose–ways–means reduces to conduct (A4.1).

CONTESTED: coercion could be folded into the game as A2.5, giving four features. It is kept separate for two reasons. Its operative move, a threat held in reserve, has its own mechanisms (M03, M06–M08, M18, M19). It also owes a different warrant: deterrence succeeds through non-events, which the game cell's warrants cannot see.

### 4. Face O — Objects

Named objects inherit the ID of the sub-cell they sit in (e.g. Rubinstein bargaining is an object of ST.O.A2.3). Card IDs are evidence pointers, not ST IDs.

#### ST.O.A1 Actors

**SCQA.** Every game has players. Strategic texts assume unitary actors, then explain failures by factions, agents and allies. What does strategy claim about the players themselves? Four things: what counts as a player, the system of players, their alignment, and their internal agency.

**Key line** (inductive; plural noun: aspects of the actor; order: from one actor to many, then inside one).
1. ST.O.A1.1 The unit: who counts as a player — state, regime, movement, population, firm, leader.
2. ST.O.A1.2 The system: the number and distribution of players' power, as the arena a player must use.
3. ST.O.A1.3 Alignment: how players combine, divide gains and defect.
4. ST.O.A1.4 Agency: whether a player acts directly or through agents, and whether it can bind itself.

**Named objects.**
- ST.O.A1.1 — Revolution, plot and insurgency as three ways to take power [galula-c01]. Asymmetry of tangible and intangible assets [galula-c02]. The cause [galula-c03]. Population support as the shared objective, gained through an active minority [galula-c04], [galula-c05], [galula-c07]. Political mobilization [mao-c13]. The rural base [wylie-c11]. Strategic culture (no card yet).
- ST.O.A1.2 — Five competitive forces [porter-c01]; barriers to entry [porter-c02]; strategic groups and mobility barriers [porter-c07]. Balancing versus buck-passing as a choice set by structure and geography [mearsheimer-c08]. The offshore balancer [mearsheimer-c05]. One power reshaping the system [kissinger-c01]. Border: the explanatory theory of the international system (offensive realism's five assumptions [mearsheimer-c01]; multipolarity and war [mearsheimer-c09]) is PO.O.A5.1. ST.O.A1.2 uses PO.O.A5.1.
- ST.O.A1.3 — Coalition value by marginal contribution (M26) [can_we_win-c14], [negot-c06]. Shapley value: in a transferable-utility cooperative game, it is the unique allocation satisfying efficiency, symmetry, null player and additivity (Shapley 1953; uses MA.B.C4 (91A)). Veto players [advanced-c14]. Triangular diplomacy [kissinger-c08]. Calculated betrayal and exit [can_we_win-c03], [can_we_win-c15], [can_we_win-c20]. Alliance entrapment and abandonment (no card yet). Minimum-winning coalitions as a claim about cabinets are PO.O.A2; ST.O.A1.3 uses PO.O.A2.
- ST.O.A1.4 — Delegation and mediation as strategic moves [soc-c05]. Career concerns [advanced-c13] (its reputation claim is M10, ST.O.A2.3). Moral hazard under a bonus-plus-fine contract [aos-c11]; the contract theorem is EC.O.A3.1. The succession problem of Realpolitik [kissinger-c06]. Mobilization schedules removing crises from political control [kissinger-c07]. Civil–military relations and delegation to machines (no card yet).

Panel axes homed: 6 (actor structure), 7 (agency). Concordance row homed: M26.

#### ST.O.A2 The game

**SCQA.** Strategic interaction can be written as players, moves and payoffs. The same surface situation can be zero-sum or mixed-motive, one-shot or repeated, and the classification decides the answer. What does strategy claim about the game? Four things: its class, how coordination happens, how time shapes it, and which moves change it.

**Key line** (inductive; plural noun: aspects of the game; order: from classifying the game to changing it).
1. ST.O.A2.1 Class: whether interests are opposed or admit joint gain, and what lies beneath stated positions.
2. ST.O.A2.2 Coordination: how parties converge without, or despite, communication.
3. ST.O.A2.3 Time: how sequence and repetition change what is rational.
4. ST.O.A2.4 Strategic moves: commitments, promises, randomization and redesign that change the game itself.

**Named objects.**
- ST.O.A2.1 — Zero-sum versus mixed-motive as the first diagnosis (M11) [gos-c01], [soc-c03], [can_we_win-c07], [negot-c08]. Payoffs beneath declared positions [can_we_win-c08]. The four 2×2 coordination and anti-coordination games [gos-c15]. Stag hunt as assurance [advanced-c02].
- ST.O.A2.2 — Focal points and tacit coordination (M09) [soc-c03], [arms-c05], [negot-c03]. Correlated equilibrium [advanced-c10]. Global games [advanced-c11]. Two-sided markets and tipping [advanced-c16]; the market allocation itself is EC.O.A2.
- ST.O.A2.3 — Reason backward from the end-state (M12) [aos-c01], [gos-c02], [can_we_win-c04], with its experimental limit [gos-c03]. Repetition and the shadow of the future (M10) [gos-c10], [negot-c11], [can_we_win-c12], [advanced-c13]. Rubinstein bargaining [gos-c14]: with two players, alternating offers, an infinite horizon, complete information and discount factors below one, there is a unique subgame-perfect equilibrium with immediate agreement (Rubinstein 1982; uses MA.B.C4 (91A)). Rollback bargaining and outside options [aos-c10]. Grim trigger with forgiveness [negot-c13]. Folk theorem: under infinite repetition, with discount factors close enough to one, any feasible and individually rational payoff can be sustained in equilibrium; for three or more players a full-dimensionality condition is also needed (uses MA.B.C4 (91A)).
- ST.O.A2.4 — Self-binding commitment (M01) [soc-c01], [soc-c02], [aos-c03], [advanced-c15]. Randomized commitment [soc-c06]. Mixed strategy when predictability is punished [gos-c06]. Issue linkage [kissinger-c09]. The negotiation as a designable tuple; BATNA architecture [negot-c04]. The mechanism-design menu [negot-c05]. Protocol as mechanism design [negot-c07]. Game redesign in business [can_we_win-c10].

Panel axes homed: 1 (payoff structure), 5 (time structure). Concordance rows homed: M01, M09, M10, M11, M12.

Border in one line: the theorem is MA.B.C4 (91A), the equilibrium as allocation is EC.O.A2, and the choice of game and of the move that changes it is ST.O.A2.

#### ST.O.A3 Coercion

**SCQA.** Schelling separates brute force, which takes, from coercion, which makes the other yield by threat [arms-c01]. Coercion works through events that do not happen, so success is hard to observe and easy to misread. What does strategy claim about coercion? Four things: the modes of threat, the manipulation of shared risk, the structure of escalation, and the stability of mutual threat.

**Key line** (inductive; plural noun: aspects of coercion; order: from the single threat to the stability of the whole relation).
1. ST.O.A3.1 Threat modes: deterrence prevents a change; compellence forces one; both depend on credibility and on connection to the demand.
2. ST.O.A3.2 Shared risk: brinkmanship coerces by raising a risk neither side fully controls.
3. ST.O.A3.3 Escalation: conflict rises through recognized thresholds, and the structure of the steps governs risk.
4. ST.O.A3.4 Stability: whether fear of the other's first move creates an incentive to move first.

**Named objects.**
- ST.O.A3.1 — Deterrence (M06) [arms-c02], [soc-c09], [brodie-c09], [kaplan-c08], [luttwak-c15]; relinquishing the initiative [soc-c04]. Compellence (M07) [arms-c03], [soc-c09], [buchanan-c08]. Action as message (M03) [arms-c04], [kaplan-c14], [buchanan-c01]. The credibility doctrine and its risk [kissinger-c10]. A trade threat and its credibility [gos-c05]. Existential stakes override deterrent calculus (M05) [suntzu-c11], [jervis-c03], [mao-c12]. Economic coercion and sanctions (corpus holds only [gos-c05]).
- ST.O.A3.2 — Brinkmanship (M08) [soc-c07], [soc-c08], [aos-c07], [gos-c11], [can_we_win-c18]; the Cuban case [aos-c06] (the dated event uses HI.O.A1).
- ST.O.A3.3 — Escalation as a bounded, stepped structure (M19) [kahn-c04], [kaplan-c09], [soc-c08], with its limit [freedman_strategists-c19]. Measures of escalation [kahn-c05]. The nuclear/conventional threshold as symbolic [soc-c10]. The nuclear taboo [arms-c09], [arms-c10]. No reliable threshold below the nuclear boundary [future_of_war-c10]. Below-threshold and grey-zone competition [buchanan-c02], [future_of_war-c01].
- ST.O.A3.4 — The security-dilemma spiral (M04) [soc-c11], [arms-c07], [soc-c12], [mearsheimer-c02], [freedman_strategists-c16], [jervis-c03]. Survivable second strike (M18) [arms-c06], [brodie-c09], [kaplan-c04]. Accidental war from arms-race growth [arms-c08]. Warning cannot be relied on [brodie-c10]. Stability of mutual deterrence [brodie-c15]. The MX shell game [kaplan-c16]. Arms control and verification as stability instruments (no card yet). Machine-speed escalation (no card yet).

Panel axes homed: 2 (coercion mode), 4 (first-move stability), 8 (stakes). Concordance rows homed: M03, M04, M05, M06, M07, M08, M18, M19.

CONTESTED: axis 8 and M05 could sit in ST.O.A1.1, because stakes are an actor's valuation. They are placed in A3.1 because the Panel uses the axis to test whether standard cost-imposition logic still holds.

#### ST.O.A4 Conduct

**SCQA.** Force and other means are applied over ground, sea, air and time. Classical texts state principles, and later theorists show the principles hold only inside unstated assumptions [wylie-c04], [brodie-c02]. What does strategy claim about conduct? Four things: the link of means to purpose, where effort is massed, the forms of offence and defence, and how time and sustainment shape the contest.

**Key line** (inductive; plural noun: aspects of conduct; order: from purpose to point to form to time).
1. ST.O.A4.1 Purpose and levels: means serve a political or corporate purpose, across levels from tactics to grand strategy.
2. ST.O.A4.2 Point of effort: where to mass means — the decisive point, the centre of gravity, weakness rather than strength.
3. ST.O.A4.3 Form and medium: offence against defence, attrition against annihilation, and the service theories of each medium.
4. ST.O.A4.4 Time, sustainment and control: culmination, protraction, sequence, friction, logistics and command in time.

**Named objects.**
- ST.O.A4.1 — War as the continuation of policy (M14) [clausewitz-c02], [mao-c06], [wylie-c15], [gray-c01]. The trinity [clausewitz-c03]. Positive and negative purpose [clausewitz-c05]; the object of war [mao-c05]. Strategy as control [wylie-c03], [wylie-c18]; as creating power [freedman-c02]; strategic effect [gray-c03]; nature and character [gray-c04]. The kernel: diagnosis, guiding policy, coherent action [gsbs-c01]. Levels: five levels in two dimensions [luttwak-c17]; the technical level is never autonomous [luttwak-c07]; grand strategy as top floor [luttwak-c14]; one fact valued differently at different levels [luttwak-c16]; tactics and strategy as one continuum [freedman_strategists-c07]. Operational art as campaign design (thin; [luttwak-c09]).
- ST.O.A4.2 — Concentration at the decisive point (M13) [clausewitz-c08], [suntzu-c07], [mao-c07], [advanced-c07]. Colonel Blotto: two players divide fixed budgets across battlefields, each won by the larger allocation; its mixed-strategy solution is owned by MA.B.C4 (91A). Centre of gravity (M27) [clausewitz-c11], [wylie-c16]; terrorism read through centre-of-gravity theory [wylie-c19]. Direct and indirect [suntzu-c06]; the indirect approach and its circularity [freedman-c11], [wylie-c13]. Victory as isolation of the insurgent [galula-c06]. Generic strategies and "stuck in the middle" [porter-c03], [porter-c04]. Chain-link systems [gsbs-c05].
- ST.O.A4.3 — Defence as the stronger form (M17) [clausewitz-c09], [luttwak-c10], [mearsheimer-c03]. Attrition versus annihilation (M16) [freedman-c10], [mao-c10], [luttwak-c09]. Mobile, guerrilla and positional war [mao-c09]. Maritime theory [wylie-c08]; the stopping power of water [mearsheimer-c05] (its grand-strategy use is A1.2). Air theory [wylie-c09], [brodie-c04], [brodie-c05], [brodie-c06], [brodie-c07], [wylie-c12], [luttwak-c13], [luttwak-c19]. Continental theory [wylie-c10]. The "nonstrategy" of naval, air and nuclear theory [luttwak-c12]. Cyber sabotage design poles [buchanan-c06], [buchanan-c07], [future_of_war-c21]. Targeted killing [future_of_war-c22]. Deliberate barbarism as instrument [future_of_war-c19]. Lanchester square law: with aimed fire, homogeneous forces and constant attrition coefficients, fighting strength scales with the square of numbers (uses MA.B.C4 (91A)). Space power and autonomous weapons as means (no cards yet).
- ST.O.A4.4 — Culminating point (M15) [clausewitz-c10], [luttwak-c03], [luttwak-c04]. Sequential versus cumulative strategy (M21) [wylie-c05], [wylie-c06], [galula-c10]. Mission command under a fixed intent (M20) [tetlock-c14], [gray-c02], [mao-c08]. Friction [clausewitz-c07]. Protraction: three stages [mao-c03], the jig-saw pattern [mao-c04], the economics of a protracted campaign [suntzu-c03]. The eight-step area strategy [galula-c09]. Paradoxical logic: good road and bad road [luttwak-c01], specialization defeating itself [luttwak-c05], the oversuccessful Maginot Line [luttwak-c11]. Industry evolution [porter-c08]. Logistics (no card yet; LCC U168).

Panel axis homed: 9 (accumulation type). Concordance rows homed: M13, M14, M15, M16, M17, M20, M21, M27.

#### ST.O.A5 Information

**SCQA.** Strategy is played on beliefs about capability, resolve, intention and the future. Private information, misperception, overconfidence and fat tails each defeat a plan that assumes the facts are known. What does strategy claim about information? Five things: how private information is revealed, how adversaries misperceive, how well judgement predicts, how exposure survives the tail, and how information is attacked.

**Key line** (inductive; plural noun: aspects of information; order: from equilibrium transmission to deliberate attack).
1. ST.O.A5.1 Signals: what a rational receiver infers from a costly or cheap message.
2. ST.O.A5.2 Perception: how decision-makers misread an adversary, in patterned ways.
3. ST.O.A5.3 Judgement: how accurately a strategist can estimate and forecast.
4. ST.O.A5.4 Exposure: how to hold a position that survives thin- or fat-tailed uncertainty.
5. ST.O.A5.5 The information contest: operations that create, steal, corrupt or deny the other's information.

Inner rule: a claim about inference inside an agreed game is A5.1. A claim about an operation outside that inference (deception, forgery, espionage) is A5.5.

**Named objects.**
- ST.O.A5.1 — Costly signalling separates types [aos-c04], [negot-c18], [advanced-c13]: a separating equilibrium exists when the signal's cost differs enough between types (uses MA.B.C4 (91A)). Adverse selection [aos-c05], [gos-c07] (as market failure it is EC.O.A2; here it is a counterpart's weapon). Self-selection [gos-c08]. Bayesian persuasion [advanced-c06]. The negotiator's dilemma and unravelling [negot-c14]. Information revealed across stages [negot-c15], [negot-c16]. Market signals [porter-c06]. Information cascades [advanced-c08]. Cursed equilibrium [advanced-c04].
- ST.O.A5.2 — Psychological versus operational milieu [jervis-c01]. Situational versus dispositional inference [jervis-c02]. Consistency, evoked sets, centrality, wishful thinking, dissonance [jervis-c04], [jervis-c05], [jervis-c08], [jervis-c11], [jervis-c12]. Lessons from firsthand and generational experience [jervis-c06], [jervis-c07]. Overestimating the adversary's coordination and one's own importance [jervis-c09], [jervis-c10]. Correctives [jervis-c13]. Mental models [heuer-c01]; mirror-imaging [heuer-c03]. Reference dependence (M22) [advanced-c05], [freedman-c18], [negot-c10], [can_we_win-c16]; the bias itself uses MS. Bounded rationality (M23) [can_we_win-c13], [heuer-c02], [advanced-c03]. Exploitable cognitive edges [negot-c09]. Trained incapacity [kahn-c14].
- ST.O.A5.3 — Confidence outruns accuracy (M24) [heuer-c04], [tetlock-c03], [negot-c09]. Outside view before inside view (M25) [tetlock-c08], [gsbs-c07], [gsbs-c08]. Forecasting as a learnable skill [tetlock-c01], [tetlock-c05], [tetlock-c07], [tetlock-c10], [tetlock-c11], [tetlock-c12], [tetlock-c13]; its limit [tetlock-c15]. Self-defeating prophecy [kahn-c01]. Real decisions are ill-posed [kahn-c11]. Imagined futures as causes; no dominant model of future war [future_of_war-c24], [future_of_war-c09].
- ST.O.A5.4 — Black Swan attributes [taleb_bs-c01]; Mediocristan and Extremistan [taleb_bs-c03]; the turkey problem [taleb_bs-c04]; narrative fallacy [taleb_bs-c05]; the antilibrary [taleb_bs-c02]. Fragile, robust, antifragile [taleb_af-c01]. Mitigate fragility first [taleb_af-c02]. Barbell [taleb_af-c04], [taleb_bs-c06]. Flexibility as a resource (M02) [kissinger-c05], [taleb_af-c05]. Skin in the game [taleb_af-c06].
- ST.O.A5.5 — All warfare is based on deception [suntzu-c02]; the artifice of deviation [suntzu-c08]. Spies and foreknowledge [suntzu-c13]; reading signs [suntzu-c10]. Active measures: definition, misconceptions, craft, epistemic erosion, asymmetry, digital acceleration, attribution [rid-c01]–[rid-c07]. Home-field advantage, NOBUS, fourth-party collection [buchanan-c03], [buchanan-c04], [buchanan-c05]. Hiding loose slot machines [aos-c13]. AI-enabled deception and decision support (no card yet).

Panel axes homed: 3 (information structure), 10 (uncertainty regime). Concordance rows homed: M02, M22, M23, M24, M25.
