### 5. Face M — Methods

Key line: inductive; plural noun: roles; dimension: what the practice does to a strategic claim; order: the Encyclopedia's protocol order, from diagnosis to audit. The prototype's role IDs ST.M.B1–B5 are kept.

1. Diagnose: writes the situation down — its features, its balance, its centre of gravity.
2. Model: writes the situation as a game and solves it.
3. Simulate and play: runs the contest artificially, with machines or people as the engine.
4. Compare cases: reads a claim across past contests and past strategists.
5. Estimate and score: states a probability, tests it against outcomes, and audits the plan.

| Role ID | Role | Maps to M1–M7 (secondary) | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| ST.M.B1 | Diagnose | M1 Represent (M3) | The ten-axis Panel; game classification [gos-c01]; Rumelt's diagnosis [gsbs-c01]; competitor analysis [porter-c05]; Gray's dimensions [freedman-c03]; net assessment (no card yet) |
| ST.M.B2 | Model | M2 Derive (M1) | Rollback [gos-c02]; dominance and best response [aos-c02]; mixed strategy by indifference [gos-c06]; Blotto [advanced-c07]; signalling and screening models; the evidence-graded inventory [advanced-c19] |
| ST.M.B3 | Simulate and play | M5 Compute (M4) | Wargames (LCC U310–310.2); red-cell play; campaign simulation; systems analysis [kaplan-c10]; the theory/combat discrepancy as its limit [kaplan-c03] |
| ST.M.B4 | Compare cases | M6 Compare and reconstruct (M3) | Structured case comparison; reconstruction of strategists [wylie-c01], [gray-c06]; the record of futurology [future_of_war-c24]; event datasets [future_of_war-c12], [future_of_war-c13]; "scavenging" [freedman_strategists-c04] |
| ST.M.B5 | Estimate and score | M7 Verify (M5) | Analysis of Competing Hypotheses [heuer-c05]; Brier scoring [tetlock-c02]; Fermi decomposition [tetlock-c09]; tournament design [tetlock-c04]; red-team review of a plan; the Encyclopedia's Doctrine Panel and Citation Audit |

Notes.
- No M4 role. A strategist rarely runs a controlled intervention on the real contest, because the opponent adapts to the probe. The probe (reconnaissance in force, a test of resolve, a market trial) is a secondary of ST.M.B3.
- Red-teaming has two homes by function. Playing the adversary in a game is ST.M.B3. Critiquing a finished plan from the adversary's view is ST.M.B5.
- Game theory ownership. Von Neumann's minimax theorem (finite two-person zero-sum games, mixed strategies) and Nash's existence theorem (finite games, mixed strategies) are MA.B.C4 (91A). Their use as an account of allocation is EC.O.A2. Their use against an adaptive opponent is ST.M.B2.
- Operations research (MSC 90) is MA.B.C4, used by ST.M.B2 and ST.M.B3.

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)

A heuristic is a rule of thumb with known failures. It is not a result and not a role. IDs renamed from ST.H.n to ST.M.H.n.

| ID | Heuristic | Source | Known failure |
|---|---|---|---|
| ST.M.H.1 | Supreme excellence is to break resistance without fighting. | [suntzu-c04] | Fails against an actor with existential stakes (M05). |
| ST.M.H.2 | Know the enemy and know yourself. | [suntzu-c04] | Says nothing about how to know. |
| ST.M.H.3 | All warfare is based on deception. | [suntzu-c02] | A stance; the claim about deception's effect is ST.O.A5.5. |
| ST.M.H.4 | The obvious course is the anticipated one; prefer the bad road. | [luttwak-c01] | Holds only when the adversary reacts. |
| ST.M.H.5 | The principles of war (mass, surprise, economy of force). | [brodie-c02], [wylie-c04] | Principles conflict with each other. |
| ST.M.H.6 | Attack needs three-to-one. | [luttwak-c10] | Depends on level and on the measure of force. |
| ST.M.H.7 | Never let survival ride on one engagement. | [mao-c12] | Can forfeit a fleeting decisive opportunity. |
| ST.M.H.8 | Think from the end. | [can_we_win-c04] | Slogan form of M12; fails where rollback fails [gos-c03]. |
| ST.M.H.9 | Five dangerous faults of a general. | [suntzu-c09] | A character checklist, not a predictor. |
| ST.M.H.10 | Weinberger's six tests; Blair's five tests. | [freedman_strategists-c01], [freedman_strategists-c02] | Satisfiable by rhetoric. |
| ST.M.H.11 | The dicta of strategy; fourteen trinities. | [gray-c04], [gray-c05], [gray-c07] | Each dictum needs a case to bite. |
| ST.M.H.12 | Ten commandments for superforecasters. | [tetlock-c16] | Validated only on short-horizon tournament questions. |
| ST.M.H.13 | Via negativa: remove before you add. | [taleb_af-c03] | Can justify inaction where action is required. |
| ST.M.H.14 | Mirror-imaging is the default sin. | [heuer-c03] | An adversary can share one's logic. |
| ST.M.H.15 | The five-question mental firewall; "which game are you trapped in?" | [can_we_win-c01], [can_we_win-c02] | No scored evidence. |
| ST.M.H.16 | Do not rely on the enemy's command errors. | [mao-c11] | A caution, not a rule for exploiting them. |
| ST.M.H.17 | Tactical negotiation playbooks ("read the opponent"). | [negot-c19] | The corpus records a game-theoretic verdict against them. |

### 6. Face W — Warrants

Controlled list W1–W13 from the standard. A-level rows state the cell's default; each sub-cell has its own row. Named objects share their sub-cell's row. W13 is never used in ST.

| Cell ID | Warrant W# (secondary) | Standard of acceptance | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|
| ST.O.A1 | W6 (W1) | The actor model predicts across cases with different actor types. | Unitary-actor assumption; selection on survivors. |
| ST.O.A1.1 | W6 (W7) | The actor claim holds across cases with different actor types. | Factions hidden by the unitary assumption; selection on successful movements. |
| ST.O.A1.2 | W6 (W9) | Structure predicts choices across systems, with stated scope. | Indeterminacy the theory admits [mearsheimer-c04]; few systems. |
| ST.O.A1.3 | W1 (W6) | The coalition result holds under the stated value function; cases show the predicted split. | Utility not transferable; trust and identity outside the model. |
| ST.O.A1.4 | W1 (W6) | The delegation result follows from stated information and incentives. | Agents' incentives misdescribed; ratification rules unknown. |
| ST.O.A2 | W1 (W6) | A solved model plus evidence that the payoffs hold. | Payoffs asserted, not evidenced. |
| ST.O.A2.1 | W12 (W9) | The classification grid is explicit and applied consistently. | A mixed-motive case misread as zero-sum. |
| ST.O.A2.2 | W1 (W3, laboratory) | Equilibrium-selection argument plus laboratory or field convergence. | Salience is culture-bound; communication changes the game. |
| ST.O.A2.3 | W1 (W3, laboratory) | Backward induction or repeated-game result under stated discounting. | Laboratory evidence against rollback [gos-c03]; finite horizons unravel cooperation. |
| ST.O.A2.4 | W1 (W6) | The move changes the equilibrium under stated payoffs; a case shows the change. | Commitment not observable or reversible. |
| ST.O.A3 | W6 (W1) | The mechanism is shown in cases where rival explanations fail. | Success is a non-event; few cases. |
| ST.O.A3.1 | W6 (W1) | As A3, with the threat and the demand both documented. | Selection of crises that escalated; signal failure [kaplan-c14]. |
| ST.O.A3.2 | W1 (W6) | A probabilistic-threat model fits the stated beliefs of the parties [gos-c11]. | Very few nuclear crises; beliefs reconstructed after the fact. |
| ST.O.A3.3 | W9 (W6) | Thresholds identified in advance and respected in cases. | The ladder metaphor misleads [freedman_strategists-c19]; thresholds shift with technology. |
| ST.O.A3.4 | W1 (W2) | Stability condition derived; vulnerability computed under stated assumptions [kaplan-c04]. | Assumed rationality under stress; warning failure [brodie-c10]. |
| ST.O.A4 | W6 (W7) | The pattern holds across campaigns of differing type and era. | Hindsight; unstated service assumptions. |
| ST.O.A4.1 | W9 (W8) | Argument from definitions, traced to classical texts read in context. | Concept stretching; anachronistic reading of Clausewitz [wylie-c15]. |
| ST.O.A4.2 | W6 (W7) | The claim holds across campaigns of differing type and era. | Hindsight names the "decisive point"; narrative fallacy [taleb_bs-c05]. |
| ST.O.A4.3 | W6 (W7, W4) | Comparative evidence across theatres; measured outcomes where recorded. | Confounded tests [brodie-c07]; unstated service assumptions [wylie-c14]. |
| ST.O.A4.4 | W6 (W7) | The pattern recurs across protracted and short wars. | The culminating point is identifiable only after the fact. |
| ST.O.A5 | W1, W4, W6 by sub-cell | As the sub-cell rows. | As the sub-cell rows. |
| ST.O.A5.1 | W1 (W3) | Separating or pooling equilibrium under stated costs; experimental support. | Costs not observable to the receiver; multiple equilibria. |
| ST.O.A5.2 | W6 (W3) | The pattern appears in documented decisions and in controlled studies. | Laboratory-to-crisis transfer; documents written to justify. |
| ST.O.A5.3 | W4 (W3) | Scored accuracy (Brier) against outcomes, sample size stated [tetlock-c02]. | Short horizons; tournament questions unlike strategic ones [tetlock-c15]. |
| ST.O.A5.4 | W9 (W4) | Argument from the payoff's convexity, supported by tail data where it exists. | Tail type unknown; "antifragile" unfalsifiable when undefined in advance. |
| ST.O.A5.5 | W7 (W6) | Attribution and effect established from dated records [rid-c07]. | Covert record; contested attribution; unmeasurable effect. |

Observation the table forces: W3 is almost absent outside laboratory sub-claims. An adaptive opponent makes the strategic world non-stationary. Strategy's characteristic warrants are W1 for the model and W6 for its use. A claim that has only W1 has not yet shown it is about a real contest.

Key line: inductive; plural noun: bridges; dimension: the feature the community takes as home; order: structural, matching Face O.

| ST.B.C2 | Bargaining and competitive strategy (negotiation, business strategy, platform strategy) | ST.O.A2 (boundary ST.O.A1.2, EC.O.A2) | ST.M.B1, ST.M.B2, ST.M.B5 | The negotiation; the industry |

| ST.B.C5 | Intelligence and the information contest (intelligence analysis, forecasting, cyber and information operations, strategic risk) | ST.O.A5 (boundary ST.O.A3.1 for cyber coercion) | ST.M.B5, ST.M.B4 | Intelligence services; networks |

Notes. Counterinsurgency is C4 because its theory of victory is conduct [galula-c09], [galula-c10]. Galula's claim that the population is the decisive actor sits in ST.O.A1.1, and C4 cites it. Cyber operations are C5 because their value lies "almost entirely in shaping" [buchanan-c01]; cyber coercion stays in ST.O.A3.1. Business strategy is C2 on the industry substrate: five forces at A1.2, entry deterrence at A3.1, generic strategies at A4.2.

### 8. Assignment rules

1. **Unit is the claim.** A codex card can hold two claims; each gets its own home. Five cards sit in two Concordance rows and are split by claim (section 11).
2. **Adaptive-other rule.** A claim belongs here only if its truth depends on an opponent, rival or counterpart who reacts. A choice against a non-reacting environment is EC.O.A1 (decision) or MA.B.C4 (operations research). CONTESTED exception: ST.O.A5.3 and ST.O.A5.4 are kept, because the corpus treats markets and nature as environments whose response to exposure is adversarial in effect. Alternative: move both to EC.O.A1.
3. **Threat rule.** If the operative move is a conditional threat to impose cost, the claim is ST.O.A3, whatever the instrument. A move that restructures the game without a threat is ST.O.A2.
4. **Medium rule.** Land, sea, air, space, cyber and the information environment are substrates. Coercion through a medium is A3; force applied in it is A4; information created, stolen or corrupted in it is A5.5.
5. **Level rule.** Tactical, operational, strategic and grand-strategic are a scale, not cells [luttwak-c17]. A claim about the levels themselves is ST.O.A4.1.
6. **Case rule.** A dated event (Cannae, Barbarossa, Cuba 1962) is a case. It is evidence handled by ST.M.B4; its historical truth uses HI.O.A1.
7. **Game rule (from MA and EC).** The theorem is MA, the allocation is EC, the choice of game is ST. Test: does the claim change if the opponent can change the game? If yes, ST.
8. **Politics rule.** Why polities go to war is PO.O.A5.1 (Fearon's bargaining failure). How a party coerces or fights is ST.
9. **Business rule.** The firm's conversion, cost, capital and governance are BU.O.A3. Positioning against rivals who react is ST.
10. **Mind rule.** A cognitive bias as a process is MS. Its exploitation or correction against a counterpart is ST.O.A5.2.
11. **Law and philosophy rule.** Whether a use of force is lawful is LA. Whether it is just is PL. Whether it works is ST.
12. **Artefact rule.** How a weapon or exploit works is EN or CS. What it does to the contest is ST.
13. **No Other.** A strategic claim that will not reduce to one feature is a test of the dimension, not a residual cell.

Proposed edits to other pyramids (recorded, not imposed): BU.O.A3 drops "strategy is the choice of which conversion" in favour of "uses ST.O.A1.2, ST.O.A4.2". PO.O.A5.1 keeps war onset and cites ST.O.A3.1 for deterrence and ST.O.A1.3 for alliance credibility (adopted in p2_PO.md). EC.O.A2 adds "used by ST.O.A2". MS judgement heuristics add "used by ST.O.A5.2".

## 2.12 PO — Politics

Standard v2 cited, not copied. Diagnosis: audit_politics_law_philosophy.md. v1 source: twelve.txt 3918–4172.

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** Politics studies who holds power over a community, how they gain and keep it, and what they do with it.
**Complication.** v1 was master-level only. It mixed normative argument about legitimacy (PL) with empirical claims, and it misstated selectorate theory (the key quantity is the winning coalition).
**Question.** By what dimension can every empirical claim about political power be placed once?
**Answer.** By the aspect of power the claim concerns: its authority, its institutions, the behaviour of the governed, its policies, and its relations beyond the state.

### 2. Governing thought
Politics is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five aspects of power: authority, institutions, political behaviour, policy and administration, and relations among states.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **the aspect of power the claim concerns.** Order: from the source of authority outward.
1. Authority
2. Institutions
3. Political behaviour
4. Policy and administration
5. Relations among states

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### PO.O.A1 Authority
**SCQA.** Power is political when it claims the right to rule. Empirical politics can study whether people accept that claim, though not whether they should. What is claimed about authority? Key line: states and sovereignty, legitimacy as belief, regimes.
- PO.O.A1.1 States and sovereignty — Weber's state: a community that successfully claims a monopoly of legitimate force in a territory.
- PO.O.A1.2 Legitimacy as belief — measured acceptance of a ruler's right. Whether the rule is justified is PL.
- PO.O.A1.3 Regimes — democracy, autocracy, hybrid regimes, classified by stated criteria (V-Dem, Polity: W12 for coding rules).

#### PO.O.A2 Institutions
**SCQA.** Authority acts through rules and organisations. Which rules produce which outcomes? Key line: constitutions, electoral systems, parties, legislatures and executives.
- PO.O.A2.1 Constitutional design — presidential and parliamentary systems, federalism. The legal text is LA; its political effect is PO.
- PO.O.A2.2 Electoral systems — Duverger's law: plurality rule in single-member districts tends toward two-party competition. It is a tendency with known exceptions (e.g. India, Canada).
- PO.O.A2.3 Parties — party systems, organisation, cleavages.
- PO.O.A2.4 Legislatures and executives — veto players: policy stability rises with the number and ideological distance of veto players (Tsebelis).
- PO.O.A2.5 Selectorate theory — leaders keep office by rewarding a winning coalition W drawn from a selectorate S. When W is small relative to S, leaders favour private goods over public ones (Bueno de Mesquita et al., 2003).

#### PO.O.A3 Political behaviour
**SCQA.** The governed vote, protest, believe and organise. What explains their political action? Key line: voting, public opinion, participation and movements, conflict within states.
- PO.O.A3.1 Voting — the median voter theorem: with one policy dimension, single-peaked preferences and majority rule, the median voter's ideal point beats any other. Theorem MA.B.C4; use PO.
- PO.O.A3.2 Public opinion — attitudes measured by survey. The cognitive mechanism is MS.
- PO.O.A3.3 Participation and social movements — turnout, collective action. Olson: large groups under-provide collective goods without selective incentives.
- PO.O.A3.4 Conflict within states — civil war onset, coups, revolution. The conduct of the fighting is ST.

#### PO.O.A4 Policy and administration
**SCQA.** Governments make and carry out policy. How is policy made and delivered? Key line: agenda and decision, bureaucracy, implementation, policy effects.
- PO.O.A4.1 Agenda and decision — punctuated equilibrium; the multiple streams model (a framework, W9).
- PO.O.A4.2 Bureaucracy — delegation, principal–agent control (theory EC.O.A2.3).
- PO.O.A4.3 Implementation — the gap between decision and delivery.
- PO.O.A4.4 Policy effects — the causal effect of a policy is owned by the field of the outcome (EC, MD, ED), and PO cites it. The political cause of the policy is PO.

#### PO.O.A5 Relations among states
**SCQA.** States act in a system without a common government. What patterns hold among them? Key line: international order, cooperation and institutions, war and peace.
- PO.O.A5.1 International order — anarchy, balance of power, hegemony. Realist, liberal and constructivist claims are competing programmes (CONTESTED).
- PO.O.A5.2 Cooperation and international institutions — regimes, alliances, the UN. The treaty's legal force is LA.
- PO.O.A5.3 War and peace — the democratic peace: pairs of democracies rarely fight each other. It is an empirical regularity; its causes are CONTESTED. The strategy of coercion and conduct is ST.

### 5. Face M — Methods

| ID | Role | Shared role | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| PO.M.1 | Theorise and model | M1 Represent, M2 Derive | formal models, typologies |
| PO.M.2 | Survey and measure | M3 Observe | election studies, indices |
| PO.M.3 | Identify causes | M3 Observe (causal) | natural experiments |
| PO.M.4 | Experiment | M4 Intervene | field experiments on turnout |
| PO.M.5 | Compare cases | M6 Compare and reconstruct | comparative historical analysis, process tracing |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
"All politics is local"; follow the coalition; "where you stand depends on where you sit"; the iron law of oligarchy (CONTESTED as a law).

### 6. Face W — Warrants

| Cell | Warrant | Standard | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|
| PO.O.A1 | W4 + W6 | measured beliefs; comparative coding | coder bias, concept stretching |
| PO.O.A2 | W5 + W1 + W6 | identified effect of a rule; formal result; cases | endogenous institutions |
| PO.O.A3 | W4 + W3 + W5 | survey with stated error; experiment; identified effect | social desirability bias |
| PO.O.A4 | W6 + W7 | case studies; the documentary record | selection on the dependent variable |
| PO.O.A5 | W6 + W5 | cross-national datasets, comparison | few cases, rare events |

### 7. Face B — Bridges

| ID | Field | Cells | Methods | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|

| PO.B.C7 | Political theory (shared) | A1.2 | M.1 | concepts (the argument is PL) |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. Place an empirical claim by the aspect of power.
2. Whether rule is justified is PL; whether people believe it is justified is PO.
3. Legal validity is LA; the political cause or effect of a law is PO.
4. The policy's causal effect belongs to its outcome field; its political origin is PO.
5. The conduct of war, coercion and the choice of game are ST; war onset and the international order are PO.
6. The theorem is MA; its political use is PO.
7. A dated political event is HI; its explanation is PO.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused | Owner |

| normative political theory | PL |
| legal validity of rules and treaties | LA |
| conduct of war and coercion | ST |
| dated political events | HI |
| median voter and social choice theorems | MA.B.C4 |
| policy outcomes in health, economy, education | MD, EC, ED |

Other 44 groups go elsewhere: 4401 Anthropology → MS; 4402 Criminology → MS and LA; 4403 Demography → MS; 4404 Development studies → EC and PO; 4405 Gender studies → MS; 4406 Human geography → MS and EA; 4409 Social work → MS; 4410 Sociology → MS.

## 2.13 LA — Law

Standard v2 cited, not copied. Diagnosis: audit_politics_law_philosophy.md. v1 source: twelve.txt 4173–4363.

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** Law states what is valid, owed and permitted within a legal system, and how a forum decides it.
**Complication.** v1's governing thought listed four relations ending "order a remedy", while its key line listed five ending "constitute the forum". This breaks R7. v1 also had no first descent.
**Question.** By what dimension can every legal claim be placed once, with all members stated identically?
**Answer.** By the legal relation the claim states. There are five relations: what counts as law, what people owe each other, what follows a wrong, what public power may do, and how a forum decides.

### 2. Governing thought
Law is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five legal relations: sources, obligations, wrongs and remedies, public power, and forums and proof.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **the legal relation the claim states.** Order: from validity to decision.
1. Sources
2. Obligations
3. Wrongs and remedies
4. Public power
5. Forums and proof

R7 repair: v1's "order a remedy" is now member 3, "Wrongs and remedies". v1's "constitute the forum" is member 5, "Forums and proof". All three places now list the same five members.

Every legal claim is indexed to a system and a date: "under New Zealand law at 2026". A claim without its system is not placeable.

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### LA.O.A1 Sources
**SCQA.** Before any duty, a legal claim needs a valid source. Systems differ in what they count as law. What is law in this system? Key line: legislation, case law, custom and tikanga, international law.
- LA.O.A1.1 Legislation — statutes and delegated legislation, valid when made by the procedure the system recognises.
- LA.O.A1.2 Case law — precedent binds lower courts in common-law systems (stare decisis); its weight in civil-law systems is lower.
- LA.O.A1.3 Custom and tikanga — in New Zealand, the Supreme Court in Ellis v R (2022) held that tikanga Māori forms part of the common law, as a source to be applied with care (UNVERIFIED wording; Māori-led review required). The tikanga itself, as tradition-internal law, is owned by IK.
- LA.O.A1.4 International law — treaties and custom (ICJ Statute art. 38). Domestic effect depends on the system.
- LA.O.A1.5 Validity — what makes a rule law in a system (for example Hart's rule of recognition). The concept analysis is PL; the claim that a rule is valid here is LA.

#### LA.O.A2 Obligations
**SCQA.** Valid law creates duties and rights between persons. What is owed, and by whom? Key line: contract, property, trusts, family and succession.
- LA.O.A2.1 Contract — formation needs offer, acceptance, consideration (in common law) and intention to create legal relations.
- LA.O.A2.2 Property — title, possession, land registration (Torrens in NZ and Australia). Title to goods sold passes under the sale contract and the governing law, not under Incoterms.
- LA.O.A2.3 Trusts and equity.
- LA.O.A2.4 Family and succession.
- LA.O.A2.5 Tax duty — the liability to pay, set by statute. Incidence is EC and computation is BU.

#### LA.O.A3 Wrongs and remedies
**SCQA.** When duties are broken, law states the wrong and what follows. Key line: torts, crimes, remedies.
- LA.O.A3.1 Torts — negligence requires a duty of care, breach, causation and damage. New Zealand's accident compensation scheme (ACC) bars most personal-injury suits.
- LA.O.A3.2 Crimes — liability needs the prohibited act and the fault element, unless the offence is strict.
- LA.O.A3.3 Remedies — damages, injunctions, specific performance, sentences.

#### LA.O.A4 Public power
**SCQA.** Law also binds the state. What may public power do? Key line: constitutional law, administrative law, human rights, Te Tiriti o Waitangi.
- LA.O.A4.1 Constitutional law — New Zealand has no single entrenched constitution; it relies on statutes such as the Constitution Act 1986, conventions and Te Tiriti.
- LA.O.A4.2 Administrative law — judicial review for illegality, irrationality and procedural impropriety.
- LA.O.A4.3 Human rights — NZ Bill of Rights Act 1990: rights may be subject to reasonable limits demonstrably justified in a free and democratic society (s 5).
- LA.O.A4.4 Te Tiriti o Waitangi (1840) — its legal force depends on incorporation in statute. The Waitangi Tribunal (est. 1975) makes recommendations, mostly not binding.

#### LA.O.A5 Forums and proof
**SCQA.** A legal claim is settled before a forum, on facts proved to a standard. What forum decides, and how? Key line: jurisdiction and courts, procedure, evidence and standards of proof, dispute resolution.
- LA.O.A5.1 Jurisdiction and courts — which forum may hear a claim. The NZ Supreme Court replaced Privy Council appeals from 2004.
- LA.O.A5.2 Procedure — pleadings, appeal.
- LA.O.A5.3 Evidence and standards of proof — beyond reasonable doubt in criminal cases; balance of probabilities in civil cases.
- LA.O.A5.4 Arbitration and alternative dispute resolution — awards are enforceable under the New York Convention (1958) in contracting states.

### 5. Face M — Methods

| ID | Role | Shared role | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| LA.M.1 | State the rule | M1 Represent | drafting, codification |
| LA.M.2 | Interpret | M2 Derive | statutory interpretation, legal argument |
| LA.M.3 | Find facts | M3 Observe | evidence before a forum |
| LA.M.4 | Compare systems | M6 Compare and reconstruct | comparative law, legal history |
| LA.M.5 | Adjudicate | M7 Verify | judgment by a forum |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
Canons of construction (expressio unius, ejusdem generis); "hard cases make bad law"; read the statute first.

### 6. Face W — Warrants

| Cell | Warrant | Standard | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|
| LA.O.A1 | W10 + W12 | valid source under the system's recognition rule | repeal, inconsistency with a higher source |
| LA.O.A2 | W10 | source plus proved facts | defences, vitiating factors |
| LA.O.A3 | W10 | elements proved to the standard | defences, limitation periods |
| LA.O.A4 | W10 + W9 | source plus argued interpretation before a court | non-justiciability |
| LA.O.A5 | W10 | the forum's own rules of procedure and evidence | lack of jurisdiction |

W10 does not travel. A judgment that a fact is proved settles it for the forum, not for HI or PH.

### 7. Face B — Bridges

| ID | Field | Cells | Methods | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| LA.B.C1 | Private law | A2, A3.1 | M.2 | persons |
| LA.B.C2 | Criminal law | A3.2, A5.3 | M.2, M.3 | offences |
| LA.B.C3 | Public law | A4 | M.2 | the state |
| LA.B.C4 | International law | A1.4 | M.2, M.4 | states |
| LA.B.C5 | Commercial and tax law | A2.1, A2.5 | M.2 | businesses (uses BU) |
| LA.B.C6 | Māori and Indigenous law | A1.3, A4.4 | M.2, M.4 | iwi, hapū (uses IK) |
| LA.B.C7 | Jurisprudence (shared) | A1.5 | M.2 | concepts (argument PL) |
| LA.B.C8 | Law and economics | A2, A3 | M.2 | rules (uses EC) |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. Place by the legal relation, indexed to a system and a date.
2. Incidence is economics; the duty is law.
3. What law is, as a concept, is PL; what is law in this system is LA.
4. A dated event in legal history is HI; the rule then in force is LA.
5. The political cause of a law is PO.
6. Tikanga as tradition is IK; tikanga as recognised by a state court is LA.
7. A proved fact is settled for the forum only (W10 does not travel).

## 2.14 PL — Philosophy

Sources: v1 text twelve.txt 4364–4796; diagnosis audit_politics_law_philosophy.md (PL errors 1–19, fact check, duplicates, PhilPapers table). Standard: 00_STANDARD_V2.md (cited, not copied). Mathematics owner: maths_reconciled.md. Theology is RE·T in the current set; the audit's proposed "TH" is read as RE.O·T throughout.

Fact status. PhilPapers figures (7 top-level areas, 49 second-level, 6,164 categories) and the audit's 25 checked claims are taken as checked by the audit (checked: philpapers.org/browse/all, 2026-10-08). Other dates and attributions from standard reference knowledge carry (UNVERIFIED) where they are not in the audit.

### 1. SCQA

Situation. Philosophy asks what there is, how it is known, what follows from what, and what is worth doing. PhilPapers sorts its literature into seven top-level areas and 6,164 categories, and lets a category have more than one parent. Complication. Multiple parents double-count: a paper on consciousness is filed under mind and cognitive science, and a paper on Kant under history and epistemology. Traditions look like extra questions, and two PhilPapers areas are miscellany bins. Version 1 also broke its own "philosophy of X" rule for law and mind, and gave Nyāya two homes. Question. What single partition places every philosophical claim, practice and tradition exactly once, and states what PL owns against MA, MS, PO, LA, AR and RE?

### 2. Governing thought

Philosophy is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges. Its objects divide into five claim-types: being, knowledge and mind, meaning and inference, value, and the standing of the sciences and mathematics.

### 3. Key line

Logic inductive · plural noun claim-types · dimension what the question is about · order structural (being, knowing, saying, valuing, the sciences).

1. Being
2. Knowledge and mind
3. Meaning and inference
4. Value
5. Standing of the sciences and mathematics

Phase 2 repair (R7, owner's brief item 3): the earlier draft put the four faces in the key line. The key line is now the Face O partition; the faces are named in the governing thought.

### 4. Face O — Objects

Key line of Face O. Logic inductive · plural noun claim-types · dimension what the question is about · order structural (being, knowing, saying, valuing, the sciences).

1. PL.O.A1 Being
2. PL.O.A2 Knowledge and mind
3. PL.O.A3 Meaning and inference
4. PL.O.A4 Value
5. PL.O.A5 Standing of the sciences and mathematics

Restated "philosophy of X" rule (audit error 11). What X is entitled to assert goes to A5. What X may enforce, or ought to do, goes to A4. What the X-object is goes to A1 or A2. Philosophy of mind is therefore A2.3, and philosophy of law is A4.3, by this rule and not as exceptions.

#### PL.O.A1 Being

SCQA. Situation: metaphysics asks what exists and what the most general features of existing things are. Complication: ontology, modality, identity, free will and philosophy of religion are taught separately. In v1 four bullets mapped to no member, and the realism member had no bullet. Question: which metaphysical questions are distinct and jointly exhaustive?

Key line. Logic inductive · plural noun metaphysical questions · dimension the feature of being asked about · order structural (what, how possibly, how through change, whether apart from us).

1. PL.O.A1.1 Ontology and existence
2. PL.O.A1.2 Modality
3. PL.O.A1.3 Causation, persistence and identity
4. PL.O.A1.4 Realism and anti-realism

Named objects.
- PL.O.A1.1 Ontology and existence. Particular and universal; object and property; tropes; substance; mereology; abstract objects against nominalism. Existence answers: materialism, idealism, dualism, neutral monism. These are answers, not further questions.
- PL.O.A1.1 also holds the god question argued from shared premises. Cosmological, design and ontological arguments are arguments here (W9). The logical problem of evil holds only if a god is defined as omnipotent, omniscient and wholly good. The ontological argument as an argument from premises any reasoner may grant is PL.O.A1.1; the RE prototype's rule agrees.
- PL.O.A1.1 holds Māori whakapapa read as an ontology of relational descent (Stewart, *Māori Philosophy*, 2021, per audit). The claim, not the community, sits here.
- PL.O.A1.2 Modality. Possible worlds as a device; de re and de dicto; essentialism. Rule: a necessity claim must say which type it is: logical, metaphysical or nomological. Modal systems K, T, S4 and S5 as calculi are MA.O.A1; which one models metaphysical necessity is PL.O.A1.2.
- PL.O.A1.3 Causation, persistence and identity. Causation answers: regularity, counterfactual dependence, process, interventionist (Woodward 2003). Persistence answers: endurance, perdurance (Lewis 1986).
- PL.O.A1.3 identity. Leibniz's law (the indiscernibility of identicals) holds for numerical identity in extensional contexts. It fails as a test inside belief and other opaque contexts. Trouble case: the statue and the clay.
- PL.O.A1.3 personal identity is the identity question asked of persons. The psychology of the self is MS.
- PL.O.A1.3 free will. Whether responsibility is compatible with determinism. Frankfurt cases (1969) (UNVERIFIED) target the principle of alternate possibilities. The empirical psychology of choice stays in MS (audit error 5).
- PL.O.A1.3 holds al-Ghazālī's occasionalist critique of necessary causal connection (audit item; date UNVERIFIED).
- PL.O.A1.4 Realism and anti-realism. Whether the world has a structure apart from our concepts. Putnam's model-theoretic argument assumes that reference is fixed only by total theory plus operational constraints (UNVERIFIED wording). Underdetermination of theory by evidence is cited here from A5.1.

#### PL.O.A2 Knowledge and mind

SCQA. Situation: epistemology asks when belief is knowledge; philosophy of mind asks what a mental state is. Complication: they share a subject and not a question. The epistemology of religion was unplaced in v1. Question: which questions does this claim-type contain?

Key line. Logic inductive · plural noun questions about knowledge and mind · dimension entitlement, its source, or the nature of the mental · order structural.

1. PL.O.A2.1 Knowledge
2. PL.O.A2.2 Sources of entitlement
3. PL.O.A2.3 Mind

Named objects.
- PL.O.A2.1 Knowledge. The analysis is a deductive group: truth, belief, and a further condition. Truth: a known falsehood is not knowledge.
- PL.O.A2.1 belief condition. CONTESTED: Radford's unconfident examinee (1966) denies that knowledge entails belief. Williamson's knowledge-first view (2000) denies that knowledge is analysable at all.
- PL.O.A2.1 further condition. Justified true belief is the standard post-Gettier framing; whether it was ever the received view is disputed (Dutant 2015).
- PL.O.A2.1 Gettier cases (1963) assume two things. A false belief can be justified. Justification is closed under deduction the subject performs. Given both, justified true belief is not sufficient for knowledge.
- PL.O.A2.1 replies to Gettier: one inductive group of five. No false lemmas (Clark 1963); causal connection (Goldman 1967); reliability; tracking (Nozick 1981); virtue (Sosa). This list replaces v1's two lists (audit error 6).
- PL.O.A2.1 scepticism is not a member of that group. It claims the further condition is never met for a stated domain.
- PL.O.A2.1 structure of justification: foundationalism, coherentism, externalism, as answers to the regress.
- PL.O.A2.2 Sources of entitlement. Perception, memory, testimony, reason, introspection. Rationalism and empiricism are answers about source. A priori is a way of being entitled, not a place.
- PL.O.A2.2 social epistemology: testimony and peer disagreement. Disagreement alone does not prescribe a unique revision.
- PL.O.A2.2 epistemology of religion (audit error 19): religious experience, reformed epistemology (Plantinga), Hume on testimony to miracles. Boundary: the argument that scriptural authority is rational to accept is PL.O.A2.2. A claim resting on that authority is RE.O·T.
- PL.O.A2.3 Mind. Identity theory, functionalism, eliminativism, dualism, as answers. Intentionality.
- PL.O.A2.3 the hard problem (Chalmers 1995): why physical processing is accompanied by experience. Stated as a problem, not as a proof of dualism. MS defers here.
- PL.O.A2.3 Searle's Chinese room (1980) assumes that running a program is purely syntactic manipulation. It concludes that syntax does not suffice for semantics.
- PL.O.A2.3 the extended mind (Clark and Chalmers 1998); machine consciousness; perception (veridical and not); action as doing for a reason. Action sits here when the question is the mental cause, and in A4 when it is the reason's worth.
- PL.O.A2.3 Yogācāra read as a theory of mind and appearance (audit item; CONTESTED, alternative A1.4).

#### PL.O.A3 Meaning and inference

SCQA. Situation: we say things, and some sayings follow from others. Complication: language and logic are separate departments, and MA also owns logic. v1 let PL and MA both claim consequence. Question: which questions does this claim-type contain, and where is the line with MA and LN?

Key line. Logic inductive · plural noun questions about saying and following · dimension significance or consequence · order structural.

1. PL.O.A3.1 Meaning
2. PL.O.A3.2 Consequence

Named objects.
- PL.O.A3.1 Meaning. Reference and sense (Frege's puzzle: "a = a" is trivial, "a = b" can be informative). Use as meaning. Speech acts. Literal and implicated content as concepts.
- PL.O.A3.1 truth-conditional semantics (Davidson 1967): to know a sentence's meaning is to know under which conditions it is true. It assumes a recursive truth theory for the language.
- PL.O.A3.1 names: descriptivism against direct reference. Kripke's modal argument (1980) assumes proper names are rigid designators across possible worlds.
- PL.O.A3.1 compositionality (Frege's principle) as a concept. Its empirical testing in a named language is LN.O.A3.
- PL.O.A3.1 Grice's cooperative principle as a concept of conversational rationality. Implicature patterns in a language are LN.O.A3.
- PL.O.A3.2 Consequence. Deduction preserves truth if valid. Induction does not, and can still be reasonable. Abduction infers to an explanation. Validity is form; soundness adds true premises.
- PL.O.A3.2 explosion: from a contradiction anything follows, in classical and intuitionistic logic. Relevance and paraconsistent logics reject it.
- PL.O.A3.2 holds which logic is correct, logical pluralism, and the significance of the liar and sorites paradoxes.
- PL.O.A3.2 uses MA.O.A1 for metatheorems. Gödel's first incompleteness theorem holds for a consistent, effectively axiomatised theory that interprets elementary arithmetic. What it shows about mind or truth is PL.
- PL.O.A3.2 uses CS.O.A1.5 for Curry–Howard. It pairs intuitionistic natural deduction with the simply typed lambda calculus. Classical logic needs control operators (Griffin 1990).
- PL.O.A3.2 holds Mohist logic and Mīmāṃsā's hermeneutics of injunction as reconstructed consequence and meaning claims (audit items; Mīmāṃsā CONTESTED, alternative A3.1).
- PL.O.A3.2 decision under uncertainty, when it is a consequence relation on credences. When the utilities are values, it is A4.2.
- Fallacies (affirming the consequent, equivocation, ad hominem) are a checked list in the heuristics register, not a system.

#### PL.O.A4 Value

SCQA. Situation: some acts, characters, works and institutions are better than others, and we argue about which. Complication: ethics, politics, law and aesthetics are departments. PO and LA also claim legitimacy and the concept of law. Applied ethics and gender, race and sexuality had no member in v1. Question: which value questions are distinct?

Key line. Logic inductive · plural noun objects of value · dimension what is being valued · order structural (the value claim itself, the act, the institution, the work).

1. PL.O.A4.1 Metaethics
2. PL.O.A4.2 Normative and applied ethics
3. PL.O.A4.3 Political and legal philosophy
4. PL.O.A4.4 Aesthetics

Named objects.
- PL.O.A4.1 Metaethics. Cognitivism, expressivism, realism. The Frege–Geach problem: expressivism must explain how "lying is wrong" keeps its meaning inside a conditional.
- PL.O.A4.1 Moore's open-question argument (1903, §13). It assumes a correct analysis of "good" would make "is N good?" closed to a competent speaker. Moore offered it for non-naturalism. Main reply: synthetic property identity (Putnam; Boyd).
- PL.O.A4.2 Normative ethics, worked descent kept from v1. Key line inductive · answer-families · dimension the bearer of value · order outcome, constraint, character: consequentialism, deontology, virtue ethics.
- PL.O.A4.2 a hybrid theory is not a fourth family until it says which bearer decides a conflict.
- PL.O.A4.2 Kant's categorical imperative is one deontic construction; rights theories are another. A constraint that forbids a small harm and permits a disaster needs a threshold or an admission.
- PL.O.A4.2 virtue rightness criterion (Hursthouse 1999): an act is right if a virtuous agent would characteristically do it in the circumstances.
- PL.O.A4.2 applied ethics (audit error 15): life, war, animals, environment, technology, professional ethics, AI moral status and alignment ethics. A case is not a new theory.
- PL.O.A4.2 owns the normative claim in professional and AI ethics. The professional duty as a legal duty is LA; the constraint on a specification is CS or EN.
- PL.O.A4.3 Political and legal philosophy. Legitimacy of coercion, political obligation, liberty, equality, authority, justice. Owner for PO.O.A1 and LA.O.A1, which cite it.
- PL.O.A4.3 Rawls (1971): parties behind a veil of ignorance, mutually disinterested and choosing under uncertainty, would choose the two principles. The maximin step assumes they cannot assign probabilities.
- PL.O.A4.3 Nozick (1974): entitlement theory, assuming self-ownership and a just initial acquisition. Berlin (1958): negative and positive liberty. Cohen on egalitarian justice (UNVERIFIED work date).
- PL.O.A4.3 the concept of law: positivism (Hart), interpretivism (Dworkin), natural law (Finnis 1980). CONTESTED: "what makes a norm law" is an entitlement question and fits A5 under the restated rule. Kept at A4.3 to keep PO and LA citing one cell.
- PL.O.A4.3 philosophy of gender, race and sexuality, when the claim is about a social category and a harm. When the claim is about what the category picks out, it is A1.1.
- PL.O.A4.3 Dussel's philosophy of liberation and critical theory's emancipation claim, as reconstructed political claims.
- PL.O.A4.3 African personhood debate: Menkiti against Gyekye on whether personhood is acquired in community (audit item; CONTESTED, alternative A1.3).
- PL.O.A4.4 Aesthetics. What a work is and why it merits attention. Intention, form, reception as answers. Hume's standard of taste (1757). Owner for AR, which applies criteria to named works.
- PL.O.A4.4 the ontology of a musical work (type and token) is A1.1, as the AR prototype already cites.

#### PL.O.A5 Standing of the sciences and mathematics

SCQA. Situation: the sciences and mathematics assert laws, explanations and theorems. Philosophy asks what those assertions are entitled to. Complication: v1 called this cell "special science" but included mathematics, said "six fields" and named three, and had no key line. Cognitive science, computing and information, probability and chemistry were missing. Question: which entitlement questions does this cell hold, without absorbing any science?

Key line. Logic inductive · plural noun sciences asked about · dimension what kind of warrant the science owes · order from the general to the formal to the human.

1. PL.O.A5.1 General philosophy of science
2. PL.O.A5.2 Natural sciences
3. PL.O.A5.3 Formal sciences
4. PL.O.A5.4 Mind and social sciences

Named objects.
- PL.O.A5.1 General philosophy of science. Explanation, confirmation, law, model, reduction. A model is not the system.
- PL.O.A5.1 underdetermination: more than one theory can fit the same finite evidence. It is a limit, not a licence to ignore evidence.
- PL.O.A5.1 holds logical empiricism and naturalism as answers about entitlement (see PL.B.C5).
- PL.O.A5.2 Natural sciences. Philosophy of physics: time, probability in physics, the measurement problem. The formalism stays in PH.
- PL.O.A5.2 philosophy of chemistry (audit error 10): reduction of chemistry to physics; the chemical bond as a kind. The bond itself is CH.
- PL.O.A5.2 philosophy of biology: function, species, units of selection. Population genetics stays in BI.
- PL.O.A5.3 Formal sciences. Philosophy of mathematics: platonism, nominalism, structuralism, as answers to what a theorem is about. The proof stays in MA.
- PL.O.A5.3 philosophy of probability: frequency, propensity, subjective and logical interpretations. The measure is MA.O.A5; the agent model is EC.O.A1. CONTESTED: alternative home A3.2.
- PL.O.A5.3 philosophy of computing and information: what computation is, and whether information is physical. Cites CS.
- PL.O.A5.4 Mind and social sciences. Philosophy of cognitive science: representation and explanation in cognitive models. Cites MS.
- PL.O.A5.4 philosophy of social science: whether reasons are causes, and whether a social kind is like a natural kind. The data stay in MS, EC or PO.
- PL.O.A5.4 philosophy of history: whether historical explanation is causal or interpretive. Cites HI; HI's historiography bridge cites this cell.
- Boundary rule kept from v1. A philosophical claim that contradicts a settled measurement has left philosophy. It then owes the owner's empirical standard.

### 5. Face M — Methods

Key line. Logic inductive · plural noun roles · dimension what the practice does to a question · order structural.

| role ID | role | maps to M1–M7 | example |
|---|---|---|---|
| PL.M.B1 | Argument | M2 Derive | Premises, inference, conclusion; objection and reply. A thought experiment is M2 on a case, and shows only what the case assumes. |
| PL.M.B2 | Analysis | M1 Represent | Necessary and sufficient conditions; family resemblance; Carnap's explication (UNVERIFIED date). Ordinary language is evidence about a concept, not a veto. |
| PL.M.B3 | Formalization | M1 Represent (with M2 inside the calculus) | Modal logic for A1.2; deontic logic for A4 with its paradoxes labelled. The calculus as an object is MA.O.A1. Computational simulation of norms is M5; CONTESTED whether it is a sixth role. |
| PL.M.B4 | Description | M3 Observe | Phenomenological description, bracketing existence (Husserl's epoché). Experimental philosophy surveys of intuitions are placed here as evidence about concepts. CONTESTED: first-person description as M3. |
| PL.M.B5 | Reconstruction and interpretation | M6 Compare and reconstruct | State the position, the problem it answered, the objection it missed. Hermeneutics moves here from v1's B4. Charity is a method rule, not agreement. |

M7 Verify. Peer review in philosophy is shared refereeing, not a PL role. It is stated, not counted.

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)

PL.M.H. Appeal to intuition. Appeal to a canon. "Category mistake" as a diagnosis that still needs an argument. A definition that settles a dispute by vocabulary is a stipulation and must say so. The named-fallacies list. Burden of proof as a dialectical convention.

### 6. Face W — Warrants

Every sub-cell has its own row. A-level rows state the cell default. W13 is never a warrant in PL; a claim warranted by tradition-internal authority moves to RE.O·T.

| cell ID | warrant W# | standard of acceptance | typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|
| PL.O.A1 | W9 | Argument plus replies; theoretical virtues argued | Counterexample; conflict with an owner science |
| PL.O.A1.1 | W9; W1 for formal ontology | Parsimony and explanatory power argued; replies given | Ideological cost; counterexample; contradicting PH or BI results |
| PL.O.A1.2 | W9, W1 | Valid in a stated modal system; intuitions reconciled | Counter-model; wrong necessity type |
| PL.O.A1.3 | W9 | Fits paradigm cases; survives trouble cases | Pre-emption and overdetermination; statue and clay; Frankfurt cases; relativity against presentism |
| PL.O.A1.4 | W9 | Argument plus replies | Underdetermination; model-theoretic arguments |
| PL.O.A2 | W9 | No accepted counterexample | Case counterexamples |
| PL.O.A2.1 | W9 (case method) | No accepted counterexample to the analysis | Gettier-type cases; sceptical scenarios; lottery cases |
| PL.O.A2.2 | W9 | Argument plus replies | Regress; testimony and disagreement cases |
| PL.O.A2.3 | W9, using MS results (W3, W4) as premises | Consistent with the owner's evidence | Empirical refutation; explanatory gap; multiple realisability |
| PL.O.A3 | W9, W1 | Fit to usage and to inferential practice | Paradox; counter-model |
| PL.O.A3.1 | W9; uses LN data (W4) | Accounts for usage data and inference | Frege's puzzle; Kripke's modal and epistemic arguments |
| PL.O.A3.2 | W1 (in a stated system, uses MA.O.A1); W9 (which logic) | Proof in a stated system; fit to practice | Counter-model; liar; sorites; explosion |
| PL.O.A4 | W9 | Argument plus replies; reflective equilibrium | Counterexample intuitions |
| PL.O.A4.1 | W9 | Argument plus replies | Open-question argument; Frege–Geach; moral disagreement |
| PL.O.A4.2 | W9 (reflective equilibrium) | Coherence of principles and considered judgements | Demandingness; separateness of persons; counterexample cases |
| PL.O.A4.3 | W9; uses PO (W5) and LA (W10) facts | Argument plus replies; consistent with institutional facts | Feasibility; reasonable pluralism; counterexample regimes |
| PL.O.A4.4 | W9, W8 | Fits critical practice and works | Readymades; intention-versus-reception cases |
| PL.O.A5 | W9, W8 (reading the science's record) | Accurate to the science's practice | Misdescription of the science |
| PL.O.A5.1 | W9, W8 | Fits episodes in the record of science | Historical counter-episodes |
| PL.O.A5.2 | W9, W8; inherits PH, CH, BI warrants as premises | Follows the owner's evidence | Contradicting a settled measurement |
| PL.O.A5.3 | W9; uses MA (W1) and CS (W2, W11) | Consistent with the proofs and practice | Misstated theorem; missing hypothesis |
| PL.O.A5.4 | W9, W8; uses MS, EC, PO, HI warrants | Accurate to research practice | Misdescribed method; replication failure in the cited science |

### 7. Face B — Bridges

Key line. Logic inductive · plural noun tradition bridges · dimension the claim-type the community takes as first (A1–A5) · order the Face O order. Proposal, CONTESTED (R6): v1 keyed C4 by method and C5 by value; this re-keys all five by first claim-type (audit error 13). Experience-first is now a method trait (PL.M.B4) inside a bridge.

| bridge ID | named field | cell(s) | method subset | substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| PL.B.C1 | Being-first traditions | PL.O.A1 first; A2–A4 | B1, B5 | Plato; Aristotle; medieval Christian, Jewish and Islamic metaphysics (Avicenna on essence and existence); Advaita Vedānta; Madhyamaka (emptiness); Neo-Confucian li and qi; Heidegger; Māori philosophy read via whakapapa |
| PL.B.C2 | Knowledge-first traditions | PL.O.A2 first | B1, B2, B4 | Descartes; British empiricism; Kant; Nyāya pramāṇa theory (one home only, audit error 12); Husserlian phenomenology; Yogācāra |
| PL.B.C3 | Language-first traditions | PL.O.A3 first | B2, B3 | Analytic philosophy from Frege and Russell (formal and ordinary-language wings); Mohist logic; Mīmāṃsā |
| PL.B.C4 | Value-first traditions | PL.O.A4 first | B1, B5 | Confucian role ethics; Buddhist liberation-directed analysis (no-self serving the end of suffering); Stoic ethics (UNVERIFIED placement); critical theory; philosophy of liberation (Dussel); African personhood-in-community traditions |
| PL.B.C5 | Science-first traditions | PL.O.A5 first | B2, B3 | Logical empiricism; naturalism; pragmatism (CONTESTED, alternative C2) |

Contested placements in Face B.
- Hegel: CONTESTED. Placed C1 (being-first). Alternative C2 or C3, reading the system as a logic or theory of knowledge (Pippin 1989).
- Kant: placed C2. Alternative C1 for the claim about objects-for-us.
- Heidegger: placed C1; alternative C2 with phenomenology.
- Confucian thought moves from v1 C5 to C4. The RE prototype cites "PL.B.C5" for Confucianism as philosophy; that citation must change to PL.B.C4.
- Nyāya keeps language theory as a secondary trait, not a second home.

### 8. Assignment rules

1. A claim is placed by the sentence it makes, not by the department or century it comes from.
2. Logic as a claim about consequence is PL.O.A3.2. Calculi and metatheorems are MA.O.A1, cited as "uses MA.O.A1".
3. History of philosophy is reconstruction (PL.M.B5) of answers already placed on Face O. That a philosopher held a view at a date is HI.O.A1; whether the argument succeeds is PL.
4. Philosophy of X: entitlement to assert is A5; what X may enforce or ought to do is A4; what the X-object is, A1 or A2.
5. Philosophy of religion is PL. An argument from premises any reasoner may grant is PL.O.A1.1. Religious epistemology is PL.O.A2.2. The same conclusion argued from a creed is RE.O·T.
6. A moral claim argued from shared premises is PL.O.A4. One citing scripture, creed or school is RE·T. One reporting what a tradition holds is RE·S.
7. Normative legitimacy, obligation and justice are PL.O.A4.3. PO cites them; PO owns who holds power and how (PO).
8. The concept of law is PL.O.A4.3. Whether a norm is valid in a given legal system is LA (W10).
9. Causation in general is PL.O.A1.3. A legal causation test is LA; causal identification from data is EC (EC.B2 design), cited.
10. The hard problem and intentionality are PL.O.A2.3. Processing, measured, is MS.
11. Probability as measure is MA.O.A5; as an agent's choice model, EC.O.A1; its interpretation, PL.O.A5.3.
12. Curry–Howard is CS.O.A1.5; PL.O.A3.2 uses it, with the intuitionistic hypothesis stated.
13. General aesthetic value is PL.O.A4.4. A verdict on a named work is AR.
14. Meaning as a concept is PL.O.A3.1. Meaning in a particular language is LN.O.A3.
15. A tradition enters Face B only as a community of answers. Each non-Western school is named by the claim it makes, never filed as a regional bin.
16. A philosophical claim that contradicts a settled measurement owes that science's warrant, in that science's pyramid.
17. Metaphilosophy (what philosophy is) is this pyramid's master section, not a sixth claim-type.

## 2.15 HI — History

Standard v2 cited, not copied. Built up from prototype_history_language.md; its members are kept. Dates the prototype gave from memory are marked UNVERIFIED unless widely attested.

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** History states what happened in the human past, when, why, what changed, and what it meant. Its claims rest on records that survive.
**Complication.** Every other pyramid cites past events: the strategist's case, the economist's crisis, the mathematician's life. v1 had no owner for the dated fact, so each field certified its own.
**Question.** What partition places every claim about the human past once, so that other pyramids can cite one owner of the dated fact?
**Answer.** Place a claim by what it asserts about the past: occurrence, time, cause, change and continuity, or meaning and memory. Every cell owes W7, source criticism of a dated record.

### 2. Governing thought
History is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five assertions about the past: occurrence, time, cause, change and continuity, and meaning and memory.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **what the sentence asserts about the human past.** Order: from the single fact to its meaning.
1. Occurrence
2. Time
3. Cause
4. Change and continuity
5. Meaning and memory

A period, region or nation is a catalogue (substrate), not a cell. "Medieval Europe" indexes claims; it does not hold them.

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### HI.O.A1 Occurrence
**SCQA.** The base claim of history is that something happened. Records lie, decay and conflict. What makes "this happened" acceptable? Key line: the event, the actor, the place, the authenticity of the record.
- HI.O.A1.1 The event — the unit of record, separated from its interpretation (the fact–interpretation distinction, E. H. Carr, *What Is History?*, 1961).
- HI.O.A1.2 The actor — persons and groups; prosopography (collective biography).
- HI.O.A1.3 The place — located with EA tools; the claim of human presence is HI.
- HI.O.A1.4 Authenticity of the record — forgery and interpolation. Lorenzo Valla showed the Donation of Constantine to be a forgery (c. 1440) by philology.

#### HI.O.A2 Time
**SCQA.** A fact must be placed in time to be used. Records use different calendars and clocks. When did it happen? Key line: absolute date, relative order, synchronism, periodisation.
- HI.O.A2.1 Absolute date — calendars and eras: Julian, Gregorian (introduced 1582), AH, BCE/CE.
- HI.O.A2.2 Relative order — terminus post quem and ante quem.
- HI.O.A2.3 Synchronism — aligning independent records of the same moment.
- HI.O.A2.4 Periodisation — labels such as "the Renaissance" are conventions (W12), not discoveries.
- HI.O.A2.5 Scientific dating — radiocarbon and dendrochronology dates are measurements owned by EA and PH; HI uses them.

#### HI.O.A3 Cause
**SCQA.** History explains as well as records. Explanations of the past cannot be tested by re-running it. What makes a causal claim about the past acceptable? Key line: reasons of actors, conditions and structures, contingency, counterfactuals.
- HI.O.A3.1 Reasons of actors — Collingwood's re-enactment of thought (*The Idea of History*, 1946).
- HI.O.A3.2 Conditions and structures — necessary and sufficient conditions; structural causes.
- HI.O.A3.3 Contingency and chance.
- HI.O.A3.4 Counterfactual claims — they owe W7 for their premises and W9 for the inference. Unconstrained counterfactuals are a heuristic.
- HI.O.A3.5 Model-explained paths — when a growth path is explained by an economic model, the explanation is EC and HI supplies the record (cliometrics is a bridge).

#### HI.O.A4 Change and continuity
**SCQA.** Many claims concern spans, not moments. Key line: durations, processes, structures over time, comparison across places.
- HI.O.A4.1 Durations — Braudel's three: longue durée, conjoncture and événement (*La Méditerranée*, 1949).
- HI.O.A4.2 Processes — industrialisation, state formation. A demographic transition is cited from MS.
- HI.O.A4.3 Structures over time — institutions, economies, environments in human time.
- HI.O.A4.4 Comparison across places — comparative history; world and global history.

#### HI.O.A5 Meaning and memory
**SCQA.** The past meant things to those who lived it and to those who remember it. Key line: meaning to participants, collective memory, heritage, historiography.
- HI.O.A5.1 Meaning to participants — the history of mentalités.
- HI.O.A5.2 Collective memory — Halbwachs (1925); lieux de mémoire (Nora, 1984–92).
- HI.O.A5.3 Heritage and commemoration — monuments, museums. A heritage-law duty is LA.
- HI.O.A5.4 Historiography — what later historians made of the past. Schools of interpretation are answer-families, not cells.
- HI.O.A5.5 Oral history — a dated account owes W7 like any record. A tradition-internal account (whakapapa as authority) is IK under W13 and does not travel into A1 without W7.

### 5. Face M — Methods

| ID | Role | Shared role | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| HI.M.1 | Find and authenticate | M3 Observe | archives, palaeography, diplomatics |
| HI.M.2 | Date and order | M3 Observe | chronology, scientific dating (cited) |
| HI.M.3 | Reconstruct and interpret | M6 Compare and reconstruct | source criticism, hermeneutics |
| HI.M.4 | Compare and quantify | M6, M5 Compute | comparative and quantitative history |
| HI.M.5 | Narrate and edit | M1 Represent | narrative, critical editions |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
Read against the grain; ask who wrote it and why; beware hindsight; follow the money and the paper.

### 6. Face W — Warrants

| Cell | Primary | Second | Standard | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| HI.O.A1 | W7 | W4 (material or forensic evidence) | two independent authenticated sources, or one with no plausible rival | forgery, single source, interpolation |
| HI.O.A2 | W7 | W4 (dating, owned by EA or PH) | date consistent across independent clocks | calendar confusion, reused material |
| HI.O.A3 | W7 | W9 + W6 | mechanism shown in the record; rival causes considered | post hoc reasoning, hindsight, teleology |
| HI.O.A4 | W7 | W6 + W2 | series built from records; method stated | survivorship of records, anachronism |
| HI.O.A5 | W7 | W8 | interpretation traced to the record | presentism, projection |

W7 does not travel: a dated record fixes the occurrence, not a physical law.

### 7. Face B — Bridges

| ID | Field | Cells | Methods | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| HI.B.C1 | Political and military history | A1, A3 | M.1, M.3 | states, wars (ST owns strategy) |
| HI.B.C2 | Economic history and cliometrics | A4 | M.4 | economies (uses EC) |
| HI.B.C3 | Social and cultural history | A4, A5 | M.3 | societies |
| HI.B.C4 | History of science and mathematics | A1, A5 | M.1, M.3 | science (MSC 01 bridge; the theorem is MA) |
| HI.B.C5 | Archaeology | A1, A2 | M.1, M.2 | material remains (uses EA) |
| HI.B.C6 | Oral history | A1, A5.5 | M.1, M.3 | living testimony |
| HI.B.C7 | Heritage studies | A5.3 | M.3 | sites, collections |
| HI.B.C8 | New Zealand and Pacific history | A1–A5 | all | Aotearoa, Pacific (with IK) |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. The dated fact is history's. That an event occurred, and when, is HI; every other pyramid cites it.
2. The model-explained path is the model's (EC, PO, ST); HI supplies the record.
3. A mathematician's life is HI; the theorem is MA; MSC 01 is the bridge.
4. A physical date (radiocarbon) is a measurement in EA or PH; its use for a human event is HI.
5. A strategic lesson drawn from a case is ST; the case's facts are HI.
6. A tradition-internal account of the past is IK or RE under W13; it enters HI.O.A1 only by W7.
7. A legal rule in force at a date is LA; the history of that rule is HI.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused | Owner |
|---|---|
| the theorem, law or mechanism itself | MA, PH, BI and others |
| the strategic lesson | ST |
| economic explanation by model | EC |
| tradition-internal authority over the past | IK, RE (W13) |
| prehistory without human records (geologic time) | EA.O.A3 |

### 10. External crosswalk
**ANZSRC 43** (checked 9 Oct 2026 against the ABS file anzsrc2020_for.xlsx) has four groups:
- 4301 Archaeology → B.C5
- 4302 Heritage, archive and museum studies → B.C7 and A5.3 (archives → IS)
- 4303 Historical studies → A1–A5
- 4399 Other → not a cell

Coverage: 3 of 3 substantive groups land.
**LCC** C (auxiliary sciences) maps to Face M (HI.M.1–M.2). D, E and F are organised by region and period, a substrate index (outlines downloaded; subclass-level map not yet written).

### 11. Validation
Sizes: key line 5. Face O 5 cells + 23 sub-cells = 28. M roles 5. Heuristics 4. W rows 5. B bridges 8.
Key line, word for word: Occurrence. Time. Cause. Change and continuity. Meaning and memory.
CONTESTED: oral tradition as W7 or W13 (alternative: a sui generis warrant; Māori-led review required). Counterfactual history as a cell or a heuristic.

## 2.16 LN — Language

Standard v2 cited, not copied. Built up from prototype_history_language.md; its five members are kept.

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** Linguistics states how human languages are produced, built, understood, varied and changed. It is taught by levels: phonetics through pragmatics, plus sociolinguistics and historical linguistics.
**Complication.** v1 had no home for language. PL owned meaning as a concept, MS owned processing and CS owned language models. No pyramid owned the grammar of a language, its sound system or its history.
**Question.** What partition places every claim about language once, with stated borders to PL, MS, CS and AR?
**Answer.** Place a claim by the aspect of language it is about: signal, form, meaning, variation in use, or diversity.

### 2. Governing thought
Language is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five aspects of language: signal, form, meaning, variation in use, and diversity.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **the aspect of language the sentence is about.** Order: from the signal to the family of languages.
1. Signal
2. Form
3. Meaning
4. Variation in use
5. Diversity

A particular language is a catalogue entry on the same five cells. There is no "exotic languages" bucket.

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### LN.O.A1 Signal
**SCQA.** Language is carried by speech or sign. How is the signal produced, perceived and organised? Key line: phonetics, phonology, sign phonology.
- LN.O.A1.1 Phonetics — articulation, acoustics, perception. Source–filter theory of speech (Fant, 1960) cites PH acoustics.
- LN.O.A1.2 Phonology — the phoneme and the minimal pair; distinctive features (Chomsky and Halle, *The Sound Pattern of English*, 1968); Optimality Theory (Prince and Smolensky, 1993).
- LN.O.A1.3 Sign phonology — handshape, location and movement as parameters (Stokoe, 1960). Sign languages are full natural languages.
- LN.O.A1.4 Writing systems — the IPA is a convention (W12); orthography is not sound.

#### LN.O.A2 Form
**SCQA.** Signals combine into words and sentences by rules. How are they built? Key line: morphology, syntax, the lexicon.
- LN.O.A2.1 Morphology — morphemes; inflection versus derivation.
- LN.O.A2.2 Syntax — constituency and recursion; dependency versus phrase structure; agreement.
- LN.O.A2.3 The lexicon — word classes, argument structure.
- LN.O.A2.4 Formal grammars — the Chomsky hierarchy is MA.O.A1; LN uses it to class natural-language phenomena. Whether natural languages are mildly context-sensitive is CONTESTED.

#### LN.O.A3 Meaning
**SCQA.** Forms carry meaning, alone and in context. What do expressions mean? Key line: lexical semantics, compositional semantics, pragmatics.
- LN.O.A3.1 Lexical semantics — sense relations; Saussure's arbitrariness of the sign (*Cours*, 1916).
- LN.O.A3.2 Compositional semantics — Frege's principle (concept cited from PL); Montague grammar.
- LN.O.A3.3 Pragmatics — Grice's cooperative principle and implicature (concept PL, empirical patterns LN); presupposition; deixis; politeness.
- LN.O.A3.4 Grammaticalised meaning — e.g. te reo Māori pronouns distinguish dual from plural, and inclusive from exclusive.

#### LN.O.A4 Variation in use
**SCQA.** Every language varies by speaker, group and situation. How and why? Key line: social variation, register, contact and multilingualism, endangerment and revitalisation.
- LN.O.A4.1 Social variation — the sociolinguistic variable (Labov, 1966).
- LN.O.A4.2 Register and genre — situational variation; diglossia (Ferguson, 1959).
- LN.O.A4.3 Contact and multilingualism — code-switching; pidgins and creoles.
- LN.O.A4.4 Endangerment and revitalisation — language shift. Te reo Māori: kōhanga reo from 1982; the Māori Language Act 1987 made it an official language of New Zealand.

#### LN.O.A5 Diversity
**SCQA.** Languages change, descend from ancestors and differ in structure. Key line: change, descent and families, typology and universals.
- LN.O.A5.1 Change — regular sound change (the Neogrammarian hypothesis, 1870s); Grimm's law (1822); Verner's law (1875); grammaticalisation.
- LN.O.A5.2 Descent and families — the comparative method; Proto-Indo-European; Austronesian, which includes te reo Māori.
- LN.O.A5.3 Typology and universals — Greenberg's universals (1963), most of them statistical tendencies rather than exceptionless laws.
- LN.O.A5.4 Origin of language — OPEN.

### 5. Face M — Methods

| ID | Role | Shared role | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| LN.M.1 | Elicit and document | M3 Observe | fieldwork, recordings, grammars |
| LN.M.2 | Analyse a corpus | M3 Observe, M5 Compute | corpus counts |
| LN.M.3 | Experiment | M4 Intervene | perception and acceptability experiments |
| LN.M.4 | Compare and reconstruct | M6 Compare and reconstruct | the comparative method |
| LN.M.5 | Model formally and computationally | M1 Represent, M2 Derive | formal grammars, phylogenetic models |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
Glottochronology (CONTESTED, filed here as a heuristic); the native speaker's intuition as a first guess; look for the minimal pair.

### 6. Face W — Warrants

| Cell | Primary | Second | Standard | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| LN.O.A1 | W4 | W3 | measured contrast, replicated across speakers | recording artefacts, one speaker |
| LN.O.A2 | W4 | W1 | systematic judgements or corpus evidence | researcher's intuition as sole data |
| LN.O.A3 | W4 | W9 | truth-value and felicity judgements in controlled contexts | translation standing in for meaning |
| LN.O.A4 | W4 | W5 | stratified sample; observer's paradox addressed | convenience samples |
| LN.O.A5 | W6 | W7 | regular correspondences across many items | chance resemblance, borrowing |

| ID | Field | Cells | Methods | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|

| LN.B.C8 | Documentation and revitalisation | A4.4 | M.1 | endangered languages (with IK) |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. The system is linguistics; the process is mind (MS).
2. Meaning as a concept is PL; meaning in a language is LN.
3. Formal languages are MA; their use to class natural languages is LN.
4. Language models are CS; whether their outputs show a grammatical generalisation is LN.
5. The poem is AR; the metrical phonology it uses is LN.
6. A scripture's doctrinal sense is RE; its grammar is LN.
7. A word in te reo is LN; its tradition-internal meaning in tikanga is IK.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused | Owner |
|---|---|
| philosophy of language | PL |
| real-time processing | MS |
| language technology | CS |
| literary value | AR |
| doctrinal meaning | RE |
| tradition-internal meaning of Indigenous terms | IK |

**ANZSRC 47** (checked 9 Oct 2026 against the ABS file anzsrc2020_for.xlsx) has six groups:
- 4701 Communication and media studies → MS and IS (refused)
- 4702 Cultural studies → MS (refused)
- 4703 Language studies → A1–A5 by language (substrate)
- 4704 Linguistics → A1–A5
- 4705 Literary studies → AR (refused)
- 4799 Other → not a cell

Coverage: 2 of 5 substantive groups land in LN; 3 are refused to named owners.
**LCC P** subclasses P–PM are organised by language family, a catalogue.

Sizes: key line 5. Face O 5 cells + 20 sub-cells = 25. M roles 5. Heuristics 3. W rows 5. B bridges 8.
Key line, word for word: Signal. Form. Meaning. Variation in use. Diversity.
OPEN: origin of language (A5.4).
CONTESTED: the mild context-sensitivity of natural language; glottochronology.
