## 2.17 AR — Arts and literature

Phase 2 rebuild of the prototype (T/prototype_arts_religion.md, Part One) at Standard v2. The prototype's cells, rules and named objects are kept unless a repair is noted. Repairs made here: two-sides wording replaced by four faces; HI and LN no longer marked provisional (both now prototyped); LCC coverage count corrected (the prototype said 22 of 30; the listed landings give 27 of 30); ANZSRC 36 crosswalk added; every named object given a stable ID; one W row per Face O cell.

Owners cited: MA (maths_reconciled.md), PH, CH, EN, CS, MS, EC, LA, PO, PL, HI, LN, RE. IDs of pyramids still in rebuild are cited as the audits and prototypes give them. If a rebuild renumbers them, the "uses" edges must be updated.

### 1. SCQA

**Situation.** Libraries file the arts by medium and language: LCC M for music, N for fine arts, P for language and literature. Departments follow: literature, art history, musicology, theatre, film. **Complication.** The same kind of sentence recurs in each department under another name. "The second subject returns in the tonic" and "the volta falls at line nine" are one kind of claim. Medium-first schemes repeat the aspects in every medium. They also mix works, such as scores and novels, with claims about works. **Question.** Which single partition places every claim about a work of art exactly once, by what the claim is about? **Answer.** Five aspects of the work, from material to judgment, with the medium as a substrate and the discipline as a bridge.

### 2. Governing thought

Arts and literature is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges. Its objects are claims about five aspects of the work: medium and making, form, meaning, context and reception, and value.

### 3. Key line

- **Logic.** Inductive.
- **Plural noun.** aspects of the work.
- **Dimension.** What feature of the work the sentence is about.
- **Order.** Structural, from the material object outward to judgment.

1. Medium and making
2. Form
3. Meaning
4. Context and reception
5. Value

**MECE test.** Exclusive: a sentence about arrangement (A2) does not assert what the work represents (A3). A reception fact (A4) does not assert worth (A5). Exhaustive: a sentence naming none of the five is about the perceiver's mind (MS), the material's chemistry (CH), a structure's stability (EN), or the concept of art (PL). Each is refused by rule (section 9).

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### AR.O.A1 Medium and making

**SCQA.** *S.* Every work is made of something, by someone, and exists in an object, a performance or an edition. *C.* Technique, attribution and performance practice sit in different departments. *Q.* Which making claims are distinct? *A.* Three, by stage of production.

**Key line.** Inductive · plural noun: making claims · dimension: the stage of production the sentence concerns · order: chronological, material then maker then realization.
1. **AR.O.A1.1 Material and technique** claims what the work is made of and by what process.
2. **AR.O.A1.2 Agency and attribution** claims who made it, alone or in a workshop.
3. **AR.O.A1.3 Realization and text** claims how a work exists in instances: performance, staging, edition.

**Named objects.**
- **AR.O.A1.1.1 Buon fresco versus secco.** Pigment bound by lime as plaster sets, versus pigment applied to dry plaster with a binder.
- **AR.O.A1.1.2 Oil glazing** in fifteenth-century Netherlandish panels.
- **AR.O.A1.1.3 Lost-wax casting.** A wax model is invested, melted out and replaced by metal.
- **AR.O.A1.1.4 Pentimento.** A change of composition visible under the paint layer.
- **AR.O.A1.1.5 Dendrochronological date of a panel.** A felling date is a lower bound on the painting date. It holds only if the board's outer rings survive or sapwood is estimated. The method's chronology is EA (uses EA, UNVERIFIED cell id).
- **AR.O.A1.1.6 Whakairo technique.** Māori carving technique, as a making claim. See T/p2_ANZSRC45.md.
- **AR.O.A1.2.1 Workshop attribution grades.** "Rembrandt", "studio of", "circle of", "follower of".
- **AR.O.A1.2.2 Rembrandt Research Project** (1968–2014) as an attribution programme (UNVERIFIED dates; from the prototype's link, not rechecked).
- **AR.O.A1.2.3 Stylometric attribution.** An authorship claim supported by function-word frequencies. The model is CS's; the attribution is here.
- **AR.O.A1.2.4 Whakairo lineage of training.** Which carving school trained a carver.
- **AR.O.A1.3.1 Tuning and temperament of a performance.** Twelve-tone equal temperament divides the octave into twelve equal frequency ratios of 2^(1/12). The ratio fact uses MA; that a named instrument was so tuned is here.
- **AR.O.A1.3.2 Figured bass.** A notation the performer realizes in chords.
- **AR.O.A1.3.3 Labanotation record** (Laban, 1928) of a named choreography.
- **AR.O.A1.3.4 Stemma and copy-text.** Lachmann's stemma assumes descent by copying with shared errors. Greg's copy-text rationale (1950) chooses the text for accidentals. Each yields a claim about the authorial reading.
- **AR.O.A1.3.5 Historically informed performance claims.** Example: the extent of continuous vibrato in eighteenth-century string playing. CONTESTED; the alternative reads treatises as prescriptive, not descriptive.

#### AR.O.A2 Form

**SCQA.** *S.* Critics describe arrangement: meter, harmony, composition, plot. *C.* Some formal names pick out a shared code; others pick out one work's design. Mixing them confuses the norm with the instance. *Q.* Which formal claims are distinct? *A.* Two: system and work.

**Key line.** Inductive · plural noun: formal claims · dimension: whether the sentence concerns a shared system or one work's arrangement (binary, MECE by construction) · order: general to particular.
1. **AR.O.A2.1 Formal systems** claim the shared codes many works use.
2. **AR.O.A2.2 Work-level design** claims one work's arrangement and its departures from the system.

**Named objects.**
- **AR.O.A2.1.1 Meter and prosody.** Iambic pentameter; quantitative meter; the mora in Japanese verse.
- **AR.O.A2.1.2 Tonality.** A hierarchy of pitches around a tonic, with functional harmony. Twelve-tone serialism (Schoenberg, early 1920s) is an alternative system.
- **AR.O.A2.1.3 Raga and maqam** as melodic-modal systems.
- **AR.O.A2.1.4 Sonata form (textbook model).** Abstracted from existing works by Reicha (1826), Marx (1845) and Czerny (1848). Koch (1793) described it by modulation. It is an analytic model, not a rule composers followed.
- **AR.O.A2.1.5 Linear perspective.** A central projection onto a plane. It assumes one fixed eye, a planar picture surface and straight light paths. Under these, parallel lines not parallel to the plane meet at a vanishing point. The projective geometry uses MA; the pictorial system is here. Brunelleschi (c. 1415–20) and Alberti, *De pictura* (1435).
- **AR.O.A2.1.6 Focalization** (Genette, 1972): who sees versus who speaks. Free indirect discourse.
- **AR.O.A2.1.7 Fabula and syuzhet** (Russian Formalists): story order versus telling order.
- **AR.O.A2.1.8 Continuity editing and montage** in film.
- **AR.O.A2.1.9 Genre as formal definition.** The sonnet's fourteen lines and volta. Genre as history is AR.O.A4.3.
- **AR.O.A2.1.10 Style as shared formal traits.** "Baroque" as traits. CONTESTED; "Baroque" as dates is HI.O.A2.
- **AR.O.A2.2.1 The recapitulation of a named movement.**
- **AR.O.A2.2.2 The volta of a named sonnet.**
- **AR.O.A2.2.3 The Sydney Opera House as a design.** Its form; its stability is EN.

#### AR.O.A3 Meaning

**SCQA.** *S.* Works represent, symbolise, express and allude. *C.* Iconography, theme, tone and intertextuality are taught as rival theories. They answer one question by different routes. *Q.* By which routes does content attach to a work? *A.* Four, from most direct to most mediated.

**Key line.** Inductive · plural noun: routes of meaning · dimension: how content attaches to the work · order: structural, most direct to most mediated.
1. **AR.O.A3.1 Depiction and reference** claims what the work represents or denotes.
2. **AR.O.A3.2 Coded meaning** claims what it signifies by a convention: symbol, allegory, attribute, leitmotif.
3. **AR.O.A3.3 Expression** claims the attitude, feeling or tone the work presents.
4. **AR.O.A3.4 Intertextual meaning** claims content carried by relation to other works.

**Named objects.**
- **AR.O.A3.1.1 Subject and mimesis.** What a painting depicts; the story a ballet tells.
- **AR.O.A3.1.2 Program music.** Music tied to a stated extra-musical subject.
- **AR.O.A3.2.1 Iconographic type.** Saint Catherine identified by the wheel. Panofsky's levels (1939) are a method (AR.M.5).
- **AR.O.A3.2.2 Allegory and emblem.**
- **AR.O.A3.2.3 Leitmotif in Wagner** as an in-work code.
- **AR.O.A3.2.4 Affektenlehre** as conventional affects of Baroque figures. CONTESTED: code (A3.2) when conventional; expression (A3.3) when the claim is the felt quality.
- **AR.O.A3.3.1 Rasa** (Nāṭyaśāstra): the aesthetic flavour a drama presents.
- **AR.O.A3.3.2 Irony and the unreliable narrator** (Booth, 1961).
- **AR.O.A3.4.1 Intertextuality** (Kristeva, 1966; published 1969) as a theory of meaning by relation. The dated fact of a borrowing is AR.O.A4.3.
- **AR.O.A3.4.2 Allusion, parody, pastiche and musical quotation.**

#### AR.O.A4 Context and reception

**SCQA.** *S.* Works are commissioned, published, exhibited, performed, reviewed and taught. *C.* Patronage, book history, reception theory and canon studies look like four fields. They follow one work through its social life. *Q.* Which stages of that life are distinct? *A.* Four, in time order.

**Key line.** Inductive · plural noun: stages of the work's social life · dimension: the stage the sentence concerns · order: chronological, commission to institution.
1. **AR.O.A4.1 Production context** claims the conditions under which the work was commissioned and made.
2. **AR.O.A4.2 Circulation** claims how the work moved: publication, ownership, exhibition, performance history.
3. **AR.O.A4.3 Reception** claims how audiences and later makers took it up.
4. **AR.O.A4.4 Canon and institution** claims where the work sits in lists, collections and curricula.

**Named objects.**
- **AR.O.A4.1.1 Patronage contract.** Baxandall (1972) on contracts specifying pigments, such as ultramarine for the Virgin's robe.
- **AR.O.A4.2.1 Provenance.** The chain of ownership of a named object.
- **AR.O.A4.2.2 Publication history.** The serial novel; folio and quarto.
- **AR.O.A4.2.3 Premiere and performance history.** The 1913 Paris premiere of *The Rite of Spring*.
- **AR.O.A4.3.1 Horizon of expectations** (Jauss, 1967) and the implied reader (Iser, 1972).
- **AR.O.A4.3.2 Influence and borrowing as dated facts.** Joyce's use of the *Odyssey*. Bloom's anxiety of influence (1973) is a contested theory of it.
- **AR.O.A4.3.3 Distant reading** (Moretti, 2000). Counts over a corpus as reception evidence. The corpus model is CS's.
- **AR.O.A4.3.4 Genre as history.** The Gothic novel's emergence and audience.
- **AR.O.A4.4.1 The canon as a reading list.** Anthologies, syllabi, museum holdings. A count, not a verdict.
- **AR.O.A4.4.2 Critics' polls as data.** The *Sight and Sound* poll, decennial since 1952. The result is here; each ballot is AR.O.A5.2.

#### AR.O.A5 Value

**SCQA.** *S.* Critics judge works and performances and give reasons. *C.* Philosophy owns what merit is (PL.O.A4.4). The applied verdict on a named work has no other home. *Q.* Which applied verdicts are distinct? *A.* Three, by the object judged.

**Key line.** Inductive · plural noun: verdicts · dimension: the object judged · order: structural, one work, a set of works, a realization.
1. **AR.O.A5.1 Verdict on a work** claims how one named work stands on a stated criterion.
2. **AR.O.A5.2 Comparative and canon judgments** claim how works rank, and which deserve a place.
3. **AR.O.A5.3 Judgment of a realization** claims how a performance, staging, edition or restoration stands.

**Named objects.**
- **AR.O.A5.1.1 Eliot on *Hamlet*** ("Hamlet and His Problems", 1919): "most certainly an artistic failure".
- **AR.O.A5.1.2 Architecture criticism of a named building.**
- **AR.O.A5.1.3 Xie He's six principles applied to a named painting.** The principles as general criteria are PL.O.A4.4.
- **AR.O.A5.2.1 Canon arguments.** Bloom, *The Western Canon* (1994), and its critics.
- **AR.O.A5.2.2 A prize jury's reasons; a critic's poll ballot.**
- **AR.O.A5.3.1 Record review of a named performance.** Tempo and balance judgments.
- **AR.O.A5.3.2 Judgment of a restoration.** Whether the Sistine ceiling cleaning (1980–94) removed original secco work. CONTESTED.

### 5. Face M — Methods

Five roles. Order: from the work to its world, fix it, read it, examine it, make it, set it among others.

| Role ID | Role | Maps to | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| AR.M.1 | Description and notation | M1 Represent | Scansion; critical edition; catalogue raisonné; Schenkerian graph as notation; Labanotation; shot list |
| AR.M.2 | Close reading and formal analysis | M2 Derive | Close reading; motivic and harmonic analysis; Sonata Theory (Hepokoski and Darcy, 2006) applied to a movement |
| AR.M.3 | Technical and archival examination | M3 Observe | X-radiography; infrared reflectography; XRF; dendrochronology; analytical bibliography; watermarks; provenance research |
| AR.M.4 | Making and realization | M4 Intervene | Practice-as-research; historically informed performance; reconstruction staging; conservation treatment |
| AR.M.5 | Comparison and interpretation | M6 Compare and reconstruct | Comparative literature; connoisseurship; Panofsky's iconological method; theory-driven readings |

**Boundaries of the roles.** The edition's text decision is a claim (AR.O.A1.3); notation is the role. A reading not traceable to the record has left AR.M.2. An instrument's physics and chemistry are PH and CH; the dated finding is AR. A making result counts as a claim only with a documented record (W8) or a controlled comparison (W3); CONTESTED. A theory used in reading is an answer-family. Its general truth is owed to its home pyramid (MS, EC, PL).

**Shared roles borrowed, not counted.** M5 Compute: stylometry and corpus models are CS methods (uses CS). M7 Verify: authentication boards, peer review and collation checks are community practices of the bridges.

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)

- **AR.M.H1** Form follows function (Sullivan, 1896). A design maxim, not a law.
- **AR.M.H2** The rule of thirds. A composition habit, not a finding.
- **AR.M.H3** The golden section in the Parthenon or in Mozart. Mostly unmeasured; a heuristic or myth until measured.
- **AR.M.H4** Chekhov's gun. A dramaturgical economy rule.
- **AR.M.H5** Show, don't tell. A workshop rule.
- **AR.M.H6** "The test of time." Hume's argument (1757) is PL.O.A4.4; the slogan is a heuristic.
- **AR.M.H7** "The author is dead" (Barthes, 1967). The argument is PL; the slogan settles nothing.
- **AR.M.H8** The intentional fallacy (Wimsatt and Beardsley, 1946) used as a veto. The argument is PL; the veto is a heuristic.
- **AR.M.H9** Morellian details (ears, hands). A cue for AR.M.3 and AR.M.5, not a proof.

### 6. Face W — Warrants

Named objects share their sub-cell's row. This is stated once here and applies to every AR.O.Ax.y.z ID. A-level rows state the cell's dominant warrant; sub-cell rows govern placement. W13 has no AR row: a tradition's authority over sacred art is RE·T.

| Cell ID | Warrant | Standard of acceptance | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|
| AR.O.A1 Medium and making | W4, W7 | Instrumental and documentary evidence converge on the making | Sample or document not from the work |
| AR.O.A1.1 Material and technique | W4 (secondary W7) | Instrumental result with stated uncertainty, traced to a sampled location | Contaminated or unrepresentative sample; later retouching read as original |
| AR.O.A1.2 Agency and attribution | W7 (secondary W6, W4) | Documents and technical evidence converge on a hand or workshop | Forged document; workshop copy; connoisseurship uncorroborated |
| AR.O.A1.3 Realization and text | W7 (secondary W8) | Edition or practice traced to dated sources; collation stated | Single late source; treatise read as universal practice |
| AR.O.A2 Form | W12, W8 | System defined; instance located in the work | System imposed where the work departs |
| AR.O.A2.1 Formal systems | W12 (secondary W6) | Definition stated and its fit to a named corpus shown | Norm drawn from an unrepresentative corpus; anachronistic category |
| AR.O.A2.2 Work-level design | W8 (secondary W4) | Each formal claim locatable in the text, score or object | Wrong edition; claim not locatable |
| AR.O.A3 Meaning | W8, W6 | Reading cites features of the work and, for codes, a period corpus | Imported code; critic's mood as the work's |
| AR.O.A3.1 Depiction and reference | W8 (secondary W7) | Reading cites features and, where available, the program | Title or label read as content without check |
| AR.O.A3.2 Coded meaning | W6 (secondary W7) | The code is attested in a corpus of the period and place | Code imported from another period; isolated parallel |
| AR.O.A3.3 Expression | W8 (secondary W9) | Features cited; an argument links them to the expressive claim | Report of the critic's mood offered as the work's |
| AR.O.A3.4 Intertextual meaning | W8 (secondary W6, W7) | Both texts cited; the relation specified | Resemblance by common source; parallel by selection |
| AR.O.A4 Context and reception | W7 | Dated records of each stage | Gaps filled by assumption |
| AR.O.A4.1 Production context | W7 (secondary W8) | Dated documents of commission or making | Biography projected into the work without a record |
| AR.O.A4.2 Circulation | W7 (secondary W4) | Dated record of each transfer, edition or performance | Provenance gaps filled by assumption |
| AR.O.A4.3 Reception | W7 (secondary W4, W6) | Dated reviews, readerships or counts, with sampling stated | Elite reviews read as general reception |
| AR.O.A4.4 Canon and institution | W4 (secondary W7) | Counts over stated lists, collections or syllabi | Unstated sample; count read as merit |
| AR.O.A5 Value | W9 | Criterion stated and applied to the work's features | Unstated criterion |
| AR.O.A5.1 Verdict on a work | W9 (secondary W8) | Criterion stated; the work's features shown to meet or fail it | Unstated criterion; misdescribed work |
| AR.O.A5.2 Comparative and canon judgment | W9 (secondary W6) | Criterion stated and applied to every work compared | Criterion changed mid-comparison; survivor sample |
| AR.O.A5.3 Judgment of a realization | W9 (secondary W7) | Criterion and the realization's evidence stated | A realization judged against a later norm without saying so |

### 7. Face B — Bridges

Two families by defining role: scholarship (AR.M.1–3, AR.M.5) or practice (AR.M.4). Within each, one bridge per medium, in the order verbal, visual and built, sonic, performed and screen. The medium is the substrate.

| Bridge ID | Named field | Cell(s) | Method subset | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| AR.B.C1 | Literary studies (national and comparative literature, textual scholarship, book history, literary theory, folk literature) | AR.O.A1.3, A2, A3, A4, A5 | AR.M.1, 2, 3, 5 | Verbal works |
| AR.B.C2 | Art and architectural history (including technical art history and conservation science) | AR.O.A1–A5 | AR.M.1, 3, 5; conservation adds AR.M.4 | Visual and built works |
| AR.B.C3 | Musicology (historical musicology, music theory, ethnomusicology) | AR.O.A1.3, A2, A3, A4 | AR.M.1, 2, 3, 5 | Musical works and performances |
| AR.B.C4 | Performance and screen studies (theatre, dance, film, television, new media) | AR.O.A1.3, A2, A3, A4 | AR.M.1, 2, 5 | Performed and screen works |
| AR.B.C5 | Creative writing | AR.O.A1, A2 | AR.M.4 | Verbal works |
| AR.B.C6 | Studio art and architectural design | AR.O.A1.1, A2.2 | AR.M.4 | Visual and built works |
| AR.B.C7 | Composition and musical performance | AR.O.A1.3, A2 | AR.M.4 | Musical works |
| AR.B.C8 | Theatre-making, choreography and film-making | AR.O.A1.3, A2.2 | AR.M.4 | Performed and screen works |

**Notes.** Ethnomusicology: a claim about the music is AR; a claim about the society, with music as evidence, is MS.O.A5. Cultural studies is not a bridge here: its claims about shared meaning are MS.O.A5; its claims about works are AR. The structural engineering of a design is EN, not AR.B.C6.

### 8. Assignment rules

1. **The work is not a claim.** A score, novel, painting or film is a substrate. Only sentences about works are filed.
2. **Medium is a substrate.** Music, painting, verse and film name bridges, never Face O cells.
3. **Direction of evidence decides arts versus history.** If the past is evidence for the work, the sentence is AR. If the work is evidence for the past, it is HI. "The contract required ultramarine" is AR.O.A4.1. "Guild records show pigment prices rising" is HI or EC.
4. **Value divides with philosophy at generality.** What merit is, and which properties confer it on any work, is PL.O.A4.4. Applying a stated criterion to named works is AR.O.A5.
5. **Valued is not valuable.** That a work is canonical, sold or polled is AR.O.A4. That it ought to be is AR.O.A5.
6. **Response mechanism is mind.** How perceivers process works in general is MS.O.A1. That a named work was received in a named way is AR.O.A4.3.
7. **Structure stays in engineering.** Whether a shell or vault stands is EN.O.A1. Its form and meaning are AR.O.A2 and AR.O.A3.
8. **Material identity stays in chemistry.** That a pigment is lead-tin yellow type I is CH.O.A1. That this panel used it is AR.O.A1.1, which uses CH.O.A1.
9. **The language system is linguistics.** English stress phonology is LN.O.A1. The metrical scheme of a poem is AR.O.A2.1. A poem's syntax as a fact of the language is LN.O.A2.
10. **Markets, rights and rule are elsewhere.** Prices and auction effects are EC.O.A2. Copyright and moral rights are LA.O.A2.2. Censorship as an act of authority is PO; its effect on the work is AR.O.A4.2.
11. **Sacred art splits.** An icon's form and making are AR. Its veneration and theological status are RE.O.A3.1. The ruling of Nicaea II (787) is an RE·T sentence.
12. **Style labels split.** "Baroque" as shared formal traits is AR.O.A2.1. "Baroque" as a span of dates is HI.O.A2. CONTESTED.
13. **No miscellaneous bucket.** Non-Western forms are filed by aspect. Raga is AR.O.A2.1; rasa is AR.O.A3.3; a haka's performance is AR.O.A1.3.
14. **Built environment (gap claim absorbed).** Design to a stated use is EN. Its aesthetic verdict is AR.O.A5. Its site is EA; its price is EC.
15. **Cited results.** Equal-temperament ratios and projective geometry use MA (uses MA.O.A2, UNVERIFIED cell). Stylometric models use CS. Dendrochronological chronology uses EA.

## 2.18 RE — Religion and theology

Phase 2 rebuild of the prototype (T/prototype_arts_religion.md, Part Two) at Standard v2. The prototype's cells, two-mode rule, authority-order register and tradition matrix are kept. Repairs made here: "two sides" wording replaced by four faces; key line restated as one plural noun on one dimension; every named object given a stable ID; one W row per Face O cell, A-level and sub-cell; the six bridges given B-table rows; HI no longer marked provisional (now prototyped); philosophy of religion pointed at PL's rebuilt IDs (PL.O.A1.1, PL.O.A2.2); ANZSRC 50 crosswalk added; Māori claims cross-referenced to T/p2_ANZSRC45.md.

Owners cited: PL (p2_PL.md), MS (p2_MS.md), HI and LN (prototype_history_language.md), AR (p2_AR.md), LA, PO, EC (audit IDs), MA (maths_reconciled.md, not needed for any RE result). If a rebuild renumbers an owner, the "uses" edges must be updated.

**Two-mode rule (binding, from the prototype).** Every RE sentence carries one mode. **·S (study)** owes W6, W7 or W8. **·T (theology)** owes W13, with W9 or W8. The ID is cell plus mode: RE.O.A1.1·T. W13 appears only in ·T.
- **RE-1 Tag.** Every RE sentence carries ·S or ·T.
- **RE-2 Separate warrants.** W13 never warrants a ·S sentence. W6 or W7 alone never warrants a ·T sentence.
- **RE-3 Report is not assertion.** A ·S sentence may report what a tradition teaches. Reporting is not adopting.
- **RE-4 Premises cross one way, labelled.** A ·T sentence may cite a ·S result as a premise. It must name the authority that makes the premise bear on doctrine.
- **RE-5 One sentence, one mode.** A sentence needing both modes is two sentences.
- **RE-6 Methodological agnosticism.** ·S brackets the truth of the ultimate claim (Smart; Berger's "methodological atheism", 1967, UNVERIFIED date). A ·S sentence that denies a doctrine has become a PL sentence and owes W9.

### 1. SCQA

**Situation.** Universities treat religion in two ways. Religious studies describes and compares religions from outside any one of them. Theology argues within a tradition from its authorities. **Complication.** No other pyramid owns either mode. PL declines religion as practice; MS files religion as a sector. LCC divides Christianity by topic into five subclasses but other traditions by name. A "world religions" bucket appears where schemes run out. **Question.** Which single partition places every religious claim exactly once, without merging the warrant of study with the warrant of faith? **Answer.** Four objects of the claim, each in two modes, with the tradition as substrate and the discipline as bridge.

### 2. Governing thought

Religion and theology is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges. Its objects are claims about four objects of the claim: the ultimate and the human condition, authoritative text, practice, and community and institution.

### 3. Key line

- **Logic.** Inductive.
- **Plural noun.** objects of the claim.
- **Dimension.** What the sentence is about.
- **Order.** Structural, from the referent to its carriers: what is ultimate, the text that discloses it, the practice that answers it, the community that carries it.

1. The ultimate and the human condition
2. Authoritative text
3. Practice
4. Community and institution

**MECE test.** Exclusive: by the subject rule, each sentence has one subject. "Genesis 1 is a priestly composition" is about a text (A2). "God created from nothing" is about the ultimate (A1), even when its warrant is a text. Exhaustive: religious experience is A3.2 as discipline, or MS.O.A1 as mental process. Sacred place is A1.2 (status), A3.1 (use in rite) or A4.1 (governance). Ethics and religious law are A3.3. Oral text is inside A2, which prevents a bias toward written scripture.

**CONTESTED (order).** An anthropological order would run community first. The order is structural, not a ranking.

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### RE.O.A1 The ultimate and the human condition

**SCQA.** *S.* Traditions make claims about God, gods, Brahman, emptiness or atua, about the world's dependence, and about human ends. *C.* Theism, nondualism and Buddhist analysis look like rival departments. They answer three questions in common. *Q.* Which doctrinal questions are distinct? *A.* Three, by the relatum of the claim.

**Key line.** Inductive · plural noun: doctrinal questions · dimension: the relatum of the claim · order: structural, the ultimate, the world, the person.
1. **RE.O.A1.1 The ultimate itself** claims what is ultimate and what may be said of it.
2. **RE.O.A1.2 The world's relation to the ultimate** claims creation, emanation, cosmogony, providence, revelation and sacred status.
3. **RE.O.A1.3 The human end** claims the human condition and its goal: sin and salvation, bondage and liberation, death and after.

**Named objects.**
- **RE.O.A1.1.1 The Trinity**, defined at Nicaea (325) and Constantinople (381). As true: ·T. Its conciliar history: ·S.
- **RE.O.A1.1.2 Tawḥīd and the divine attributes.** Ashʿarī, Māturīdī and Muʿtazilī answers differ.
- **RE.O.A1.1.3 Createdness of the Qurʾan** and the *miḥna* (from 833, UNVERIFIED date). Muʿtazilīs held it created; Ashʿarīs uncreated.
- **RE.O.A1.1.4 Apophatic theology.** God is known by negation. Pseudo-Dionysius (c. 500); Maimonides' negative attributes.
- **RE.O.A1.1.5 Nirguṇa and saguṇa Brahman.** Advaita (Śaṅkara, c. 8th century) against Viśiṣṭādvaita (Rāmānuja).
- **RE.O.A1.1.6 Śūnyatā** (Nāgārjuna, c. 2nd century) as a Madhyamaka claim. Its argument alone, from shared premises, is PL.
- **RE.O.A1.1.7 Ein Sof** in Kabbalah.
- **RE.O.A1.1.8 Io as supreme being** in some Māori traditions (·S). CONTESTED: its pre-contact antiquity is disputed. See T/p2_ANZSRC45.md.
- **RE.O.A1.1.9 Otto's numinous** (*Das Heilige*, 1917, UNVERIFIED date) as a ·S theory of the sacred.
- **RE.O.A1.2.1 Creation ex nihilo.**
- **RE.O.A1.2.2 Whakapapa cosmogony** from Ranginui and Papatūānuku. Human descent from a named tupuna is HI.O.A1.
- **RE.O.A1.2.3 Tapu and noa** as statuses of persons, places and things.
- **RE.O.A1.3.1 Karma and saṃsāra; mokṣa; nirvāṇa.**
- **RE.O.A1.3.2 Grace and synergy.** Salvation by grace alone against cooperation of grace and will.
- **RE.O.A1.3.3 The beatific vision.**
- **RE.O.A1.0.1 The axial age** (Jaspers, 1949: roughly 8th to 3rd centuries BCE). A ·S comparative claim (W6) of a shift toward transcendence. CONTESTED: critics find no common denominator and no clean break. Alternative home: HI.O.A2 (periodisation). Filed at A-level because it spans A1.1–A1.3.

#### RE.O.A2 Authoritative text

The cell covers written and oral text alike.

**SCQA.** *S.* Traditions hold some texts as scripture or authoritative tradition; some are written, some recited. *C.* Canon studies, textual criticism and exegesis are separate fields. They follow one text through its life. *Q.* Which text claims are distinct? *A.* Three, by stage of the text's life.

**Key line.** Inductive · plural noun: text claims · dimension: the stage of the text's life · order: chronological, status, transmission, interpretation.
1. **RE.O.A2.1 Canon and status** claims which texts count as authoritative, and in what rank.
2. **RE.O.A2.2 Transmission and establishment** claims how the text was composed, transmitted and fixed.
3. **RE.O.A2.3 Interpretation** claims what the text means.

Order note: canon is listed first because rank is what makes a text an object here. CONTESTED: historically, composition precedes canon; the alternative order is A2.2, A2.1, A2.3.

**Named objects.**
- **RE.O.A2.1.1 Biblical canons.** The Tanakh; Protestant, Catholic (with deuterocanonical books) and Ethiopian Orthodox canons differ in extent.
- **RE.O.A2.1.2 Śruti and smṛti.** Heard and remembered texts in Hindu traditions.
- **RE.O.A2.1.3 Pāli Tipiṭaka**, written down in Sri Lanka c. 1st century BCE; the Chinese and Tibetan canons.
- **RE.O.A2.2.1 The ʿUthmānic codex** (c. 650) and the *qirāʾāt* (canonical readings).
- **RE.O.A2.2.2 Ḥadīth and *isnād* criticism.**
- **RE.O.A2.2.3 Dead Sea Scrolls** (found from 1947) as textual witnesses (·S).
- **RE.O.A2.2.4 Mishnah** (c. 200 CE) and the Jerusalem and Babylonian Talmuds.
- **RE.O.A2.2.5 Vedic oral transmission** by fixed recitation patterns.
- **RE.O.A2.2.6 Karakia and kōrero tuku iho** as oral text, transmitted in whare wānanga. Placement subject to Māori-led review (T/p2_ANZSRC45.md).
- **RE.O.A2.3.1 Four senses of scripture:** literal, allegorical, moral and anagogical. Origen gave the first three; Cassian added the anagogical. As a claim about how scripture means: ·T. Its use as a procedure is RE.M.B2.
- **RE.O.A2.3.2 Tafsīr *bi'l-maʾthūr* and *bi'l-raʾy*:** by transmitted report and by reasoned opinion.
- **RE.O.A2.3.3 Midrash and PaRDeS.**
- **RE.O.A2.3.4 Mīmāṃsā rules** of Vedic interpretation.

#### RE.O.A3 Practice

**SCQA.** *S.* Adherents worship, pray, fast, meditate, keep law and live by codes. *C.* Liturgics, spirituality, ethics and religious law are separate departments. *Q.* Which practice claims are distinct? *A.* Three, by whom the act addresses.

**Key line.** Inductive · plural noun: practice claims · dimension: whom the act addresses · order: structural, the sacred, the self, others.
1. **RE.O.A3.1 Worship and rite** claims acts addressed to the sacred.
2. **RE.O.A3.2 Discipline and formation** claims acts addressed to transforming the self.
3. **RE.O.A3.3 Conduct and law** claims acts addressed to others, and the norms that govern them.

The dimension is attested inside traditions. Fiqh divides *ʿibādāt* (worship) from *muʿāmalāt* (dealings). Jewish thought divides duties toward God from duties toward others. The pyramid uses the division as a dimension, not as an authority. An act addressed to two is filed by the claim's subject. Ramadan fasting as a duty toward God is A3.1; as self-formation it is A3.2. CONTESTED: the alternative files such acts once, under the tradition's own primary label.

**Named objects.**
- **RE.O.A3.1.1 Eucharist and sacrament.**
- **RE.O.A3.1.2 Ṣalāt and ḥajj.**
- **RE.O.A3.1.3 Pūjā and śrauta ritual.**
- **RE.O.A3.1.4 Karakia in performance; pōwhiri; tangihanga.** Subject to Māori-led review.
- **RE.O.A3.1.5 Rites of passage** (van Gennep, 1909, UNVERIFIED date): separation, transition, incorporation. A ·S comparative claim.
- **RE.O.A3.1.6 Veneration of icons** and the ruling of Nicaea II (787). The icon's form and making are AR.O.A1 and AR.O.A2.
- **RE.O.A3.2.1 Meditation** (*śamatha*, *vipaśyanā*); yoga; dhikr. Its neural correlates are MS.O.A2.
- **RE.O.A3.2.2 The Rule of Benedict** (c. 530); musar.
- **RE.O.A3.2.3 Bhakti** as devotional discipline.
- **RE.O.A3.3.1 Fiqh rulings in *muʿāmalāt*.**
- **RE.O.A3.3.2 Halakha** in the Shulchan Aruch (printed 1565, UNVERIFIED date); dharmaśāstra.
- **RE.O.A3.3.3 Natural law in Aquinas** (*Summa* I–II q. 94) as moral theology (·T). The same argument from shared premises is PL.O.A4.
- **RE.O.A3.3.4 Kashrut and halal.** Filed by subject: as worship A3.1, as conduct A3.3.
- **RE.O.A3.3.5 Tikanga as correct practice** before atua and people; manaakitanga as obligation to guests. Tikanga as part of NZ law is LA.O.A1 (*Ellis v R* [2022] NZSC 114).

#### RE.O.A4 Community and institution

**SCQA.** *S.* Religions are carried by communities with offices, members and histories. *C.* Ecclesiology, church history and sociology of religion overlap. The tradition's own order differs from the general sociology of organizations. *Q.* Which community claims are distinct? *A.* Three, by what holds the community together.

**Key line.** Inductive · plural noun: community claims · dimension: what holds the community together · order: structural, authority, boundary, continuity.
1. **RE.O.A4.1 Authority and office** claims who may teach, rule, ordain or decide.
2. **RE.O.A4.2 Membership and boundary** claims who belongs, and what puts a member or teaching outside.
3. **RE.O.A4.3 Continuity and division** claims succession, lineage, reform and schism.

**Named objects.**
- **RE.O.A4.1.1 Papacy and councils.**
- **RE.O.A4.1.2 The ʿulamāʾ and the four Sunni *madhhabs*; the Twelver imamate.**
- **RE.O.A4.1.3 The rabbinate and the *posek*.**
- **RE.O.A4.1.4 Saṅgha and *vinaya*** as the monastic community's rule. Its membership rules are filed at A4.2 by subject.
- **RE.O.A4.1.5 Tohunga and kaumātua; marae *kawa***, which varies by iwi and hapū. Subject to Māori-led review.
- **RE.O.A4.2.1 Excommunication, *ḥerem*, *takfīr*.**
- **RE.O.A4.2.2 Orthodoxy and heresy.** In ·S, insider labels are marked as reported.
- **RE.O.A4.3.1 Apostolic succession.**
- **RE.O.A4.3.2 Guru-śiṣya *paramparā*; Śaṅkara's *maṭhas*.**
- **RE.O.A4.3.3 The East–West schism (1054); the Reformation (from 1517); the Sunni–Shīʿa division after 632** (·S).
- **RE.O.A4.3.4 Ringatū** (Te Kooti, c. 1868, UNVERIFIED date) and **the Rātana Church** (1925, UNVERIFIED date) as Māori Christian communities.
- **RE.O.A4.3.5 The "gate of ijtihād closed" thesis**, rejected as history by Hallaq, "Was the Gate of Ijtihad Closed?" (*IJMES* 16, 1984) (·S).

### 5. Face M — Methods

Each role is used in both modes. The mode is set by the warrant the claim owes, not by the method.

| ID | Role | Shared role | ·S example | ·T example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| RE.M.B1 | Establish the text | M3 Observe, M6 | textual criticism, manuscript study | fixing the received text for use |
| RE.M.B2 | Interpret | M2 Derive, M6 | historical-critical exegesis | exegesis within the tradition's rules (four senses, tafsīr) |
| RE.M.B3 | Observe practice | M3 Observe | ethnography, surveys of belief | pastoral observation |
| RE.M.B4 | Compare | M6 Compare and reconstruct | comparative religion, typology | comparative theology |
| RE.M.B5 | Systematise and argue | M1 Represent, M2 Derive | theory of religion | systematic and dogmatic theology |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
Methodological agnosticism in ·S (bracket truth claims); the principle of charity toward the tradition; "nobody is a Hindu in general" (study the local form first); lex orandi, lex credendi (in ·T: worship shows belief).

### 6. Face W — Warrants

The two-mode rule: a ·S claim owes W6, W7 or W8 and never W13. A ·T claim owes W13, with W9 and W8, and its W13 does not travel outside the tradition. W13 never licenses a measurement, a dated fact in HI, or a legal duty in LA.

| Cell | ·S warrant | ·T warrant | Standard | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| RE.O.A1 | W6 + W8 | W13 + W9 | ·S: comparison traced to sources; ·T: consistency with creed and argument | ·S: essentialising; ·T: heresy by the tradition's own test |
| RE.O.A2 | W7 + W8 | W13 + W8 | ·S: source criticism; ·T: reception by the community | ·S: anachronism; ·T: rejected canon |
| RE.O.A3 | W4 + W8 | W13 + W9 | ·S: ethnography, survey; ·T: authority of the law school or rule | ·S: insider–outsider gap; ·T: conflicting authorities |
| RE.O.A4 | W7 + W4 | W13 | ·S: dated record, sociology; ·T: the tradition's ecclesiology | ·S: partisan sources; ·T: schism |

### 7. Face B — Bridges

| ID | Field | Cells | Methods | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| RE.B.C1 | Biblical studies | A2 | B1, B2 | Hebrew Bible, New Testament |
| RE.B.C2 | Qurʾanic and ḥadīth studies | A2 | B1, B2 | Qurʾan, ḥadīth |
| RE.B.C3 | Indology and Buddhist studies | A1–A3 | B1, B2, B4 | South and East Asian traditions |
| RE.B.C4 | Systematic theology (·T) | A1 | B5 | a named tradition |
| RE.B.C5 | Liturgical studies | A3.1 | B2, B3 | rites |
| RE.B.C6 | Anthropology and sociology of religion (·S) | A3, A4 | B3 | communities (uses MS) |
| RE.B.C7 | Comparative religion (·S) | A1–A4 | B4 | several traditions |
| RE.B.C8 | Church and religious history (·S) | A4.3 | B1 | the dated record (uses HI) |
| RE.B.C9 | Māori religious traditions | A1–A4 | B1, B3 | iwi, hapū (with IK; Māori-led review) |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. Place by the subject of the sentence (the ultimate, the text, the practice, the community), then by mode.
2. ·S and ·T are never merged. The same sentence, asserted as true within a tradition, is ·T. Reported as held, it is ·S.
3. An argument from premises any reasoner may grant (cosmological, ontological, problem of evil) is PL. The same argument from scripture is RE ·T.
4. The dated event of a tradition's past is HI, cited by RE ·S.
5. A civil duty arising from a religion is LA. A canon-internal duty is RE ·T.
6. The neural or psychological process of religious experience is MS. The discipline as practice is RE.O.A3.2.
7. A measurement offered as doctrine is refused. Its measurement goes to its science; the doctrine stays in ·T without the measurement's warrant.
8. Comparison does not inherit any canon's W13.
9. Māori and other Indigenous religious claims follow p2_ANZSRC45.md and need Māori-led review.
10. Pilot repair (claim 19). A dated event in a text's history (when the Mishnah was redacted) is HI.O.A1. RE.O.A2.2 keeps claims about the transmitted text as text: variants, recensions and readings.
11. Pilot repair (claim 46). Reporting what a theologian held is RE·S. Evaluating an argument whose premises any reasoner may grant is PL, even when a theologian made it.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused | Owner |
|---|---|
| philosophy of religion from shared premises | PL |
| psychology and neuroscience of religion | MS |
| dated events of religious history | HI |
| legal status of religion, religious freedom | LA |
| sacred art as making and form | AR |
| tradition-internal Indigenous knowledge outside religion | IK |

### 10. External crosswalk
**LCC class B** (Philosophy, psychology, religion): BL (religions, mythology, rationalism), BM (Judaism), BP (Islam, Bahaism, theosophy), BQ (Buddhism), BR–BX (Christianity, split across five subclasses by topic: BR, BS, BT, BV, BX). Each lands in A1–A4 as substrate. The asymmetry, with Christianity divided by topic and others by name, is refused: here every tradition is a substrate on the same four cells. The B–BJ philosophy and BF psychology subclasses are refused to PL and MS.
Coverage: 9 of 9 religion subclasses land (BL, BM, BP, BQ, BR, BS, BT, BV, BX); BF and B–BJ are refused.
**ANZSRC 50** Philosophy and religious studies: religious studies (5004) and theology (5005) land here, confirmed (checked 9 Oct 2026, ABS anzsrc2020_for.xlsx). 5099 Other is not a cell. Coverage: 2 of 5 substantive groups land in RE; 5001, 5002 and 5003 go to PL; applied ethics, history and philosophy of specific fields, and philosophy go to PL.

### 11. Validation
Sizes: key line 4 members. Face O 4 cells + 12 sub-cells (A1.1–A4.3), with named objects under each. M roles 5. Heuristics 4. W rows 4, each split ·S/·T. B bridges 9.
Key line, word for word: The ultimate and the human condition. Authoritative text. Practice. Community and institution.
Four members, not five. Comparison is a method (RE.M.B4), not an object: a comparative sentence is about one of the four objects.
CONTESTED: Io's pre-contact antiquity; the axial age; the order canon-before-composition; acts addressed to two recipients; the place of tikanga (RE, IK or LA).
OPEN: none at master level.

## 2.19 ED — Education

New field, added at the owner's direction. Standard v2 cited, not copied. Why it is here: the physics audit had nowhere to put physics education research, and MSC 97 treats mathematics as the object of a learning claim. Nothing owned the learning claim itself.

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** Education research states what people learn, under which arrangements, how it is measured, and how schools and systems organise it.
**Complication.** v1 scattered these claims. Physics education went to MS, MSC 97 sat in MA, and JEL A2 and ANZSRC 39 had no home.
**Question.** By what dimension can every claim about learning and teaching be placed once, with the subject matter left to its owner?
**Answer.** By the element of the educational arrangement the claim is about: aims, learners, teaching, assessment, or institutions.

### 2. Governing thought
Education is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five elements of an educational arrangement: aims and curricula, learners, teaching, assessment, and institutions and systems.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **the element of an educational arrangement.** Order: from purpose to system.
1. Aims and curricula
2. Learners
3. Teaching
4. Assessment
5. Institutions and systems

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### ED.O.A1 Aims and curricula
**SCQA.** Every arrangement serves a stated aim. What should be learned, in what order? Key line: aims, curriculum content, sequence and progression.
- ED.O.A1.1 Aims — the stated purposes of education. The normative argument for them is PL; the statement of an adopted aim is ED (W12).
- ED.O.A1.2 Curriculum content — what is taught. The truth of the content stays with its owner (a theorem is MA).
- ED.O.A1.3 Sequence and progression — learning progressions and prerequisites.
- ED.O.A1.4 National curricula — e.g. Te Whāriki (NZ early childhood) and Te Marautanga o Aotearoa (Māori-medium) as stated frameworks (W12).

#### ED.O.A2 Learners
**SCQA.** Learning happens in a learner with prior knowledge. What does the learner bring, and how do they change? Key line: prior knowledge and misconceptions, development, motivation, diversity and need.
- ED.O.A2.1 Prior knowledge and misconceptions — e.g. the impetus misconception in force and motion (physics education research). The cognitive mechanism is MS.
- ED.O.A2.2 Development — age-related readiness, cited from MS.
- ED.O.A2.3 Motivation and engagement.
- ED.O.A2.4 Diversity and learning need — inclusion, special education, gifted learners.

#### ED.O.A3 Teaching
**SCQA.** Teaching arrangements cause learning, or fail to. Which arrangements work, for whom? Key line: instruction, feedback, practice and retrieval, technology, culturally sustaining pedagogy.
- ED.O.A3.1 Instruction — explicit instruction versus discovery; worked examples help novices (an expertise-reversal effect is a stated defeater for experts).
- ED.O.A3.2 Feedback — effects vary widely by type; some feedback lowers performance (UNVERIFIED as a meta-analytic figure).
- ED.O.A3.3 Practice and retrieval — spacing and retrieval practice improve long-term retention. The memory mechanism is MS.
- ED.O.A3.4 Technology in teaching — effects depend on use, not presence.
- ED.O.A3.5 Culturally sustaining pedagogy — Kaupapa Māori education (kura kaupapa Māori) as an arrangement. The tradition-internal content is IK.

#### ED.O.A4 Assessment
**SCQA.** Learning is inferred from performance on instruments. What does a score mean? Key line: validity, reliability, formative assessment, high-stakes testing.
- ED.O.A4.1 Validity — an argument that score interpretations are supported (W9 with W4).
- ED.O.A4.2 Reliability — score consistency; the statistics are MA.O.A5 (psychometrics as bridge).
- ED.O.A4.3 Formative assessment — assessment used to change teaching.
- ED.O.A4.4 High-stakes testing and accountability — Campbell's law: the more a quantitative indicator drives decisions, the more it is subject to corruption pressures (stated as a tendency).

#### ED.O.A5 Institutions and systems
**SCQA.** Teaching happens inside schools and systems with rules. What do institutions do to learning? Key line: schools, school systems, higher education, equity and access.
- ED.O.A5.1 Schools — school effects, leadership, class size.
- ED.O.A5.2 School systems — international comparisons (PISA, TIMSS) are W4 within their sampling designs.
- ED.O.A5.3 Higher education — universities, credentials.
- ED.O.A5.4 Equity and access — gaps by group. The returns to schooling are EC; the law on schooling is LA.

### 5. Face M — Methods

| ID | Role | Shared role | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| ED.M.1 | Design curricula and instruments | M1 Represent | curriculum framework, test design |
| ED.M.2 | Observe classrooms | M3 Observe | observation protocols, ethnography |
| ED.M.3 | Trial | M4 Intervene | randomised trials of interventions |
| ED.M.4 | Measure and model | M5 Compute | psychometrics, value-added models |
| ED.M.5 | Synthesise evidence | M6 Compare and reconstruct | meta-analysis, systematic review |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
Start from what the learner already knows (Ausubel); "teach to the test" as a warning; design-based research cycles.

### 6. Face W — Warrants

| Cell | Warrant | Standard | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|
| ED.O.A1 | W12 + W9 | stated framework; argued rationale | contested aims |
| ED.O.A2 | W4 | validated diagnostic instrument | test-specific misconceptions |
| ED.O.A3 | W3 + W5 | trial or identified effect, with the learners stated | Hawthorne effects, implementation drift |
| ED.O.A4 | W4 + W9 | reliability and a validity argument | construct-irrelevant variance |
| ED.O.A5 | W5 + W4 | identified system effect; sampled data | selection into schools |

### 7. Face B — Bridges

| ID | Field | Cells | Methods | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ED.B.C1 | Subject education research (physics, maths) | A2.1, A3 | M.2, M.3 | a discipline (MSC 97; PER) |
| ED.B.C2 | Educational psychology | A2, A3.3 | M.3, M.4 | learners (uses MS) |
| ED.B.C3 | Curriculum studies | A1 | M.1 | curricula |
| ED.B.C4 | Psychometrics and assessment | A4 | M.4 | instruments (uses MA.O.A5) |
| ED.B.C5 | Economics of education | A5.4 | M.4 | systems (uses EC) |
| ED.B.C6 | Māori and Indigenous education | A1.4, A3.5 | all | kura, wānanga (with IK) |
| ED.B.C7 | Higher education studies | A5.3 | M.2, M.4 | universities |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. Place by the element of the arrangement.
2. The truth of what is taught stays with its owner. ED owns the learning claim.
3. The mind's mechanism is MS. Its educational use is ED.
4. The economic return is EC. The legal duty is LA. The political decision is PO.
5. MSC 97 is a bridge: the mathematics is MA; learning it is ED.
6. The learning line (Phase 2.1). A claim that learners changed under a stated arrangement (teaching, curriculum, assessment, institution) is ED. A claim about how learning or memory works in any mind, with no arrangement in it, is MS.O.A1.5. Test: delete the arrangement from the sentence. If the claim survives, it is MS; if it does not, it is ED. MS.O.A1.5.5 is retired and points here.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused | Owner |
|---|---|
| the content's truth | its owner pyramid |
| general cognition and development | MS |
| returns to education | EC |
| education law | LA |
| philosophy of education | PL |

### 10. External crosswalk
**ANZSRC 39** Education (checked 9 Oct 2026 against the ABS file anzsrc2020_for.xlsx) has five groups:
- 3901 Curriculum and pedagogy → A1, A3
- 3902 Education policy, sociology and philosophy → A5 (philosophy → PL)
- 3903 Education systems → A5
- 3904 Specialist studies in education → A2.4, A3.5
- 3999 Other → not a cell

Coverage: 4 of 4 substantive groups land.
**JEL A2** (teaching of economics) lands in B.C1.

### 11. Validation
Sizes: key line 5. Face O 5 cells + 21 sub-cells = 26. M roles 5. Heuristics 3. W rows 5. B bridges 7.
Key line, word for word: Aims and curricula. Learners. Teaching. Assessment. Institutions and systems.
CONTESTED: the size of feedback effects; ED vs MS for educational psychology.
OPEN: transfer of learning across domains.

|---|---|---|---|
| ED.O.A1 | Aims and curricula | O/A1 | — |
| ED.O.A1.1 | Aims | O/A1 | PL |
| ED.O.A1.2 | Curriculum content | O/A1 | MA, PH |
| ED.O.A1.3 | Sequence and progression | O/A1 | — |

## 2.20 DE — Design and the built environment

New field, added at the owner's direction. Standard v2 cited, not copied. Why it is here: ANZSRC 33 (Built environment and design) had no core home. Architecture is neither engineering's specification cell nor art's verdict.

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** Design fields (architecture, urban planning, landscape, industrial and graphic design) make claims about arrangements proposed for human use.
**Complication.** EN owns whether an artefact meets a specification. AR owns the verdict on a work. Nothing owned "this arrangement serves this use in this place".
**Question.** By what dimension can each design claim be placed once?
**Answer.** By the aspect of a designed arrangement the claim is about: its use, its form, its performance in use, its place, or its life over time.

### 2. Governing thought
Design is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five aspects of a designed arrangement: use and need, form, performance in use, place and settlement, and life of the built thing.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **the aspect of a designed arrangement for human use.** Order: from the need to the life of the result.
1. Use and need
2. Form
3. Performance in use
4. Place and settlement
5. Life of the built thing

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### DE.O.A1 Use and need
**SCQA.** Design starts from people's use. What need does the arrangement serve? Key line: users and activities, the brief, accessibility.
- DE.O.A1.1 Users and activities — who will use it and how. Human factors cite MS.
- DE.O.A1.2 The brief — the stated programme of needs (W12 once agreed).
- DE.O.A1.3 Accessibility and universal design — designing for the widest range of users.

#### DE.O.A2 Form
**SCQA.** Needs are answered by spatial and visual arrangements. What form, and why? Key line: spatial organisation, typology, visual and material language.
- DE.O.A2.1 Spatial organisation — plan, section, circulation.
- DE.O.A2.2 Typology — building and product types.
- DE.O.A2.3 Visual and material language — composition, colour, materials. An aesthetic verdict with a stated criterion is AR.O.A5.

#### DE.O.A3 Performance in use
**SCQA.** A design is judged by how it works for its users. Key line: comfort and environment, wayfinding and usability, safety in use, post-occupancy evaluation.
- DE.O.A3.1 Comfort and indoor environment — thermal, acoustic, daylight. The physics is PH; structural adequacy is EN.
- DE.O.A3.2 Wayfinding and usability.
- DE.O.A3.3 Safety in use — building codes are W12; their legal force is LA.
- DE.O.A3.4 Post-occupancy evaluation — measured performance after use begins (W4).

#### DE.O.A4 Place and settlement
**SCQA.** Designs sit in places and form settlements. Key line: site, urban form, landscape, planning.
- DE.O.A4.1 Site — the physical site claim uses EA.
- DE.O.A4.2 Urban form — density, street networks, mixed use.
- DE.O.A4.3 Landscape architecture.
- DE.O.A4.4 Planning — zoning and land use. The legal instrument is LA; the political decision is PO.

#### DE.O.A5 Life of the built thing
**SCQA.** Built things age, are kept, adapted and removed. Key line: maintenance and adaptation, sustainability, heritage conservation.
- DE.O.A5.1 Maintenance and adaptive reuse.
- DE.O.A5.2 Sustainability — embodied and operational carbon. The environmental impact claim is EV.
- DE.O.A5.3 Conservation of built heritage — the heritage meaning is HI.O.A5.3.

### 5. Face M — Methods

| ID | Role | Shared role | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| DE.M.1 | Draw and model | M1 Represent | drawings, BIM, prototypes |
| DE.M.2 | Study users and sites | M3 Observe | site analysis, user research |
| DE.M.3 | Propose and iterate | M4 Intervene | design studio, prototyping |
| DE.M.4 | Simulate performance | M5 Compute | daylight and energy simulation |
| DE.M.5 | Review and evaluate | M7 Verify | design review, code compliance, POE |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
Form follows function (CONTESTED as a rule); pattern languages (Alexander); "less is more"; precedent study.

### 6. Face W — Warrants

| Cell | Warrant | Standard | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|
| DE.O.A1 | W4 + W12 | user research; agreed brief | unrepresented users |
| DE.O.A2 | W9 + W6 | argued rationale; precedent comparison | taste presented as necessity |
| DE.O.A3 | W4 + W11 | measured performance; compliance with code | simulated vs actual use |
| DE.O.A4 | W4 + W6 | site data; comparative urban studies | context dependence |
| DE.O.A5 | W4 + W2 | life-cycle assessment with stated boundary | boundary choice |

### 7. Face B — Bridges

| ID | Field | Cells | Methods | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| DE.B.C1 | Architecture | A1–A3, A5 | all | buildings |
| DE.B.C2 | Urban and regional planning | A4 | M.2, M.5 | cities |
| DE.B.C3 | Landscape architecture | A4.3 | M.1, M.3 | landscapes |
| DE.B.C4 | Industrial and product design | A1–A3 | M.1, M.3 | products |
| DE.B.C5 | Graphic and communication design | A2.3 | M.1, M.3 | media |
| DE.B.C6 | Interior design | A2, A3.1 | M.1 | interiors |
| DE.B.C7 | Building science (shared) | A3.1 | M.4 | envelopes (uses PH, EN) |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. Place by the aspect of the arrangement for use.
2. Structural and system adequacy is EN; use, form and place are DE.
3. A verdict on beauty with a stated criterion is AR; design rationale is DE.
4. Planning law is LA; the planning decision is PO; land price is EC.
5. Environmental impact is EV.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused | Owner |
|---|---|
| structural and engineering adequacy | EN |
| aesthetic verdict | AR |
| planning law | LA |
| land economics | EC |
| environmental impact | EV |

### 10. External crosswalk
**ANZSRC 33** (checked 9 Oct 2026 against the ABS file anzsrc2020_for.xlsx) has five groups:

## 2.21 AG — Agriculture, veterinary and food

New field, added at the owner's direction. Standard v2 cited, not copied. Why it is here: ANZSRC 30 had no core home. A crop is not only a species, an animal disorder is not human medicine, and a food standard is not only chemistry.

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** Agricultural, veterinary and food sciences state how organisms are produced and kept for human use, how their health is maintained, and how food is made safe.
**Complication.** v1 spread these across BI, CH and MD. "Yield of this cultivar under this management" had no home.
**Question.** By what dimension can each claim about producing and keeping organisms and food be placed once?
**Answer.** By the stage of the production system the claim is about: the organism bred, its cultivation or husbandry, its health, the food made from it, and the farming system.

### 2. Governing thought
Agriculture is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five stages of production: breeding, cultivation and husbandry, crop and animal health, food, and farming systems.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **the stage of the production system.** Order: from the organism to the whole system.
1. Breeding
2. Cultivation and husbandry
3. Crop and animal health
4. Food
5. Farming systems

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### AG.O.A1 Breeding
**SCQA.** Production starts with chosen organisms. How are they improved? Key line: selection, genetic improvement, genetic resources.
- AG.O.A1.1 Selection — the breeder's equation R = h²S predicts response to selection, assuming additive inheritance and a stable environment. The genetics is BI.
- AG.O.A1.2 Genetic improvement — hybrids, marker-assisted selection, gene editing. Regulation is LA.
- AG.O.A1.3 Genetic resources — seed banks, landraces.

#### AG.O.A2 Cultivation and husbandry
**SCQA.** Organisms are grown and kept under managed conditions. What management gives what result? Key line: soils and nutrients, water, crop management, animal husbandry, aquaculture and forestry.
- AG.O.A2.1 Soils and nutrients — fertiliser response. Liebig's law of the minimum holds as a first approximation, when one nutrient limits growth. The soil's state is EA.
- AG.O.A2.2 Water and irrigation.
- AG.O.A2.3 Crop management — rotation, tillage, sowing. A yield claim must state season, site and population.
- AG.O.A2.4 Animal husbandry — pastoral and housed systems, nutrition, welfare. NZ pastoral dairying is a substrate.
- AG.O.A2.5 Aquaculture, fisheries and forestry production.

#### AG.O.A3 Crop and animal health
**SCQA.** Producing organisms fall ill and are attacked. How is their health kept? Key line: plant protection, veterinary medicine, biosecurity, zoonoses.
- AG.O.A3.1 Plant protection — pests, weeds, pathogens; integrated pest management.
- AG.O.A3.2 Veterinary medicine — diagnosis and treatment of animal disorders.
- AG.O.A3.3 Biosecurity — border and incursion management (e.g. Mycoplasma bovis in NZ from 2017, UNVERIFIED date).
- AG.O.A3.4 Zoonoses — the animal side is AG; human disease is MD (One Health is a bridge).

#### AG.O.A4 Food
**SCQA.** Produce becomes food. What makes food safe, nutritious and stable? Key line: composition and nutrition, processing, safety, standards.
- AG.O.A4.1 Composition and nutrition — the molecule is CH; human nutritional need is MD.
- AG.O.A4.2 Processing and preservation — fermentation, pasteurisation, drying.
- AG.O.A4.3 Food safety — HACCP as a control system (W11); pathogen limits.
- AG.O.A4.4 Food standards — Codex Alimentarius; FSANZ for Australia and NZ (W12; legal force LA).

#### AG.O.A5 Farming systems
**SCQA.** Farms are systems of land, organisms, labour and markets. Key line: system productivity, farm management, environmental effect.
- AG.O.A5.1 System productivity — total factor productivity of farms (measurement here; economics EC).
- AG.O.A5.2 Farm management — decisions under weather and price risk.
- AG.O.A5.3 Environmental effect of farming — nitrate leaching, methane. The impact claim is EV; AG owns the practice that changes it.

### 5. Face M — Methods

| ID | Role | Shared role | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| AG.M.1 | Field trial | M4 Intervene | randomised plot and herd trials |
| AG.M.2 | Survey and monitor | M3 Observe | farm surveys, disease surveillance |
| AG.M.3 | Breed and select | M4 Intervene | breeding programmes |
| AG.M.4 | Model systems | M5 Compute | crop and farm-system models |
| AG.M.5 | Inspect and certify | M7 Verify | food inspection, organic certification |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
"Feed the soil, not the plant"; right product, right rate, right time, right place; farmer knowledge as a first guess.

### 6. Face W — Warrants

| Cell | Warrant | Standard | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|
| AG.O.A1 | W3 + W4 | replicated trials across environments | genotype-by-environment interaction |
| AG.O.A2 | W3 | replicated field trial with stated site and season | site specificity, weather years |
| AG.O.A3 | W3 + W4 | controlled trial; surveillance with stated sensitivity | under-reporting |
| AG.O.A4 | W4 + W11 + W12 | laboratory analysis; conformity with standard | sampling error |
| AG.O.A5 | W4 + W2 | farm data; system model with validation | model scope |

### 7. Face B — Bridges

| ID | Field | Cells | Methods | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| AG.B.C1 | Agronomy and crop science | A1, A2 | M.1, M.3 | crops |
| AG.B.C2 | Animal science | A1, A2.4 | M.1, M.3 | livestock |
| AG.B.C3 | Veterinary science | A3.2, A3.4 | M.1, M.2 | animals |
| AG.B.C4 | Food science and technology | A4 | M.5 | foods (uses CH) |
| AG.B.C5 | Horticulture and viticulture | A2.3 | M.1 | fruit, vines |
| AG.B.C6 | Fisheries, aquaculture, forestry | A2.5 | M.2 | waters, forests |
| AG.B.C7 | One Health | A3.4 | M.2 | animals and people (with MD) |
| AG.B.C8 | Brewing and fermentation science | A4.2 | M.1, M.5 | beverages (yeast biology BI, chemistry CH) |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. Place by the stage of production.
2. The taxon and its biology are BI. Its production is AG.
3. An animal disorder is AG; a human disorder is MD.
4. The molecule is CH; the food's safety and standard are AG.
5. Farm economics and prices are EC; environmental impact is EV.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused | Owner |
|---|---|
| general biology of the taxon | BI |
| human disease and nutrition need | MD |
| chemistry of food molecules | CH |
| agricultural prices and markets | EC |
| environmental impact | EV |
| food law | LA |

**ANZSRC 30** (checked 9 Oct 2026 against the ABS file anzsrc2020_for.xlsx) has ten groups:

## 2.22 EV — Environmental science

New field, added at the owner's direction. Standard v2 cited, not copied. Why it is here: ANZSRC 41 is not the same as Earth science. A process claim (EA) and a harm claim were forced into one cell.

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** Environmental science studies coupled human and natural systems: what people do to the environment, what state results, what harm follows, and what is done about it.
**Complication.** EA owns how the Earth works and BI owns how ecosystems work. Nothing owned "this pressure causes this harm to this system", which is the core environmental claim.
**Question.** By what dimension can each coupled claim be placed once, with the process science left to EA, BI and CH?
**Answer.** By the link in the causal chain the claim concerns: pressure, state, impact, response or limit. This is an adaptation of the EEA's DPSIR framework, with drivers folded into pressures and limits added.

### 2. Governing thought
Environmental science is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five links of a coupled system: pressures, states, impacts, responses, and limits.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **the link in the human–environment causal chain.** Order: causal.
1. Pressures
2. States
3. Impacts
4. Responses
5. Limits

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### EV.O.A1 Pressures
**SCQA.** Human activity loads the environment. What load, from what activity? Key line: emissions, land and sea use change, extraction, introduced species.
- EV.O.A1.1 Emissions — greenhouse gases, pollutants. Inventories follow IPCC guidelines (W12 for method).
- EV.O.A1.2 Land and sea use change — clearance, drainage, urbanisation.
- EV.O.A1.3 Extraction — water, fish, minerals.
- EV.O.A1.4 Introduced species — pests and invasives (e.g. possums and mustelids in NZ).

#### EV.O.A2 States
**SCQA.** Pressures change the state of air, water, soil and biota. What is the measured state? Key line: air, freshwater, marine, soil, biodiversity.
- EV.O.A2.1 Air quality — the measurement uses CH and EA.
- EV.O.A2.2 Freshwater quality — nitrate, E. coli, sediment.
- EV.O.A2.3 Marine environment — acidification, warming.
- EV.O.A2.4 Soil state — contamination, erosion.
- EV.O.A2.5 Biodiversity status — threat classifications (IUCN Red List criteria, W12).

#### EV.O.A3 Impacts
**SCQA.** A changed state harms ecosystems and people. What harm, attributed how? Key line: ecosystem harm, human health impact, attribution, ecosystem services.
- EV.O.A3.1 Ecosystem harm — loss of function and species.
- EV.O.A3.2 Human health impact — the exposure–response is MD.
- EV.O.A3.3 Attribution — the share of an observed change caused by a stated pressure (e.g. event attribution in climate science). The physical model is EA.
- EV.O.A3.4 Ecosystem services — benefits people receive; valuation is EC.

#### EV.O.A4 Responses
**SCQA.** Societies respond. Which responses change pressures or states? Key line: restoration, conservation, regulation, monitoring of outcomes.
- EV.O.A4.1 Restoration — measured recovery after intervention.
- EV.O.A4.2 Conservation — protected areas, predator control (Predator Free 2050 is a stated goal, UNVERIFIED name and date).
- EV.O.A4.3 Regulation and instruments — the policy is PO, the price instrument EC, the duty LA. EV owns the measured environmental effect.
- EV.O.A4.4 Kaitiakitanga in practice — guardianship as a response; its tradition-internal meaning is IK.

#### EV.O.A5 Limits
**SCQA.** Some states should not be crossed. Where are the limits? Key line: thresholds and tipping points, standards, carrying capacity and boundaries.
- EV.O.A5.1 Thresholds and tipping points — a regime shift occurs past a critical value, assuming bistable dynamics (theory MA.O.A4).
- EV.O.A5.2 Standards and bottom lines — legal or agreed limits (W12; force LA).
- EV.O.A5.3 Carrying capacity and planetary boundaries — the planetary boundaries framework (Rockström et al., 2009) is a proposed set, CONTESTED in its numbers.

### 5. Face M — Methods

| ID | Role | Shared role | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| EV.M.1 | Monitor | M3 Observe | state-of-environment monitoring |
| EV.M.2 | Assess impact | M6 Compare and reconstruct | EIA, before–after–control–impact designs |
| EV.M.3 | Model the coupled system | M5 Compute | integrated assessment models |
| EV.M.4 | Intervene and restore | M4 Intervene | restoration trials |
| EV.M.5 | Account | M1 Represent | emissions inventories, life-cycle assessment |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
Precautionary principle (a decision rule, not evidence); polluter pays; "think globally, act locally".

### 6. Face W — Warrants

| Cell | Warrant | Standard | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|
| EV.O.A1 | W4 + W12 | inventory under a stated method | under-reporting, method change |
| EV.O.A2 | W4 | monitoring with stated uncertainty | sparse sites, drift |
| EV.O.A3 | W5 + W2 | causal attribution; model with stated skill | confounded drivers |
| EV.O.A4 | W3 + W5 | BACI design or identified effect | short records, displacement |
| EV.O.A5 | W2 + W12 | modelled threshold; agreed standard | non-stationarity |

### 7. Face B — Bridges

| ID | Field | Cells | Methods | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| EV.B.C1 | Climate change impacts and adaptation | A3, A4 | M.3 | climate (EA owns the physics) |
| EV.B.C2 | Conservation biology | A2.5, A4.2 | M.1, M.4 | species (uses BI) |
| EV.B.C3 | Pollution and environmental chemistry | A1.1, A2 | M.1 | contaminants (uses CH) |
| EV.B.C4 | Environmental management | A4 | M.2 | catchments |
| EV.B.C5 | Ecological economics (shared) | A3.4 | M.3 | services (uses EC) |
| EV.B.C6 | Environmental policy (shared) | A4.3 | M.2 | instruments (uses PO, LA) |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. Place by the link in the chain.
2. The process (fluid, chemical, ecological) is EA, CH or BI. The coupled human-caused claim is EV.
3. The policy is PO; the price is EC; the duty is LA.
4. A human health exposure–response is MD.
5. A farming practice is AG; its environmental effect is EV.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused | Owner |
|---|---|
| Earth-system physics | EA |
| ecology without human pressure | BI |
| environmental law | LA |
| environmental economics and pricing | EC |
| environmental politics | PO |

### 10. External crosswalk
**ANZSRC 41** (checked 9 Oct 2026 against the ABS file anzsrc2020_for.xlsx) has seven groups:
- 4101 Climate change impacts and adaptation → B.C1
- 4102 Ecological applications → B.C2
- 4103 Environmental biotechnology → A4.1

## 2.23 IS — Information and the record

New field, added at the owner's direction. Standard v2 cited, not copied. Why it is here: ANZSRC 46 mixes computation with the record. CS owns cost and correctness. Nothing owned the catalogue, the archival rule, or retrieval as a claim about records. This pyramid is also the natural home of the Pyramids of Knowledge themselves, as a classification.

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** Library, archival and information science states how records are identified, described, arranged, found and kept.
**Complication.** CS owned the algorithms, HI owned source criticism, and LN owned language. The catalogue, the classification scheme and the archive had no owner, though every pyramid depends on them.
**Question.** By what dimension can each claim about records be placed once?
**Answer.** By the stage in the life of a record the claim concerns: the item, its description, its arrangement, its retrieval, and its preservation.

### 2. Governing thought
Information science is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five stages of a record's life: items, description, arrangement, retrieval, and preservation.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **the stage in the life of a record.** Order: from creation to keeping.
1. Items
2. Description
3. Arrangement
4. Retrieval
5. Preservation

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### IS.O.A1 Items
**SCQA.** A record must first be identified as a unit. What is the item? Key line: works and manifestations, records and evidence, data sets, identifiers.
- IS.O.A1.1 Works and manifestations — FRBR and its successor LRM distinguish work, expression, manifestation and item.
- IS.O.A1.2 Records as evidence — an archival record is evidence of an activity. Its source criticism for history is HI.
- IS.O.A1.3 Data sets — research data as items.
- IS.O.A1.4 Persistent identifiers — DOI, ORCID, ISBN (W12).

#### IS.O.A2 Description
**SCQA.** Items are found by their descriptions. How are they described? Key line: metadata standards, cataloguing rules, subject indexing, linked data.
- IS.O.A2.1 Metadata standards — Dublin Core, MARC (W12).
- IS.O.A2.2 Cataloguing rules — RDA.
- IS.O.A2.3 Subject indexing — controlled vocabularies and thesauri (MeSH, LCSH).
- IS.O.A2.4 Linked data — SKOS and RDF. The logic is MA.O.A1; the vocabulary is IS.

#### IS.O.A3 Arrangement
**SCQA.** Descriptions are organised into schemes. How is knowledge arranged? Key line: classification schemes, faceted classification, archival arrangement, ontologies.
- IS.O.A3.1 Classification schemes — LCC, DDC, UDC, MSC. A scheme is W12; whether it is MECE is testable (W9 with W4).
- IS.O.A3.2 Faceted classification — Ranganathan's Colon Classification (PMEST facets).
- IS.O.A3.3 Archival arrangement — respect des fonds and original order.
- IS.O.A3.4 Ontologies — BFO and DOLCE. Their philosophical commitments are PL.
- IS.O.A3.5 This taxonomy — the Pyramids of Knowledge as a classification of claims. Its reliability is tested by the placement protocol.

#### IS.O.A4 Retrieval
**SCQA.** Arranged records must be found. How well can they be found? Key line: retrieval effectiveness, relevance, bibliometrics, information behaviour.
- IS.O.A4.1 Retrieval effectiveness — precision and recall. The algorithm's cost is CS.
- IS.O.A4.2 Relevance — topical and user relevance; judged by assessors (W4).
- IS.O.A4.3 Bibliometrics — Lotka's law (author productivity) and Bradford's law (scattering) are empirical regularities within stated literatures.
- IS.O.A4.4 Information behaviour — how people seek information. The cognitive mechanism is MS.

#### IS.O.A5 Preservation
**SCQA.** Records decay and formats die. How are they kept usable? Key line: physical conservation, digital preservation, access and rights, Indigenous data governance.
- IS.O.A5.1 Physical conservation — the chemistry of paper and media is CH.
- IS.O.A5.2 Digital preservation — the OAIS reference model (ISO 14721, W12).
- IS.O.A5.3 Access and rights — open access; copyright is LA.
- IS.O.A5.4 Indigenous data governance — the CARE principles; Māori data sovereignty (Te Mana Raraunga). Tradition-internal authority over the data is IK.

### 5. Face M — Methods

| ID | Role | Shared role | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| IS.M.1 | Describe and classify | M1 Represent | cataloguing, indexing |
| IS.M.2 | Appraise | M7 Verify | archival appraisal, quality control |
| IS.M.3 | Evaluate retrieval | M3 Observe | test collections (TREC-style) |
| IS.M.4 | Measure literatures | M5 Compute | bibliometric analysis |
| IS.M.5 | Study users | M3 Observe | user studies |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
Literary warrant (classify by what is published); "more product, less process" in archives; collect broadly, weed later.

### 6. Face W — Warrants

| Cell | Warrant | Standard | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|
| IS.O.A1 | W12 + W7 | conformity with identity rules; provenance | lost provenance |
| IS.O.A2 | W12 + W4 | standard followed; indexer consistency measured | low inter-indexer agreement |
| IS.O.A3 | W12 + W9 + W4 | scheme rules; argued MECE; reliability measured | overlap, residual classes |
| IS.O.A4 | W4 | test collection with stated relevance judgements | assessor disagreement |
| IS.O.A5 | W11 + W4 | conformity with OAIS; fixity checks | format obsolescence |

### 7. Face B — Bridges

| ID | Field | Cells | Methods | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| IS.B.C1 | Library science | A1–A3 | M.1 | collections |
| IS.B.C2 | Archival science | A1.2, A3.3, A5 | M.2 | archives |
| IS.B.C3 | Information retrieval | A4.1, A4.2 | M.3 | test collections (uses CS) |
| IS.B.C4 | Scientometrics | A4.3 | M.4 | literatures |
| IS.B.C5 | Knowledge organisation | A3 | M.1 | schemes (ISKO) |
| IS.B.C6 | Digital humanities (shared) | A2, A4 | M.4 | texts (uses HI, AR) |
| IS.B.C7 | Museum studies (shared) | A1, A5 | M.1 | collections (uses HI) |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. Place by the record's life stage.
2. The algorithm's cost or correctness is CS; the retrieval effectiveness claim is IS.
3. Source criticism for a historical claim is HI; the archival rule is IS.
4. Copyright is LA.
5. Tradition-internal authority over Indigenous data is IK; its governance framework as a records rule is IS.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused | Owner |
|---|---|
| algorithms and systems | CS |
| historical source criticism | HI |
| copyright and privacy law | LA |
| media and communication effects | MS |
| philosophy of information | PL |

### 10. External crosswalk
**ANZSRC 46** (checked 9 Oct 2026 against the ABS file anzsrc2020_for.xlsx) has 14 groups. 4610 Library and information studies lands in A1–A5. 4609 Information systems and 4605 Data management are split: records go to IS, systems to CS. The remaining groups go to CS. Coverage for IS: 1 group whole and 2 in part, of 13 substantive groups.
