## 2.24 IK — Indigenous knowledge (ANZSRC 45)

New field, added at the owner's direction. Standard v2 cited, not copied. Read with p2_ANZSRC45.md, which gives the claim-type table.

**Status: drafted outside the tradition. Every placement is provisional until Māori-led review, and review led by Aboriginal, Torres Strait Islander and Pacific peoples for their claims. Reviewers may move a claim, refuse a split, or withhold a claim. Some kōrero is tapu and not for open classification.**

### 1. SCQA
**Situation.** Indigenous knowledge systems, mātauranga Māori among them, hold knowledge about people, land, practice and authority, transmitted by their own institutions.
**Complication.** Placed only by subject, mātauranga is split across BI, EA, HI, LA, LN and RE, and its own ordering principles vanish. Placed as one bucket, it becomes a residual "other", which the standard forbids.
**Question.** What does an Indigenous-knowledge pyramid own that no other pyramid owns, without letting W13 license measurement?
**Answer.** It owns claims about a knowledge system as a system: its ordering principles, its relations to land, its governance, its transmission, and its research and data. Each claim is in one of two modes: ·S (study, W6–W8) or ·M (tradition-internal, W13). Measurements, dated facts, legal duties and word meanings keep their owners.

### 2. Governing thought
Indigenous knowledge is exhausted by four faces of one pyramid: objects, methods, warrants and bridges.
Its objects divide into five aspects of a knowledge system: ordering principles, relations to land and waters, governance and custom, transmission, and research and data.

### 3. Key line
Dimension: **the aspect of a knowledge system the claim is about.** Order: from the system's principles to its relation with outside research.
1. Ordering principles
2. Relations to land and waters
3. Governance and custom
4. Transmission
5. Research and data

A people (iwi, hapū, nation) is a substrate, not a cell. A ·M sentence names its iwi or hapū, because authority is local.

### 4. Face O — Objects

#### IK.O.A1 Ordering principles
**SCQA.** A knowledge system orders the world by its own principles. What are they, as held? Key line: whakapapa as an ordering principle, categories of status, cyclical time.
- IK.O.A1.1 Whakapapa as ordering — genealogy as the principle that relates people, atua and the natural world. Whakapapa as human descent is HI; whakapapa of atua is RE.
- IK.O.A1.2 Categories of status — tapu and noa as ordering categories outside rite (in rite, RE.O.A1.2).
- IK.O.A1.3 Cyclical and ancestral time — "ka mua, ka muri" (walking backwards into the future) as a stated orientation (·S W8; ·M W13).

#### IK.O.A2 Relations to land and waters
**SCQA.** Knowledge systems hold relations to particular places. What is claimed about that relation? Key line: kaitiakitanga, maramataka, place knowledge.
- IK.O.A2.1 Kaitiakitanga — guardianship as obligation. Its measured environmental effect is EV.O.A4.4.
- IK.O.A2.2 Maramataka — the lunar calendar as a system for timing activity. An ecological observation it records (a fish run) is BI or EA under W4; the system itself is IK.
- IK.O.A2.3 Place knowledge — names, boundaries and histories of places, held by mana whenua. The landform is EA; the dated event is HI.

#### IK.O.A3 Governance and custom
**SCQA.** Communities govern themselves by custom and authority. Key line: tikanga as custom, rangatiratanga, marae kawa, customary institutions.
- IK.O.A3.1 Tikanga as custom — correct practice in social life. In rite before atua it is RE.O.A3.1. As recognised by a state court it is LA.O.A1.3.
- IK.O.A3.2 Rangatiratanga — authority and self-determination as held. Its Treaty and legal dimension is LA.O.A4.4; its political dimension is PO.
- IK.O.A3.3 Marae kawa — the protocols of a particular marae (local, ·M).
- IK.O.A3.4 Customary institutions — rūnanga, hui as decision forums.

#### IK.O.A4 Transmission
**SCQA.** Knowledge lives only if transmitted. How is it passed on, and who may hold it? Key line: institutions of learning, oral and performed transmission, restricted knowledge.
- IK.O.A4.1 Institutions of learning — whare wānanga, past and present. Modern wānanga as tertiary institutions are ED.
- IK.O.A4.2 Oral and performed transmission — kōrero tuku iho, mōteatea, haka. Their poetics are AR; their language is LN.
- IK.O.A4.3 Restricted knowledge — knowledge held by designated people and not for open classification. The pyramid records that restriction exists, never the content.

#### IK.O.A5 Research and data
**SCQA.** Outside research has often taken Indigenous knowledge without consent. What rules govern research and data? Key line: Kaupapa Māori research, data sovereignty, consent and benefit.
- IK.O.A5.1 Kaupapa Māori research — research by, with and for Māori, under Māori control (Smith, *Decolonizing Methodologies*, 1999).
- IK.O.A5.2 Data sovereignty — Te Mana Raraunga principles; the CARE principles. The records framework is IS.O.A5.4.
- IK.O.A5.3 Consent and benefit — Wai 262 (*Ko Aotearoa Tēnei*, 2011) on flora, fauna and mātauranga (UNVERIFIED date). Legal effect is LA.

### 5. Face M — Methods

| ID | Role | Shared role | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| IK.M.1 | Hold and recite | M1 Represent | recitation of whakapapa by designated holders |
| IK.M.2 | Learn by participation | M3 Observe | wānanga, practice on the marae |
| IK.M.3 | Interpret within the tradition | M2 Derive | interpretation by kaumātua and tohunga (·M) |
| IK.M.4 | Study comparatively | M6 Compare and reconstruct | Indigenous studies scholarship (·S) |
| IK.M.5 | Co-design research | M4 Intervene | Kaupapa Māori and partnership research |

#### Heuristics register (outside the count)
Ask who holds the knowledge before asking what it is; "he aha te mea nui o te ao? He tangata" (people first); no placement without the holders.

### 6. Face W — Warrants

The two-mode rule (as in RE): a ·S claim owes W6, W7 or W8 and never W13. A ·M claim owes W13, from whakapapa, kaumātua, tohunga and kawa, and W13 does not travel. W13 never licenses a measurement, a date, a legal duty or a word's meaning in the language, and it does not outrank W3 or W4 in their own cells. Those warrants likewise do not overrule a ·M sentence on its own terms. They are different sentences.

| Cell | ·S warrant | ·M warrant | Standard | Typical defeaters |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| IK.O.A1 | W8 | W13 | ·S: traced to recorded kōrero; ·M: held by the designated people | ·S: outsider misreading; ·M: unauthorised speaker |
| IK.O.A2 | W8 + W6 | W13 | as above | ·S: generalising across iwi |
| IK.O.A3 | W8 + W7 | W13 | as above | ·S: colonial-era sources taken at face value |
| IK.O.A4 | W7 + W8 | W13 | as above | disrupted transmission |
| IK.O.A5 | W9 + W12 | W13 | argued principles; adopted frameworks | tokenistic consultation |

### 7. Face B — Bridges

| ID | Field | Cells | Methods | Substrate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| IK.B.C1 | Māori studies | A1–A5 | all | iwi, hapū |
| IK.B.C2 | Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander studies | A1–A5 | all | nations (led by those peoples) |
| IK.B.C3 | Pacific studies | A1–A5 | all | Pacific peoples |
| IK.B.C4 | Indigenous environmental knowledge | A2 | M.2, M.5 | rohe (with EV, BI, EA) |
| IK.B.C5 | Indigenous research methodology | A5 | M.5 | research partnerships |
| IK.B.C6 | Global Indigenous studies | A1–A5 | M.4 | many peoples (·S) |

### 8. Assignment rules
1. Place by the aspect of the knowledge system, then by mode (·S or ·M).
2. A measurement, a dated fact, a legal duty and a word's meaning keep their owners (BI, EA, HI, LA, LN). The tradition may be the record they cite.
3. A claim about rite before atua is RE; a claim about custom in social life is IK.
4. Tikanga recognised by a state court is LA.
5. Rongoā efficacy for a named condition is MD under W3; the rite of rongoā is RE or IK.
6. No residual "Indigenous" cell exists. A claim that fits no IK cell goes to its subject's owner.
7. Māori-led review may withhold any claim.

### 9. Scope boundary

| Refused | Owner |
|---|---|
| measured ecological fact | BI, EA |
| dated event and human descent | HI |
| legal duty and Treaty law | LA |
| word form and meaning | LN |
| rite before atua, cosmogony | RE |
| clinical efficacy | MD |

### 10. External crosswalk
**ANZSRC 45** Indigenous studies has 20 groups (checked in p2_ANZSRC45.md): 4501–4506 Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander; 4507–4512 Māori; 4513–4518 Pacific Peoples; 4519 Indigenous data, methodologies and global studies; 4599 a residual.
Each people group pairs a people (substrate) with a topic. The topics land as follows: culture, language and history → IK.A1–A4, with HI and LN; education → ED; environmental knowledge → IK.A2; health and wellbeing → MD; law and governance → IK.A3 and LA; sciences → IK.A2 with BI and EA; 4519 → IK.A5.
Coverage: 19 of 20 land (as substrate × cell); 4599 is refused as a residual.

### 11. Validation
Sizes: key line 5. Face O 5 cells + 16 sub-cells = 21. M roles 5. Heuristics 3. W rows 5, each split ·S/·M. B bridges 6.
Key line, word for word: Ordering principles. Relations to land and waters. Governance and custom. Transmission. Research and data.
CONTESTED: the whole pyramid. One alternative holds that mātauranga is a whole system owning its phenomena (ecology and health stay with the tradition). It is flagged here and not adopted. A second alternative holds that IK should not exist as a pyramid at all, and claims should be split by subject as in p2_ANZSRC45.md. Reviewers decide.
OPEN: who convenes review, and how disagreement between iwi is recorded.

## 2.25 ANZSRC 45 — Indigenous Studies: scope note (no new pyramid)

**Status.** Written as a scope note. The owner has since added a pyramid, IK (p2_IK.md), which owns claims about a knowledge system as a system. This table still governs which subject claims stay with other owners. Claims are homed in existing pyramids by R1. Every placement is provisional until Māori-led review.

**Authority.** ANZSRC 2020 Division 45 has 20 groups: 4501–4506 Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander; 4507–4512 Māori; 4513–4518 Pacific Peoples; 4519 Indigenous data, methodologies and global studies; 4599 Other (checked: https://researchonline.jcu.edu.au/view/subjects/450000.html). Each people-group pairs a people (substrate) with a topic (sector). So 19 of 20 land as substrate × sector, never as cells. 4599 is a residual, not a cell.

### Where W13 applies

Only inside RE·T. It warrants sentences about correct relation to atua, tapu and noa, or the authority of kōrero tuku iho, argued from whakapapa, kaumātua, tohunga and marae kawa (RE.O.A1–A4 (·T)). Authority is local, so a ·T sentence names its iwi or hapū.

### Where W13 does not travel

W13 never licenses a measurement, a date, a legal duty or a word's meaning in the language. A mātauranga record can be evidence in those cells, weighed by their warrant (W4, W7, W8, W10). Those warrants likewise do not overrule a ·T sentence on its own terms. They are different sentences.

### Claim types and owners

| Claim type | Owner | Warrant | Refused by |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tikanga as correct practice before atua (rite) | RE.O.A3.1·T (·S if reported) |
| Tikanga as custom in social life | IK.O.A3.1 (·M or ·S) | W13 + W8; ·S W7 | — |
| Tikanga as part of NZ law (*Ellis v R* [2022] NZSC 114) | LA.O.A1 | W10 | RE |

### Contested placement (R6)

CONTESTED. An alternative holds that mātauranga Māori is a whole system that owns its phenomena. On that view ecological and health claims stay with the tradition. This note does not adopt it; it splits sentences by R1. The cost: a holistic claim becomes several sentences. Reviewers may reject the split itself.

### Māori-led review (binding)

These placements were drafted outside the tradition. They need review led by Māori scholars and holders of the relevant mātauranga, by iwi or hapū where the claim is local. Reviewers may move a claim, refuse a split, or withhold a claim from filing. Withholding is valid: some kōrero is tapu and not for open classification. Aboriginal, Torres Strait Islander and Pacific claims follow the same rule, led by those peoples. OPEN: who convenes review, and how disagreement between iwi is recorded.

# Part 5 — Placement protocol

This is a protocol, not a study. No coding has been done and no α is reported. Every number below is a design parameter or a comparator, not a result.

## 5.1 What is tested
Whether three independent coders, given only this file, place the same claim in the same home. A home has four parts: the owning pyramid, the Face O cell, the Face W warrant type, and the Face M role of the method behind the claim. Each part is scored separately.

## 5.2 Sample: fifty claims
- **Unit.** One declarative sentence, as published, with its citation. Not a paper, not a topic.
- **Frame.** Sentences drawn from abstracts and textbooks across all 25 pyramids.
- **Allocation.** Stratified: 25 claims, one per pyramid (MA and the 24 others), drawn at random from that field's literature. Then 25 border claims, drawn from the registry's "uses" edges, where one pyramid cites another. The border half is where the system is most likely to fail, so it is over-sampled on purpose.
- **Exclusions.** Claims under IK and RE·T are coded only with Māori-led and tradition-competent review. They are reported separately and never pooled.
- **Freeze.** The 50 sentences are fixed, hashed and published before coding begins.

## 5.3 Coders
- Three coders: one subject specialist per draw (rotating), one information scientist, and one generalist. Optionally a fourth, AI coder, reported separately and never pooled with the humans.
- Coders work blind to each other and to the author's intended home.
- Training: one hour on the standard and on ten practice claims that are not in the sample.
- Each coder records a home for each of the four faces and a one-line reason.

## 5.4 Agreement measure
- **Statistic.** Krippendorff's α, nominal level, computed separately for each face: owner, O-cell, W-type and M-role. Three coders, fifty units; missing values allowed.
- **Uncertainty.** Bootstrap 95% interval (10,000 resamples of units).
- **Thresholds (Krippendorff's conventional bands).** α ≥ .800 means the face is reliable. .667 ≤ α < .800 is tentative. α < .667 means the face is not yet a usable code.
- **Comparator.** Agreement between MathSciNet and zbMATH on MSC codes for the same articles: F1 0.83 at the top level, 0.72 at the second and 0.58 at the third, on 78,063 articles (source as given in related_work.md). α and F1 are different statistics. The comparison is a reading guide: the owner face should do no worse than the top-level F1, and O-cells no worse than the second.

## 5.5 Decision rules (fixed before coding)
1. If owner α < .667, the ownership rule and the border rules are revised before any other face is examined.
2. If O-cell α is tentative or worse in a pyramid, that pyramid's key line is re-cut. Re-cutting goes back to Phase 3.
3. If W-type α < .667, the controlled list W1–W13 is not yet a code. Merge or redefine the types the confusion matrix shows are confused.
4. Every disagreement is logged with the three reasons. The log is published with the ledger.
5. The protocol's results are reported whatever they show.

## 5.6 What would count against the system
- Coders agree on an owner but disagree on the cell. That means the key lines are not mutually exclusive.
- Coders place border claims by surface vocabulary rather than by warrant. That means the ownership rule is not being applied.
- Agreement is high only for the specialist coder. That means the file depends on expertise it does not write down.


---

# Part 5A — Placement pilot (AI coders), run 9 October 2026

**This is a pilot, not the study.** It tests whether the protocol works and where the code breaks. It cannot be reported as the reliability of the taxonomy, for four reasons.
1. **The coders are AI models from the same family as the author.** Coder A was Claude Haiku, B was Claude Sonnet and C was Claude Opus, each working blind and alone. Shared training makes agreement easier than it would be for people.
2. **The author wrote the 50 claims.** They were not sampled from the literature as the protocol requires.
3. **The coders used a condensed codebook** (pilot/codebook.md), not the full file.
4. **The protocol says AI coders are reported separately and never pooled with humans.** That rule holds here. The human study (5.3) has not been run.

## Design as run
- **Claims.** 50 in all. 24 were core claims, one per pyramid excluding IK. 24 were border claims. 2 were Indigenous-knowledge claims, coded but held out of every α, as the protocol requires.
- **Coding.** Each coder recorded an owner, a cell (A-level), a warrant and a method for each claim.
- **Statistic.** Krippendorff's α (nominal), with a bootstrap 95% interval from 2,000 resamples.

## Results (48 pooled claims, 3 coders)

| Face | α | 95% interval | Band | Mean agreement with author's key |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Owner | 0.928 | 0.864–0.985 | reliable (≥ .800) | 0.90 |
| Cell (A-level) | 0.717 | 0.611–0.813 | tentative | 0.74 |
| Warrant | 0.821 | 0.726–0.906 | reliable | 0.81 |
| Method | 0.832 | 0.733–0.916 | reliable | 0.76 |

| Stratum | Owner α | Cell α |
|---|---|---|
| Core (24) | 0.971 | 0.829 |
| Border (24) | 0.883 | 0.603 (below .667: not yet a usable code) |

**Comparator, read with care.** MathSciNet and zbMATH agree at F1 0.83 at the top MSC level and 0.72 at the second. α and F1 are different statistics. This pilot is also easier than that comparison: the claims were written by the author and coded by models of one family.

## What the pilot found (decision rules 5.5 applied)
Owner α clears .800, so rule 1 does not trigger. Cell α is tentative overall and fails on border claims, so rule 2 triggers: the key lines and border rules named below are re-examined. In five owner disagreements, all three coders, or two of them, disagreed with the author's key. Those are read as faults in the key or the code, not in the coders.

| # | Claim | Key | Coders | Finding | Repair |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 19 | Mishnah redacted c. 200 CE | RE.O.A2 | HI, HI, HI | The dated-fact rule (HI) and RE.O.A2.2 "transmission" both claim it | A dated event in a text's history is HI. RE.O.A2.2 keeps claims about the transmitted text as text (variants, recension). Add to RE rules. |
| 43 | Great Wave uses Prussian blue | CH | AR, AR, AR | AR's own rule says pigment identity is CH, but the codebook lacked that rule | Add the material-identity border rule to the codebook |
| 46 | Aquinas argues from motion | RE | RE, PL, PL | The prime-mover argument starts from premises any reasoner may grant, so by RE's own rule 3 it is PL. The key was wrong. | Key corrected to PL; the report of what Aquinas held stays RE·S |
| 39 | P vs NP is open | MA | MA, CS, CS | "Computation → CS" in the codebook overrides MA.B.C3 | State "open problems and theorems in complexity → MA" in the ownership rule |
| 44 | Great Wave sold in Edo shops | HI | AR, HI, AR | Reception (AR.O.A4) against dated fact (HI) | Market facts are HI; their reading as reception is AR.O.A4. Add to AR rules. |
| 24 | TREC recall fell | IS | CS, IS, IS | Split decision; the rule holds | none |
| 49 | 1080 and kererū nesting | EV | EV, BI, EV | Split decision; the rule holds | none |

Cell-level faults in the author's own pyramids, found by the pilot:
- **PH:** nuclear decay sat in A5.4.3 while nuclear structure sat in A2.3 (claim 38). Decay moves to A2.3.
- **EN:** the key line is a set of physical balances, so a design-life requirement (claim 41) has no cell. Two coders forced it into EN.O.A1. This is a real gap. Repair: add a requirements home (W12) to EN, or file specifications under EN.M (verification).
- **DE A2 vs A3** (claim 42) and **EV A2 vs A3** (claim 23): coders split between state and impact, and between form and performance in use. Repair: sharpen the definitions of these neighbouring cells.

## Indigenous-knowledge claims (held out, not pooled)
- **Claim 25 (kaitiakitanga):** all three coders gave IK, W13. Cells split between IK.O.A2 and IK.O.A3.
- **Claim 48 (whakapapa in a Native Land Court minute book):** two coders gave HI, W7; one gave IK.O.A4, W7. The split is exactly the contested line between a dated record and transmission. Only the Māori-led review can settle it.

## Status
The pilot shows that the protocol runs and that the owner face is promising. Cells on border claims are not yet a code. The human study (three human coders, claims sampled from the literature) remains the gate for any reported reliability.

# Part 7 — Māori-led review pack (prepared, not yet convened)

**What this is.** This is the material a Māori-led review needs: what is being asked, of whom, and on what terms. The review itself has not happened. It cannot be run by the author or by an AI. The placements below stay provisional until it is done, and the ledger marks the IK review gate FAIL until then.

## 1. Purpose
Ask Māori scholars and holders of the relevant mātauranga whether these placements are right, wrong, or should not be made at all. Reviewers may:
- move a claim
- refuse a split, for example by rejecting the one-sentence-one-home rule for mātauranga
- withhold a claim from open classification altogether

Withholding is a valid outcome. Some kōrero is tapu.

## 2. Who should lead it (to be decided by Māori, not by the author)
The author should approach, through proper channels, bodies with standing in this area. Candidates are a Māori research unit at a New Zealand university, Te Mana Raraunga (Māori data sovereignty), and the relevant iwi or hapū rūnanga for any local claim. The author takes no part in choosing reviewers beyond making the invitation.
- **Panel:** at least three reviewers, Māori-majority and Māori-chaired. Add an iwi or hapū representative for any local claim, such as marae kawa or place knowledge.
- **Payment:** reviewers are paid. Koha and fees are budgeted before invitation. Knowledge shared in review stays under Māori control, under the CARE principles and Te Mana Raraunga, and is not published without consent.
- **Aboriginal, Torres Strait Islander and Pacific claims:** these follow the same rule, led by those peoples, in separate panels.

## 3. What is put to the panel

| # | Placement | Where | Question for reviewers |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | IK as a pyramid at all, with five cells (ordering principles, relations to land and waters, governance and custom, transmission, research and data) | IK §3 | Should this exist? Are the five cells right? Is the alternative of splitting claims by subject (p2_ANZSRC45) better? |
| 2 | The two-mode rule: ·S (study) against ·M (tradition-internal, W13), never merged | IK §6, RE §6 | Is the split acceptable, or does it misdescribe mātauranga? |
| 3 | "W13 never licenses a measurement, a date, a legal duty or a word's meaning" | IK, ANZSRC45 | Is this fair to mātauranga that includes observation, such as maramataka? |
| 4 | Whakapapa split three ways: ordering (IK.O.A1.1), human descent (HI.O.A1), cosmogony (RE.O.A1.2) | IK, HI, RE | Is splitting whakapapa acceptable? |
| 5 | Tikanga split three ways: custom (IK.O.A3.1), rite (RE.O.A3.1), state law (LA.O.A1.3, *Ellis v R* [2022] NZSC 114) | IK, RE, LA | Is the line between custom and rite meaningful in tikanga? |
| 6 | Kaitiakitanga as obligation (IK.O.A2.1); its measured effect as EV.O.A4.4 | IK, EV | Acceptable? |
| 7 | Maramataka as a system (IK.O.A2.2); the ecological observation it records as BI or EA under W4 | IK | Acceptable? |
| 8 | Oral history: a dated account owes W7 (HI.O.A5.5); the tradition-internal account is IK | HI | Pilot claim 48 split the coders on this exact line. Which is right? |
| 9 | Io as supreme being (RE.O.A1.1.8), flagged CONTESTED on pre-contact antiquity | RE | Should this be filed at all? |
| 10 | Karakia, pōwhiri and tangihanga as rite (RE.O.A3.1.4); karakia as oral text (RE.O.A2.2.6) | RE | Is "religion" the right home, or should these move to IK? |
| 11 | Ringatū and Rātana as Māori Christian communities (RE.O.A4.3.4) | RE | Wording and dates |
| 12 | Te Tiriti o Waitangi: legal force (LA.O.A4.4), dated signing (HI.O.A1), rangatiratanga (IK.O.A3.2) | LA, HI, IK | Acceptable? |
| 13 | Te reo: grammar (LN.O.A3.4) and revitalisation (LN.O.A4.4); tradition-internal meaning (IK) | LN | Acceptable? |
| 14 | Kura kaupapa Māori and wānanga (ED.O.A3.5, IK.O.A4.1) | ED, IK | Acceptable? |
| 15 | Rongoā: rite (RE or IK); efficacy for a named condition (MD under W3) | MD, IK | Is subjecting rongoā to W3 acceptable, and on whose terms? |
| 16 | Whakairo as making (AR.O.A1.1.6) | AR | Acceptable? |
| 17 | Whakapapa as relational ontology (PL.O.A1.1) | PL | Acceptable? |
| 18 | Māori data sovereignty as a records rule (IS.O.A5.4, IK.O.A5.2) | IS, IK | Acceptable? |
| 19 | Restricted knowledge recorded only as "restricted", never by content (IK.O.A4.3) | IK | Is even that record acceptable? |

## 4. How the review runs
1. **Invitation and agreement on terms.** Payment, data control and the right to withhold are agreed in writing first.
2. **Material.** The panel gets this pack, the IK pyramid, the ANZSRC 45 note, and the relevant cells of other pyramids.
3. **Hui.** The panel meets, in person or online, at a pace it sets.
4. **Recorded outcomes.** Each placement is recorded as accepted, moved (to where), refused, or withheld. Reasons are recorded only if the panel wishes.
5. **Disagreement.** Where reviewers or iwi disagree, both positions are recorded and the claim stays CONTESTED. The author does not resolve it.
6. **Return.** The revised placements go back to the panel for confirmation before release.

## 5. What happens after
- Accepted placements lose their "provisional" mark.
- Moved placements are applied, and the registry is updated.
- Withheld claims are removed, leaving only a note that a claim was withheld.
- If the panel rejects IK as a pyramid, the file reverts to the ANZSRC 45 scope note, and that decision is recorded in the ledger.
